- **C — cadbc (Correct):** Splitting the 16 characters into 4-letter groups gives 'abcd' repeated four times (a b c d | a b c d | a b c d | a b c d). The blanks are c, a, d, b, c -> cadbc.
- **A — cabdc:** cabdc swaps the 3rd and 4th blanks, forming 'abcb' and 'adcd'.
- **B — cadcb:** cadcb swaps the 4th and 5th blanks, forming 'adcd' and 'abbd'.
- **D — cdabc:** cdabc puts 'd' in the 2nd blank, starting the second block with 'dbc'.
- **Exam Trick:** In a 16-character pattern, try splitting into 4 groups of 4 first; compare aligned positions across groups.

### Q232 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Find the missing letters in the repeating series: _ q r _ p _ r s p q _ s _ q r s

A. psqpr
B. prsqp
C. qsrqp
D. psqrp

**Answer:** D. psqrp
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — psqrp (Correct):** The 16-character series repeats 'pqrs' four times (p q r s | p q r s | p q r s | p q r s). Filling the blanks yields p, s, q, r, p -> psqrp.
- **A — psqpr:** psqpr swaps the last two blanks, ending with 'rqrs' instead of 'pqrs'.
- **B — prsqp:** prsqp places 'r' in the 2nd blank, giving 'pqrr'.
- **C — qsrqp:** qsrqp starts the first group with 'q' instead of 'p'.
- **Exam Trick:** The complete last group 'pqrs' (or near-complete groups) gives away the exact repeating unit without guesswork.

### Q233 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Select the letters that complete the pattern: m _ n o _ m n _ m m _ o m _ n o

A. mmonm
B. mmnom
C. mnonm
D. mmonn

**Answer:** A. mmonm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — mmonm (Correct):** The 16-character sequence repeats 'mmno' four times (m m n o | m m n o | m m n o | m m n o). The blanks are m, m, o, n, m -> mmonm.
- **B — mmnom:** mmnom swaps the 3rd and 4th blanks, producing 'mmnm' and 'mmoo'.
- **C — mnonm:** mnonm puts 'n' in the 2nd blank, forming 'nmno'.
- **D — mmonn:** mmonn ends with 'n' in the last blank, forming 'mnno'.
- **Exam Trick:** Spotting 'mm' in the third group confirms the block starts with a double 'm'.

### Q234 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Which letter sequence completes the series? a b _ b _ b c _ a b _ b a _ c b

A. cbacb
B. cabcb
C. cabbc
D. acbcb

**Answer:** B. cabcb
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — cabcb (Correct):** Dividing the 16 characters into 4-letter blocks gives 'abcb' repeated four times (a b c b | a b c b | a b c b | a b c b). The blanks are c, a, b, c, b -> cabcb.
- **A — cbacb:** cbacb places 'b' in the 2nd blank, forming 'bbca'.
- **C — cabbc:** cabbc swaps the last two blanks, forming 'abbb' and 'accb'.
- **D — acbcb:** acbcb places 'a' in the 1st blank, forming 'abab'.
- **Exam Trick:** Note that blocks can repeat a letter symmetrically, like 'abcb', rather than using all distinct letters.

### Q235 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Complete the continuous pattern: x _ z x _ x y _ x y _ y z _ y

A. yyxzx
B. zyzxx
C. yyzxx
D. yyzxy

**Answer:** C. yyzxx
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — yyzxx (Correct):** The 15-character series splits into 3 groups of 5: 'xyzxy' (x y z x y | x y z x y | x y z x y). Filling the blanks gives y, y, z, x, x -> yyzxx.
- **A — yyxzx:** yyxzx places 'x' in the 3rd blank, forming 'xyxxy'.
- **B — zyzxx:** zyzxx places 'z' in the 1st blank, forming 'xzzxy'.
- **D — yyzxy:** yyzxy ends with 'y' in the last blank, forming 'xyzxy' -> 'xyzyy'.
- **Exam Trick:** A 15-character series almost always splits into either 3 blocks of 5 or 5 blocks of 3.

### Q236 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Which set of letters will complete the series? a _ a c _ b a _ a b _ c a _ a c

A. bcaba
B. babac
C. cabab
D. bacab

**Answer:** D. bacab
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — bacab (Correct):** The 16-character series repeats 'abac' four times (a b a c | a b a c | a b a c | a b a c). The blanks take b, a, c, a, b -> bacab.
- **A — bcaba:** bcaba places 'c' in the 2nd blank, forming 'cbac'.
- **B — babac:** babac places 'b' in the 3rd blank, forming 'abab'.
- **C — cabab:** cabab starts with 'c', forming 'acac'.
- **Exam Trick:** Always verify every group after plugging in your chosen option to make sure no letter is out of place.

### Q237 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Select the option that completes the repeating pattern: p _ q p _ q q _ p q _ p p _ q p

A. qppqq
B. qpqqp
C. pqqpq
D. qqpqp

**Answer:** A. qppqq
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — qppqq (Correct):** The 16-character pattern repeats 'pqqp' four times (p q q p | p q q p | p q q p | p q q p). Filling the blanks gives q, p, p, q, q -> qppqq.
- **B — qpqqp:** qpqqp swaps the 3rd and 5th blanks, breaking the 'pqqp' symmetry.
- **C — pqqpq:** pqqpq places 'p' in the 1st blank, forming 'ppqp'.
- **D — qqpqp:** qqpqp places 'p' in the 3rd blank and 'q' in the 4th, forming 'pqqp' -> 'pqpp'.
- **Exam Trick:** Palindromic 4-letter blocks like 'pqqp' and 'abba' are common in SSC CGL Tier-1.

### Q238 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Which sequence of letters completes the pattern? a b _ a c _ a _ c a _ b a b _ a c b

A. cbcbc
B. cbbcc
C. bccbc
D. cbbcb

**Answer:** B. cbbcc
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — cbbcc (Correct):** Splitting the 18 characters into 3 groups of 6 gives 'abcacb' (a b c a c b | a b c a c b | a b c a c b). The blanks are c, b, b, c, c -> cbbcc.
- **A — cbcbc:** cbcbc swaps the 4th and 5th blanks, forming 'abcabb' and 'abcacb'.
- **C — bccbc:** bccbc starts with 'b', forming 'abbacb'.
- **D — cbbcb:** cbbcb ends with 'b', forming 'abcabb'.
- **Exam Trick:** An 18-character string usually splits into 3 blocks of 6 (or 6 blocks of 3); check the distance between identical anchors like 'ab'.

### Q239 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Find the missing letters in the series: k _ m n _ k l _ n o _ l m _ o

A. lomnk
B. olmnk
C. lomkn
D. lonkm

**Answer:** C. lomkn
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — lomkn (Correct):** The 15-character series repeats the 5-letter block 'klmno' three times (k l m n o | k l m n o | k l m n o). The blanks are l, o, m, k, n -> lomkn.
- **A — lomnk:** lomnk swaps the last two blanks, ending with 'nlmo'.
- **B — olmnk:** olmnk starts with 'o', forming 'komno'.
- **D — lonkm:** lonkm puts 'n' in the 3rd blank, forming 'klnno'.
- **Exam Trick:** When 5 distinct letters appear (k, l, m, n, o), the repeating unit length is at least 5.

### Q240 · Continuous Repeating Pattern Series

Which letters will replace the blanks in the cyclic series? a _ c b _ a c _ b a _ c

A. bcba
B. cbab
C. bacb
D. bcab

**Answer:** D. bcab
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — bcab (Correct):** The 12-character series uses a cyclic shift of 'abc': 'abc | bca | cab | abc'. Filling the blanks gives b, c, a, b -> bcab.
- **A — bcba:** bcba swaps the last two blanks, ending with 'bba' -> 'bbac'.
- **B — cbab:** cbab places 'c' in the 1st blank, forming 'acc'.
- **C — bacb:** bacb places 'a' in the 2nd blank, forming 'baa'.
- **Exam Trick:** If simple identical-block repetition fails, test cyclic rotation (abc -> bca -> cab).

## Chapter 4: Coding & Decoding

### Q241 · Forward Letter Shift

In a certain code language, each letter of the word "HAT" is shifted 1 place forward in the alphabet. How is "HAT" coded?

A. JCV
B. GZS
C. IBU
D. ICU

**Answer:** C. IBU
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — IBU (Correct):** Moving each letter +1 place forward: H(8)+1=I(9), A(1)+1=B(2), T(20)+1=U(21). Hence, HAT is coded as IBU.
- **A — JCV:** Shifting each letter by +2 instead of +1 gives JCV, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **B — GZS:** Shifting each letter 1 place backward (-1) instead of forward (+1) produces GZS.
- **D — ICU:** The middle letter A(1)+1 should be B(2), not C(3).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q242 · Forward Letter Shift

If every letter in "BUS" is moved 4 places forward in the English alphabet, what will be the code for "BUS"?

A. GZX
B. EXV
C. FYW
D. FZW

**Answer:** C. FYW
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — FYW (Correct):** Moving each letter +4 places forward: B(2)+4=F(6), U(21)+4=Y(25), S(19)+4=W(23). Hence, BUS is coded as FYW.
- **A — GZX:** Shifting each letter by +5 instead of +4 gives GZX, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **B — EXV:** Shifting each letter by +3 instead of +4 yields EXV, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — FZW:** The middle letter U(21)+4 should be Y(25), not Z(26).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

- **A — ULP:** Shifting each letter by +3 instead of +2 gives ULP, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **C — SJN:** Shifting each letter by +1 instead of +2 yields SJN, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — TLO:** The middle letter I(9)+2 should be K(11), not L(12).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

B. ZBU
C. XZS
D. YBT

**Answer:** A. YAT
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — YAT (Correct):** Moving each letter +6 places forward: S(19)+6=Y(25), U(21)+6=A(1), N(14)+6=T(20). Hence, SUN is coded as YAT.
- **B — ZBU:** Shifting each letter by +7 instead of +6 gives ZBU, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **C — XZS:** Shifting each letter by +5 instead of +6 yields XZS, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — YBT:** The middle letter U(21)+6 should be A(1), not B(2).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q292 · Forward Letter Shift

How is "PEN" coded if each of its letters is shifted 7 places forward in the alphabetical order?

A. WLU
B. XMV
C. VKT
D. WMU

**Answer:** A. WLU
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — WLU (Correct):** Moving each letter +7 places forward: P(16)+7=W(23), E(5)+7=L(12), N(14)+7=U(21). Hence, PEN is coded as WLU.
- **B — XMV:** Shifting each letter by +8 instead of +7 gives XMV, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **C — VKT:** Shifting each letter by +6 instead of +7 yields VKT, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — WMU:** The middle letter E(5)+7 should be L(12), not M(13).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q293 · Forward Letter Shift

In a certain code language, each letter of the word "BOX" is shifted 1 place forward in the alphabet. How is "BOX" coded?

A. DQZ
B. ANW
C. CPY
D. CQY

**Answer:** C. CPY
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — CPY (Correct):** Moving each letter +1 place forward: B(2)+1=C(3), O(15)+1=P(16), X(24)+1=Y(25). Hence, BOX is coded as CPY.
- **A — DQZ:** Shifting each letter by +2 instead of +1 gives DQZ, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **B — ANW:** Shifting each letter 1 place backward (-1) instead of forward (+1) produces ANW.
- **D — CQY:** The middle letter O(15)+1 should be P(16), not Q(17).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q294 · Forward Letter Shift

If every letter in "RIM" is moved 3 places forward in the English alphabet, what will be the code for "RIM"?

A. VMQ
B. TKO
C. ULP
D. UMP

**Answer:** C. ULP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — ULP (Correct):** Moving each letter +3 places forward: R(18)+3=U(21), I(9)+3=L(12), M(13)+3=P(16). Hence, RIM is coded as ULP.
- **A — VMQ:** Shifting each letter by +4 instead of +3 gives VMQ, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **B — TKO:** Shifting each letter by +2 instead of +3 yields TKO, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — UMP:** The middle letter I(9)+3 should be L(12), not M(13).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q295 · Forward Letter Shift

In a code language, "INK" is written by advancing each letter 6 places in the alphabet. Find the resulting code.

A. PUR
B. NSP
C. OUQ
D. OTQ

**Answer:** D. OTQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — OTQ (Correct):** Moving each letter +6 places forward: I(9)+6=O(15), N(14)+6=T(20), K(11)+6=Q(17). Hence, INK is coded as OTQ.
- **A — PUR:** Shifting each letter by +7 instead of +6 gives PUR, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **B — NSP:** Shifting each letter by +5 instead of +6 yields NSP, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **C — OUQ:** The middle letter N(14)+6 should be T(20), not U(21).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q296 · Forward Letter Shift

How is "HAT" coded if each of its letters is shifted 7 places forward in the alphabetical order?

A. OHA
B. PIB
C. NGZ
D. OIA

**Answer:** A. OHA
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — OHA (Correct):** Moving each letter +7 places forward: H(8)+7=O(15), A(1)+7=H(8), T(20)+7=A(1). Hence, HAT is coded as OHA.
- **B — PIB:** Shifting each letter by +8 instead of +7 gives PIB, over-shifting every position by 1.
- **C — NGZ:** Shifting each letter by +6 instead of +7 yields NGZ, falling 1 position short per letter.
- **D — OIA:** The middle letter A(1)+7 should be H(8), not I(9).
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) to compute forward letter shifts in seconds during SSC exams.

### Q297 · Backward Letter Shift

In a certain code language, each letter of "MIND" is shifted 2 places backward in the alphabet. How is "MIND" coded?

A. JFKA
B. OKPF
C. KFLB
D. KGLB

**Answer:** D. KGLB
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — KGLB (Correct):** Moving each letter -2 places backward (wrapping A->Z if needed): M(13)-2=K(11), I(9)-2=G(7), N(14)-2=L(12), D(4)-2=B(2). Thus, MIND becomes KGLB.
- **A — JFKA:** Shifting each letter by -3 instead of -2 gives JFKA, moving 1 extra place backward.
- **B — OKPF:** Shifting each letter +2 forward instead of -2 backward produces OKPF.
- **C — KFLB:** The second letter I(9)-2 equals G(7), whereas F(6) reflects a -3 shift.
- **Exam Trick:** When a backward shift crosses A, add 26 to the position (e.g., A=1 -> 1-3+26 = 24 = X).

### Q298 · Backward Letter Shift

If every letter in "STAR" is moved 3 places backward in the English alphabet, what is the code for "STAR"?

A. PQXO
B. OPWN
C. VWDU
D. PPXO

**Answer:** A. PQXO
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — PQXO (Correct):** Moving each letter -3 places backward (wrapping A->Z if needed): S(19)-3=P(16), T(20)-3=Q(17), A(1)-3=X(24), R(18)-3=O(15). Thus, STAR becomes PQXO.
- **B — OPWN:** Shifting each letter by -4 instead of -3 gives OPWN, moving 1 extra place backward.
- **C — VWDU:** Shifting each letter +3 forward instead of -3 backward produces VWDU.
- **D — PPXO:** The second letter T(20)-3 equals Q(17), whereas P(16) reflects a -4 shift.
- **Exam Trick:** When a backward shift crosses A, add 26 to the position (e.g., A=1 -> 1-3+26 = 24 = X).

### Q299 · Backward Letter Shift

In a code language, "GOLD" is written by shifting each letter 4 places backward in the alphabet. Find the code.

A. BJGY
B. KSPH
C. CJHZ
D. CKHZ

**Answer:** D. CKHZ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — CKHZ (Correct):** Moving each letter -4 places backward (wrapping A->Z if needed): G(7)-4=C(3), O(15)-4=K(11), L(12)-4=H(8), D(4)-4=Z(26). Thus, GOLD becomes CKHZ.
- **A — BJGY:** Shifting each letter by -5 instead of -4 gives BJGY, moving 1 extra place backward.
- **B — KSPH:** Shifting each letter +4 forward instead of -4 backward produces KSPH.
- **C — CJHZ:** The second letter O(15)-4 equals K(11), whereas J(10) reflects a -5 shift.
- **Exam Trick:** When a backward shift crosses A, add 26 to the position (e.g., A=1 -> 1-3+26 = 24 = X).

### Q300 · Backward Letter Shift

How is "WAVE" coded if each letter is moved 1 place backward in the alphabetical order?

A. UYTC
B. XBWF
C. VZUD
D. VYUD

**Answer:** C. VZUD
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — VZUD (Correct):** Moving each letter -1 places backward (wrapping A->Z if needed): W(23)-1=V(22), A(1)-1=Z(26), V(22)-1=U(21), E(5)-1=D(4). Thus, WAVE becomes VZUD.
- **A — UYTC:** Shifting each letter by -2 instead of -1 gives UYTC, moving 1 extra place backward.
- **B — XBWF:** Shifting each letter +1 forward instead of -1 backward produces XBWF.
- **D — VYUD:** The second letter A(1)-1 equals Z(26), whereas Y(25) reflects a -2 shift.
- **Exam Trick:** When a backward shift crosses A, add 26 to the position (e.g., A=1 -> 1-3+26 = 24 = X).

### Q301 · Mixed & Progressive Shift

In a certain code language, "MIND" is written as "NKQH". How will "GAME" be written in that language?

A. HCPI
B. HCPH
C. ICOG
D. HCQJ

**Answer:** A. HCPI
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — HCPI (Correct):** The pattern adds +1, +2, +3, +4 to consecutive letters: M+1=N, I+2=K, N+3=Q, D+4=G. Applying this to GAME: G(7)+1=H(8), A(1)+2=C(3), M(13)+3=P(16), E(5)+4=I(9), giving HCPI.
- **B — HCPH:** The fourth letter E(5) must shift +4 to I(9), not +3 to H(8).
- **C — ICOG:** Applying a uniform +2 shift gives ICQG instead of the progressive +1, +2, +3, +4 pattern.
- **D — HCQJ:** The third and fourth letters follow +3 and +4, not +4 and +5.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check differences across all positions first; SSC CGL often uses progressive +1, +2, +3, +4 shifts.

### Q302 · Mixed & Progressive Shift

In a certain code language, "WATER" is coded as "YZVDT". How is "CLOUD" coded in the same language?

A. EMQVF
B. AMMVB
C. EKQTF
D. EKQTE

**Answer:** C. EKQTF
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — EKQTF (Correct):** The rule alternates +2, -1, +2, -1, +2: W+2=Y, A-1=Z, T+2=V, E-1=D, R+2=T. For CLOUD: C(3)+2=E(5), L(12)-1=K(11), O(15)+2=Q(17), U(21)-1=T(20), D(4)+2=F(6), giving EKQTF.
- **A — EMQVF:** Adding +1 at the 2nd and 4th positions instead of subtracting 1 breaks the alternating +2, -1 rule.
- **B — AMMVB:** Reversing the signs to -2, +1 produces AJMSC instead of following +2, -1.
- **D — EKQTE:** The final letter D(4) shifts +2 to F(6), not +1 to E(5).
- **Exam Trick:** Alternating +k/-m shifts are a staple of SSC CHSL; verify at least the first three letters before locking the pattern.

### Q303 · Mixed & Progressive Shift

In a certain code, "APPLE" is written as "BOOKF". Following the same rule, how is "MANGO" written?

A. NBOHP
B. LZMFN
C. LBMFP
D. LBMFN

**Answer:** C. LBMFP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — LBMFP (Correct):** Vowels shift +1 and consonants shift -1: in APPLE, A+1=B, P-1=O, P-1=O, L-1=K, E+1=F (BOOKF). Applying this to MANGO: M(13)-1=L(12), A(1)+1=B(2), N(14)-1=M(13), G(7)-1=F(6), O(15)+1=P(16), giving LBMFP.
- **A — NBOHP:** Shifting every letter +1 ignores the consonant -1 rule used in APPLE -> BOKKF.
- **B — LZMFN:** Shifting every letter -1 ignores the vowel +1 rule.
- **D — LBMFN:** The final letter O is a vowel and must shift +1 to P, not -1 to N.
- **Exam Trick:** When a uniform or alternating shift fails, test vowel-vs-consonant rules immediately.

### Q304 · Mixed & Progressive Shift

If "TIGER" is coded as "SFHJU" in a certain language, how is "HORSE" coded?

A. FTSPI
B. IPSTF
C. ESROH
D. DRQNG

**Answer:** A. FTSPI
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — FTSPI (Correct):** First reverse the word, then shift each letter +1: TIGER reversed is REGIT, and +1 gives SFHJU. Reversing HORSE gives ESROH; shifting +1 yields E+1=F, S+1=T, R+1=S, O+1=P, H+1=I, i.e., FTSPI.
- **B — IPSTF:** Shifting HORSE directly by +1 without reversing gives IPSTF instead of reversing first.
- **C — ESROH:** Simply reversing HORSE gives ESROH without the +1 forward shift.
- **D — DRQNG:** Reversing and shifting -1 gives DRQNG instead of +1.
- **Exam Trick:** If the first letter of the code matches the shifted last letter of the word, check reverse-plus-shift.

### Q305 · Criss-Cross & Pair Reversal

In a certain code language, "MOTHER" is written as "OMHTRE". How is "FATHER" written in that code?

A. REHTAF
B. THFAER
C. ERTHFA
D. AFHTRE

**Answer:** D. AFHTRE
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — AFHTRE (Correct):** Adjacent pairs of letters are swapped: MO->OM, TH->HT, ER->RE, giving OMHTRE. Applying the same to FATHER: FA->AF, TH->HT, ER->RE, which gives AFHTRE.
- **A — REHTAF:** Reversing the entire word gives REHTAF instead of swapping adjacent pairs.
- **B — THFAER:** Swapping 3-letter halves or blocks incorrectly produces THFAER instead of adjacent 2-letter swaps.
- **C — ERTHFA:** Reversing pair-swapped letters scrambles the pair order.
- **Exam Trick:** When all letters in the code are identical to the original word, look for pair, half, or full-word rearrangement.

### Q306 · Criss-Cross & Pair Reversal

If "GARDEN" is coded as "RAGNED", how will "SILVER" be coded?

A. REVLIS
B. ISVLRE
C. LISREV
D. LISSER

**Answer:** C. LISREV
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — LISREV (Correct):** The word is split into two 3-letter halves and each half is reversed: GAR->RAG and DEN->NED (RAGNED). For SILVER: SIL->LIS and VER->REV, yielding LISREV.
- **A — REVLIS:** Reversing the whole word gives REVLIS rather than reversing each 3-letter half separately.
- **B — ISVLRE:** Pair-wise swapping produces ISVLRE instead of half-word reversal.
- **D — LISSER:** Only the first half is reversed properly in LISSER; the second half VER must reverse to REV.
- **Exam Trick:** For 6-letter or 8-letter rearrangement codes, always test splitting into two equal halves.

### Q307 · Criss-Cross & Pair Reversal

In a certain code language, "PLANT" is coded as "LPBTN". How is "BRING" coded in that language?

A. RBIGN
B. BRIGN
C. GNIRB
D. RBJGN

**Answer:** D. RBJGN
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — RBJGN (Correct):** Letters 1-2 swap, letter 3 shifts +1, and letters 4-5 swap: PL->LP, A+1=B, NT->TN (LPBTN). For BRING: BR->RB, I+1=J, NG->GN, giving RBJGN.
- **A — RBIGN:** The middle letter I(9) must shift +1 to J(10), rather than remaining unchanged as I.
- **B — BRIGN:** The first two letters B and R must also swap to RB.
- **C — GNIRB:** Reversing the entire 5-letter word gives GNIRB instead of the criss-cross + middle-shift rule.
- **Exam Trick:** Odd-length words in criss-cross coding frequently keep the middle letter fixed or shift it by +1/-1.

### Q308 · Criss-Cross & Pair Reversal

In a code language, "WORK" is written as "PXLS". How is "PLAY" written in that code?

A. QMBZ
B. LPYA
C. MQZB
D. BZQM

**Answer:** C. MQZB
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — MQZB (Correct):** Each letter shifts +1 and adjacent pairs swap: WORK (+1)->XPSL, then swap pairs -> PXLS. For PLAY: (+1)->QMBZ, then swap pairs (QM->MQ, BZ->ZB) to get MQZB.
- **A — QMBZ:** Shifting +1 without swapping adjacent pairs gives QMBZ instead of MQZB.
- **B — LPYA:** Swapping pairs without the +1 shift gives LPYA.
- **D — BZQM:** Reversing the entire shifted string gives BZQM instead of swapping pairs (QM->MQ, BZ->ZB).
- **Exam Trick:** Combine positional swap checks with +1/-1 shifts when the coded letters are 1 away from the original letters.

### Q309 · Criss-Cross & Pair Reversal

If "MARKET" is coded as "TRAEKM", how is "PENCIL" coded?

A. LICNEP
B. LNEICP
C. EPCNLI
D. LENICP

**Answer:** B. LNEICP
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — LNEICP (Correct):** Outer letters (1st and 6th) swap, while inner pairs (2-3 and 4-5) swap: M<->T, AR->RA, KE->EK (TRAEKM). For PENCIL: P<->L, EN->NE, CI->IC, giving LNEICP.
- **A — LICNEP:** Full reversal gives LICNEP, whereas positions 2-3 and 4-5 are swapped locally.
- **C — EPCNLI:** Simple adjacent pair swapping gives EPCNLI instead of moving the outer letters.
- **D — LENICP:** The middle pairs EN and CI must also swap to NE and IC, not stay in original order.
- **Exam Trick:** Numbering letter positions 1 to N makes complex rearrangement patterns immediately visible.

### Q310 · Opposite Letters Coding

In a certain code language, "KING" is coded as "PRMT". How is "LION" coded in that language?

A. ORML
B. PRLM
C. OSLM
D. ORLM

**Answer:** D. ORLM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — ORLM (Correct):** Each letter is replaced by its opposite letter (sum of positions = 27): K(11)->P(16), I(9)->R(18), N(14)->M(13), G(7)->T(20). For LION: L(12)->O(15), I(9)->R(18), O(15)->L(12), N(14)->M(13), giving ORLM.
- **A — ORML:** The opposite of O(15) is L(12) and N(14) is M(13), so the last two letters are LM, not ML.
- **B — PRLM:** The opposite of L(12) is O(15) since 12+15=27, not P(16).
- **C — OSLM:** The opposite of I(9) is R(18), not S(19).
- **Exam Trick:** Opposite letter pairs always sum to 27 (A=1 & Z=26, B=2 & Y=25, etc.).

### Q311 · Opposite Letters Coding

If "COLD" is coded as "WOLX", how is "WARM" coded in the same language?

A. DZIN
B. MRAW
C. NIZD
D. OJAE

**Answer:** C. NIZD
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — NIZD (Correct):** Take the opposite of each letter and reverse the order: COLD -> XLIW -> reversed WOLX. For WARM: opposites are W->D, A->Z, R->I, M->N (DZIN); reversing gives NIZD.
- **A — DZIN:** Taking direct opposites without reversing the word gives DZIN instead of NIZD.
- **B — MRAW:** Reversing WARM to MRAW without taking opposite letters ignores the A-Z substitution.
- **D — OJAE:** Each letter in DZIN reversed is NIZD; shifting +1 gives OJAE, which is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Check both the first-to-first and first-to-last letter pairs for sum 27 when spotting opposite-letter codes.

### Q312 · Opposite Letters Coding

In a code language, "BIRD" is written as "ZSJX". How is "FISH" written in that code?

A. URHS
B. VSIT
C. TQGR
D. GJTI

**Answer:** B. VSIT
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — VSIT (Correct):** Each letter is replaced by its opposite letter and then shifted +1: B->Y+1=Z, I->R+1=S, R->I+1=J, D->W+1=X (ZSJX). For FISH: F->U+1=V, I->R+1=S, S->H+1=I, H->S+1=T, giving VSIT.
- **A — URHS:** Direct opposite letters give URHS without the +1 forward shift.
- **C — TQGR:** Subtracting 1 from the opposite letters gives TQGR instead of adding 1.
- **D — GJTI:** Simply shifting FISH by +1 gives GJTI without taking opposite letters first.
- **Exam Trick:** When opposite-letter sums are 28 instead of 27, the rule is 'opposite letter + 1'.

### Q313 · Opposite Letters Coding

In a certain code, "TRAIN" is coded as "GIZRM". How is "PLANE" coded in that language?

A. KOZMW
B. KOZMV
C. KPZMV
D. KOZNV

**Answer:** B. KOZMV
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — KOZMV (Correct):** Each letter is coded as its alphabetical opposite (sum = 27): T->G, R->I, A->Z, I->R, N->M. For PLANE: P(16)->K(11), L(12)->O(15), A(1)->Z(26), N(14)->M(13), E(5)->V(22), giving KOZMV.
- **A — KOZMW:** The opposite of E(5) is V(22), not W(23).
- **C — KPZMV:** The opposite of L(12) is O(15), not P(16).
- **D — KOZNV:** The opposite of N(14) is M(13), not N(14).
- **Exam Trick:** Mnemonic pairs like KP (Kanpur), LO (Love), AZ (Azad), MN (Man), EV (Evening) speed up opposite-letter decoding.

### Q314 · Opposite Letters Coding

If "CAKE" is coded as "EZMV" in a certain language, how is "BIKE" coded?

A. YRPV
B. DKMG
C. DKMV
D. DRMV

**Answer:** D. DRMV
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — DRMV (Correct):** Vowels take their opposite letter (A->Z, E->V, I->R) while consonants shift +2: C+2=E, A->Z, K+2=M, E->V (EZMV). For BIKE: B+2=D, I->R, K+2=M, E->V, giving DRMV.
- **A — YRPV:** Taking opposites for all letters gives YPRV, ignoring the +2 rule for consonants.
- **B — DKMG:** Shifting all letters +2 gives DKMG, ignoring the opposite-letter rule for vowels.
- **C — DKMV:** The vowel I(9) takes its opposite letter R(18), not +2 to K(11).
- **Exam Trick:** Hybrid rules in SSC CGL often treat vowels with opposite letters and consonants with a fixed shift.

### Q315 · Letter-to-Number Coding

In a certain code language, "CAT" is coded as 24 and "DOG" is coded as 26. How will "BAT" be coded?

A. 25
B. 21
C. 27
D. 23

**Answer:** D. 23
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 23 (Correct):** Each word is coded as the sum of its letter positions (A=1 to Z=26): CAT = 3+1+20 = 24, DOG = 4+15+7 = 26. Therefore, BAT = 2+1+20 = 23.
- **A — 25:** Adding B(2)+A(1)+T(20) equals 23, not 25.
- **B — 21:** 21 omits the value of B(2) from the sum.
- **C — 27:** 27 overcounts the sum by 4.
- **Exam Trick:** Always test the direct sum of A=1..Z=26 positions first when a word maps to a two-digit number.

### Q316 · Letter-to-Number Coding

If "GO" is coded as 32 and "SHE" is coded as 49, how is "SOME" coded?

A. 56
B. 52
C. 58
D. 54

**Answer:** A. 56
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 56 (Correct):** Letters are valued in reverse alphabetical order (Z=1 to A=26, i.e., 27 - forward position): GO = 20+12 = 32, SHE = 8+19+22 = 49. Thus, SOME = 8+12+14+22 = 56.
- **B — 52:** Using forward positions A=1..Z=26 gives S(19)+O(15)+M(13)+E(5) = 52 instead of reverse positions.
- **C — 58:** 58 miscalculates one of the reverse letter values by 2.
- **D — 54:** 54 falls short of the true reverse-position sum 8+12+14+22 = 56.
- **Exam Trick:** Reverse-order sum equals (27 x word length) minus the forward-order sum.

### Q317 · Letter-to-Number Coding

In a certain code, "AT" is written as 42 and "BAT" is written as 69. How is "CAT" written in that code?

A. 24
B. 48
C. 72
D. 96

**Answer:** C. 72
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 72 (Correct):** The code equals (sum of letter positions) x (number of letters): AT = (1+20)x2 = 42, BAT = (2+1+20)x3 = 69. Hence, CAT = (3+1+20)x3 = 24x3 = 72.
- **A — 24:** 24 is only the raw letter-position sum C(3)+A(1)+T(20) without multiplying by the 3 letters.
- **B — 48:** Multiplying the sum 24 by 2 instead of the word length 3 gives 48.
- **D — 96:** Multiplying 24 by 4 instead of 3 yields 96.
- **Exam Trick:** When a short word maps to a surprisingly large number, check if the sum is multiplied by word length or vowel count.

### Q318 · Letter-to-Number Coding

In a certain code language, "PEN" is coded as 35 and "READ" is coded as 56. How will "BOOK" be coded?

A. 43
B. 172
C. 84
D. 86

**Answer:** D. 86
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 86 (Correct):** Each word's letter-position sum is multiplied by the number of vowels in it: PEN = (16+5+14)x1 = 35, READ = (18+5+1+4)x2 = 56. BOOK has 2 vowels (O, O), so (2+15+15+11)x2 = 43x2 = 86.
- **A — 43:** 43 is just the raw position sum B(2)+O(15)+O(15)+K(11) without multiplying by the 2 vowels.
- **B — 172:** Multiplying 43 by the total length 4 gives 172, whereas PEN=35x1 shows the multiplier is vowel count.
- **C — 84:** 84 miscalculates the sum of BOOK as 42 instead of 43.
- **Exam Trick:** Compare a 1-vowel word (multiplier 1) against a 2-vowel word to spot vowel-count multipliers quickly.

### Q319 · Letter-to-Number Coding

In a certain code, "ACE" is written as 2610 and "BAG" is written as 4214. How is "DOG" written in that code?

A. 83014
B. 4157
C. 81514
D. 8307

**Answer:** A. 83014
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 83014 (Correct):** Each letter's alphabetical position is doubled and concatenated: A(1)x2=2, C(3)x2=6, E(5)x2=10 -> 2610; BAG -> 4, 2, 14 -> 4214. For DOG: D(4)x2=8, O(15)x2=30, G(7)x2=14, giving 83014.
- **B — 4157:** 4157 uses the raw letter positions D(4), O(15), G(7) without doubling each value.
- **C — 81514:** The middle letter O(15) must also be doubled to 30, not left as 15.
- **D — 8307:** The final letter G(7) must be doubled to 14, not left as 7.
- **Exam Trick:** Long digit codes that grow with word length usually concatenate individual letter values rather than summing them.

### Q320 · Letter-to-Number Coding

If "SUN" is coded as 57 and "MOON" is coded as 61, how is "STAR" coded in that language?

A. 58
B. 61
C. 64
D. 62

**Answer:** D. 62
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 62 (Correct):** The code equals (sum of letter positions) + (number of letters): SUN = (19+21+14)+3 = 57, MOON = (13+15+15+14)+4 = 61. Therefore, STAR = (19+20+1+18)+4 = 58+4 = 62.
- **A — 58:** 58 is the raw letter-position sum 19+20+1+18 without adding the word length 4.
- **B — 61:** Adding 3 instead of 4 (the number of letters in STAR) gives 61.
- **C — 64:** 64 overcounts the sum+length total by 2.
- **Exam Trick:** When a sum is off by 3 or 4 across different words, check if the word's letter count is being added.

### Q321 · Symbol & Direct Letter Coding

In a certain code language, "ROAD" is coded as "@#%$" and "BEAR" is coded as "*&%@". How is "DEAR" coded in that language?

A. $&%@
B. $&@%
C. #&%@
D. *&%@

**Answer:** A. $&%@
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — $&%@ (Correct):** Each letter maps directly to its corresponding symbol: R=@, O=#, A=%, D=$, B=*, E=&. Substituting into DEAR gives D($), E(&), A(%), R(@), i.e., $&%@.
- **B — $&@%:** In BEAR (*&%@), A is % and R is @, so the last two symbols are %@, not @%.
- **C — #&%@:** From ROAD (@#%$), D is $ whereas # stands for O.
- **D — *&%@:** *&%@ is the code for BEAR, not DEAR.
- **Exam Trick:** Verify shared letters across the given words (here A=% and R=@) to confirm direct 1-to-1 symbol mapping.

### Q322 · Symbol & Direct Letter Coding

If "PAINT" is coded as 74128 and "EXCEL" is coded as 93596, how is "ACCEPT" coded?

A. 455978
B. 455987
C. 433978
D. 755948

**Answer:** A. 455978
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 455978 (Correct):** Direct digit substitution gives A=4, C=5, E=9, P=7, T=8 (note E=9 appears twice in EXCEL). Mapping ACCEPT yields 4-5-5-9-7-8, i.e., 455978.
- **B — 455987:** The last two letters are P(7) and T(8), so the code ends in 78, not 87.
- **C — 433978:** From EXCEL (93596), C maps to 5 while 3 maps to X.
- **D — 755948:** A maps to 4 and P maps to 7, so 755948 swaps the digits for A and P.
- **Exam Trick:** Repeated letters in a sample word (like E in EXCEL -> 9) immediately confirm direct digit-to-letter coding.

### Q323 · Symbol & Direct Letter Coding

In a certain code, "WORK" is written as "4$#2" and "DISK" is written as "7@82". How is "RISK" written in that code?

A. $@82
B. #@82
C. #8@2
D. 4@82

**Answer:** B. #@82
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — #@82 (Correct):** Shared letter K=2 confirms positional substitution: R=#, I=@, S=8, K=2. Hence, RISK is coded as #@82.
- **A — $@82:** In WORK (4$#2), R is # while $ represents O.
- **C — #8@2:** From DISK (7@82), I is @ and S is 8, so the middle characters are @8, not 8@.
- **D — 4@82:** 4 represents W, whereas R is coded as #.
- **Exam Trick:** In mixed symbol-digit codes, anchor on the common letter first (here K=2) to rule out positional shifts.

### Q324 · Word-Substitution Coding

If "blue" is called "green", "green" is called "red", "red" is called "yellow", and "yellow" is called "white", what is the colour of human blood?

A. Red
B. Yellow
C. Green
D. White

**Answer:** B. Yellow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Yellow (Correct):** Human blood is red in reality. According to the given chain, "red" is called "yellow". Therefore, the colour of human blood is Yellow.
- **A — Red:** Red is the real-world colour of blood, but in this code "red" is called "yellow".
- **C — Green:** Green is called red, not the other way around.
- **D — White:** White is what "yellow" is called, which is one step too far.
- **Exam Trick:** In "A is called B" questions, find the real answer first and move exactly one step forward in the chain.

### Q325 · Word-Substitution Coding

If "air" is called "water", "water" is called "fire", "fire" is called "earth", and "earth" is called "sky", where do fishes live?

A. Water
B. Fire
C. Air
D. Earth

**Answer:** B. Fire
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Fire (Correct):** Fishes live in water. Since "water" is called "fire" in this code, the answer is Fire.
- **A — Water:** Fishes live in water in reality, but "water" is called "fire" here.
- **C — Air:** Air is called water, so choosing air moves backward in the substitution chain.
- **D — Earth:** Earth is what "fire" is called, not what "water" is called.
- **Exam Trick:** Never chain multiple substitutions together; apply only the single rule matching the real-world answer.

---

# Section II: Classification, Blood Relations & Ranking

## Chapter 5: Classification (Odd One Out) & Word Arrangement

### Q326 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Triangle
B. Sphere
C. Pentagon
D. Hexagon

**Answer:** B. Sphere
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sphere (Correct):** Sphere is a three-dimensional solid figure, whereas Triangle, Pentagon, and Hexagon are two-dimensional flat polygons.
- **A — Triangle:** Triangle is a 2D polygon bounded by three straight sides.
- **C — Pentagon:** Pentagon is a 2D polygon bounded by five straight sides.
- **D — Hexagon:** Hexagon is a 2D polygon bounded by six straight sides.
- **Exam Trick:** In geometry classification questions, first check dimensionality (2D vs 3D) before counting sides or angles.

### Q327 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Tuesday
B. January
C. March
D. July

**Answer:** A. Tuesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Tuesday (Correct):** Tuesday is a day of the week, whereas January, March, and July are calendar months (each having 31 days).
- **B — January:** January is the first month of the year and has 31 days.
- **C — March:** March is the third month of the year and has 31 days.
- **D — July:** July is the seventh month of the year and has 31 days.
- **Exam Trick:** When calendar terms appear in odd-one-out questions, check both category (day vs month) and month length (30 vs 31 days).

### Q328 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Copper
B. Iron
C. Oxygen
D. Silver

**Answer:** C. Oxygen
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Oxygen (Correct):** Oxygen is a non-metal (gas at room temperature), whereas Copper, Iron, and Silver are solid metals.
- **A — Copper:** Copper is a ductile metallic element used in electrical wiring.
- **B — Iron:** Iron is a magnetic metallic element widely used in alloys like steel.
- **D — Silver:** Silver is a lustrous precious metal with high electrical conductivity.
- **Exam Trick:** Classification of elements in SSC exams often tests Metals vs Non-metals, or Noble Gases vs Halogens.

### Q329 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Rose
B. Lotus
C. Jasmine
D. Sparrow

**Answer:** D. Sparrow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Sparrow (Correct):** Sparrow is a bird, whereas Rose, Lotus, and Jasmine are flowering plants.
- **A — Rose:** Rose is a well-known woody perennial flower.
- **B — Lotus:** Lotus is an aquatic flowering plant and the national flower of India.
- **C — Jasmine:** Jasmine is a fragrant flowering shrub or vine.
- **Exam Trick:** Always identify the biological kingdom or class (flora vs fauna) when living organisms are grouped together.

### Q330 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Kilogram
B. Metre
C. Second
D. Thermometer

**Answer:** D. Thermometer
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Thermometer (Correct):** Thermometer is a measuring instrument (for temperature), whereas Kilogram, Metre, and Second are SI base units of measurement.
- **A — Kilogram:** Kilogram is the SI base unit of mass.
- **B — Metre:** Metre is the SI base unit of length.
- **C — Second:** Second is the SI base unit of time.
- **Exam Trick:** The seven SI base units are metre, kilogram, second, ampere, kelvin, mole, and candela; instruments like barometer or thermometer are frequent distractors.

### Q331 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Delhi
B. Nepal
C. Mumbai
D. Chennai

**Answer:** B. Nepal
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Nepal (Correct):** Nepal is a sovereign country, whereas Delhi, Mumbai, and Chennai are major Indian cities.
- **A — Delhi:** Delhi is a major Indian city and the National Capital Territory of India.
- **C — Mumbai:** Mumbai is a major Indian city and the capital of Maharashtra.
- **D — Chennai:** Chennai is a major Indian city and the capital of Tamil Nadu.
- **Exam Trick:** In geography odd-one-out questions, distinguish between countries, capitals, states, and major cities.

### Q332 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Piano
B. Violin
C. Telescope
D. Tabla

**Answer:** C. Telescope
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Telescope (Correct):** Telescope is an optical instrument used for viewing distant objects, whereas Piano, Violin, and Tabla are musical instruments.
- **A — Piano:** Piano is a keyboard musical instrument played by pressing keys.
- **B — Violin:** Violin is a bowed string musical instrument.
- **D — Tabla:** Tabla is a pair of hand drums used as a percussion musical instrument.
- **Exam Trick:** SSC exams also test sub-categories of musical instruments: stringed (violin), wind (flute), and percussion (tabla).

### Q333 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Brazil
B. Tokyo
C. Paris
D. Cairo

**Answer:** A. Brazil
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Brazil (Correct):** Brazil is a country, whereas Tokyo (Japan), Paris (France), and Cairo (Egypt) are national capitals.
- **B — Tokyo:** Tokyo is the capital city of Japan.
- **C — Paris:** Paris is the capital city of France.
- **D — Cairo:** Cairo is the capital city of Egypt.
- **Exam Trick:** Memorizing world capitals and currencies helps solve both General Awareness and Reasoning classification questions quickly.

### Q334 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Mars
B. Moon
C. Venus
D. Jupiter

**Answer:** B. Moon
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Moon (Correct):** Moon is a natural satellite of Earth, whereas Mars, Venus, and Jupiter are planets in the Solar System.
- **A — Mars:** Mars is the fourth planet from the Sun in our Solar System.
- **C — Venus:** Venus is the second planet from the Sun in our Solar System.
- **D — Jupiter:** Jupiter is the fifth planet from the Sun and the largest planet in our Solar System.
- **Exam Trick:** Pluto is classified as a dwarf planet, while Titan, Phobos, and Europa are natural satellites (moons).

### Q335 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Heart
B. Liver
C. Ear
D. Kidney

**Answer:** C. Ear
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Ear (Correct):** Ear is an external sense organ, whereas Heart, Liver, and Kidney are internal visceral organs.
- **A — Heart:** Heart is an internal muscular organ that pumps blood through the circulatory system.
- **B — Liver:** Liver is an internal organ responsible for detoxification and bile production.
- **D — Kidney:** Kidney is an internal organ that filters waste from the blood.
- **Exam Trick:** Human anatomy questions in SSC often group external sense organs (eye, ear, nose, skin, tongue) against internal organs.

### Q336 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Helium
B. Neon
C. Argon
D. Chlorine

**Answer:** D. Chlorine
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Chlorine (Correct):** Chlorine is a reactive halogen (Group 17), whereas Helium, Neon, and Argon are inert noble gases (Group 18).
- **A — Helium:** Helium is a colourless, inert noble gas in Group 18 of the periodic table.
- **B — Neon:** Neon is an inert noble gas in Group 18 used in illuminated signs.
- **C — Argon:** Argon is an inert noble gas in Group 18 making up nearly 1% of Earth's atmosphere.
- **Exam Trick:** The six naturally occurring noble gases are Helium, Neon, Argon, Krypton, Xenon, and Radon.

### Q337 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Carat
B. Peso
C. Dinar
D. Yen

**Answer:** A. Carat
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Carat (Correct):** Carat is a unit of mass/purity for gemstones and gold, whereas Peso, Dinar, and Yen are national currencies.
- **B — Peso:** Peso is the official currency of countries such as Mexico and the Philippines.
- **C — Dinar:** Dinar is the official currency of countries such as Kuwait and Jordan.
- **D — Yen:** Yen is the official currency of Japan.
- **Exam Trick:** Do not confuse Carat (mass/purity) with Karat (gold purity); both are units, not currencies.

### Q338 · Word Classification

Select the odd word from the given alternatives.

A. Ganga
B. Chilika
C. Yamuna
D. Godavari

**Answer:** B. Chilika
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Chilika (Correct):** Chilika is a brackish water lagoon (lake) in Odisha, whereas Ganga, Yamuna, and Godavari are major Indian rivers.
- **A — Ganga:** Ganga is a major perennial river flowing through northern India.
- **C — Yamuna:** Yamuna is a major river of northern India and the largest tributary of the Ganga.
- **D — Godavari:** Godavari is the longest river in peninsular India.
- **Exam Trick:** Chilika Lake is the largest brackish water lagoon in India; Wular and Sambhar are other frequently asked Indian lakes.

### Q339 · Word Classification

Select the odd word-pair from the given alternatives.

A. Vitamin A
B. Vitamin D
C. Vitamin C
D. Vitamin E

**Answer:** C. Vitamin C
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Vitamin C (Correct):** Vitamin C is a water-soluble vitamin, whereas Vitamin A, Vitamin D, and Vitamin E are fat-soluble vitamins.
- **A — Vitamin A:** Vitamin A is a fat-soluble vitamin essential for vision.
- **B — Vitamin D:** Vitamin D is a fat-soluble vitamin that aids calcium absorption.
- **D — Vitamin E:** Vitamin E is a fat-soluble vitamin that acts as an antioxidant.
- **Exam Trick:** Remember the mnemonic 'KEDA' (Vitamins K, E, D, A) for fat-soluble vitamins; Vitamins B-complex and C are water-soluble.

### Q340 · Word Classification

Select the odd word-pair from the given alternatives.

A. Japan : Tokyo
B. France : Paris
C. Italy : Rome
D. Australia : Sydney

**Answer:** D. Australia : Sydney
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Australia : Sydney (Correct):** In the first three pairs, the second word is the official capital of the country; however, the capital of Australia is Canberra, not Sydney.
- **A — Japan : Tokyo:** Tokyo is the official capital of Japan, matching the Country : Capital relation.
- **B — France : Paris:** Paris is the official capital of France, matching the Country : Capital relation.
- **C — Italy : Rome:** Rome is the official capital of Italy, matching the Country : Capital relation.
- **Exam Trick:** SSC CGL/CHSL loves 'trap' commercial capitals like Sydney (Australia), New York (USA), Toronto (Canada), and Rio de Janeiro (Brazil).

### Q341 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. Anemometer : Humidity
B. Barometer : Pressure
C. Ammeter : Current
D. Thermometer : Temperature

**Answer:** A. Anemometer : Humidity
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Anemometer : Humidity (Correct):** An Anemometer measures wind speed, not humidity (which is measured by a Hygrometer); all other pairs correctly match an instrument to the quantity it measures.
- **B — Barometer : Pressure:** A Barometer correctly measures atmospheric pressure.
- **C — Ammeter : Current:** An Ammeter correctly measures electric current.
- **D — Thermometer : Temperature:** A Thermometer correctly measures temperature.
- **Exam Trick:** Key instruments for SSC: Barometer (pressure), Hygrometer (humidity), Anemometer (wind speed), Ammeter (electric current), Seismograph (earthquakes).

### Q342 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 3
B. 12
C. 5
D. 9

**Answer:** B. 12
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 12 (Correct):** 12 is the only even number in the group; 3, 5, and 9 are all odd numbers.
- **A — 3:** The number 3 is an odd integer.
- **C — 5:** The number 5 is an odd integer.
- **D — 9:** The number 9 is an odd integer.
- **Exam Trick:** In number classification, always test primality, squares/cubes, and divisibility first, and parity (odd/even) when simpler properties hold.

### Q343 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 63
B. 16
C. 25
D. 49

**Answer:** A. 63
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 63 (Correct):** 63 is not a perfect square, whereas 16 (4^2), 25 (5^2), and 49 (7^2) are all perfect squares.
- **B — 16:** The number 16 is a perfect square equal to 4 squared.
- **C — 25:** The number 25 is a perfect square equal to 5 squared.
- **D — 49:** The number 49 is a perfect square equal to 7 squared.
- **Exam Trick:** Memorizing squares up to 30 and cubes up to 15 allows you to spot square/cube outliers in under 3 seconds.

### Q344 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 2
B. 5
C. 9
D. 7

**Answer:** C. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 9 (Correct):** 9 is a composite number (3 x 3), whereas 2, 5, and 7 are prime numbers.
- **A — 2:** The number 2 is a prime number with only two factors, 1 and 2.
- **B — 5:** The number 5 is a prime number with only two factors, 1 and 5.
- **D — 7:** The number 7 is a prime number with only two factors, 1 and 7.
- **Exam Trick:** Remember that 2 is the smallest and only even prime number, while 1 is neither prime nor composite.

### Q345 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 27
B. 64
C. 125
D. 144

**Answer:** D. 144
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 144 (Correct):** 144 is a perfect square (12^2) and not a cube, whereas 27 (3^3), 64 (4^3), and 125 (5^3) are perfect cubes.
- **A — 27:** The number 27 is a perfect cube equal to 3 cubed.
- **B — 64:** The number 64 is a perfect cube equal to 4 cubed.
- **C — 125:** The number 125 is a perfect cube equal to 5 cubed.
- **Exam Trick:** Note that 64 is both a square (8^2) and a cube (4^3), so check whether the other numbers in the set are cubes or squares.

### Q346 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 41
B. 49
C. 43
D. 47

**Answer:** B. 49
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 49 (Correct):** 49 is a composite number (7 x 7), whereas 41, 43, and 47 are prime numbers.
- **A — 41:** The number 41 is a prime number divisible only by 1 and itself.
- **C — 43:** The number 43 is a prime number divisible only by 1 and itself.
- **D — 47:** The number 47 is a prime number divisible only by 1 and itself.
- **Exam Trick:** There are 25 prime numbers between 1 and 100; 49, 51, 57, 87, and 91 are common composite traps in SSC exams.

### Q347 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 121
B. 143
C. 178
D. 165

**Answer:** C. 178
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 178 (Correct):** 178 is not divisible by 11, whereas 121 (11 x 11), 143 (11 x 13), and 165 (11 x 15) are all multiples of 11.
- **A — 121:** The number 121 is divisible by 11 (11 x 11 = 121).
- **B — 143:** The number 143 is divisible by 11 (11 x 13 = 143).
- **D — 165:** The number 165 is divisible by 11 (11 x 15 = 165).
- **Exam Trick:** To test divisibility by 11 for a 3-digit number, check if (hundreds + units - tens) is 0 or a multiple of 11.

### Q348 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 531
B. 352
C. 424
D. 613

**Answer:** A. 531
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 531 (Correct):** The sum of the digits of 531 is 5 + 3 + 1 = 9, whereas the sum of the digits of 352, 424, and 613 is 10 in each case.
- **B — 352:** The digits of 352 sum to 3 + 5 + 2 = 10.
- **C — 424:** The digits of 424 sum to 4 + 2 + 4 = 10.
- **D — 613:** The digits of 613 sum to 6 + 1 + 3 = 10.
- **Exam Trick:** When 3-digit or 4-digit numbers show no obvious factor or square pattern, always check the sum of digits.

### Q349 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. 26
B. 63
C. 124
D. 217

**Answer:** D. 217
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 217 (Correct):** 217 follows n^3 + 1 (6^3 + 1 = 217), whereas 26 (3^3 - 1), 63 (4^3 - 1), and 124 (5^3 - 1) all follow n^3 - 1.
- **A — 26:** The number 26 equals 3 cubed minus 1 (27 - 1).
- **B — 63:** The number 63 equals 4 cubed minus 1 (64 - 1).
- **C — 124:** The number 124 equals 5 cubed minus 1 (125 - 1).
- **Exam Trick:** Numbers adjacent to cubes (n^3 ± 1) such as 7, 9, 26, 28, 63, 65, 124, 126, 215, 217 are high-frequency SSC patterns.

### Q350 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-pair from the given alternatives.

A. 10
B. 81
C. 26
D. 50

**Answer:** B. 81
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 81 (Correct):** 81 is a perfect square (9^2), whereas 10 (3^2 + 1), 26 (5^2 + 1), and 50 (7^2 + 1) follow the pattern n^2 + 1.
- **A — 10:** The number 10 follows n^2 + 1 as 3 squared plus 1.
- **C — 26:** The number 26 follows n^2 + 1 as 5 squared plus 1.
- **D — 50:** The number 50 follows n^2 + 1 as 7 squared plus 1.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check n^2 ± 1 and n^2 ± n when numbers are close to standard perfect squares.

### Q351 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-pair from the given alternatives.

A. 7 : 49
B. 9 : 81
C. 13 : 168
D. 11 : 121

**Answer:** C. 13 : 168
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 13 : 168 (Correct):** In the other pairs, the second number is the square of the first (n : n^2); however, 13^2 is 169, not 168.
- **A — 7 : 49:** In 7 : 49, the second number is the exact square of 7.
- **B — 9 : 81:** In 9 : 81, the second number is the exact square of 9.
- **D — 11 : 121:** In 11 : 121, the second number is the exact square of 11.
- **Exam Trick:** In number-pair classification, first check basic arithmetic relations: n : n^2, n : n^3, n : kn, and n : kn ± c.

### Q352 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-pair from the given alternatives.

A. 7 : 342
B. 4 : 64
C. 5 : 125
D. 6 : 216

**Answer:** A. 7 : 342
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 7 : 342 (Correct):** In the other pairs, the second number is the cube of the first (n : n^3); however, 7^3 is 343, not 342.
- **B — 4 : 64:** In 4 : 64, the second number equals 4 cubed.
- **C — 5 : 125:** In 5 : 125, the second number equals 5 cubed.
- **D — 6 : 216:** In 6 : 216, the second number equals 6 cubed.
- **Exam Trick:** Be careful with 342 vs 343 (7^3) and 511 vs 512 (8^3), as SSC often alters the units digit by 1.

### Q353 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-pair from the given alternatives.

A. 5 : 13
B. 8 : 19
C. 11 : 25
D. 14 : 30

**Answer:** D. 14 : 30
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 14 : 30 (Correct):** The other pairs follow the rule n : 2n + 3; for 14, 2(14) + 3 = 31, not 30.
- **A — 5 : 13:** In 5 : 13, doubling 5 and adding 3 gives 13.
- **B — 8 : 19:** In 8 : 19, doubling 8 and adding 3 gives 19.
- **C — 11 : 25:** In 11 : 25, doubling 11 and adding 3 gives 25.
- **Exam Trick:** When the second number is roughly double the first, test 2n + k or 2n - k immediately.

### Q354 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-triad from the given alternatives.

A. 24 : 8
B. 53 : 16
C. 36 : 18
D. 45 : 20

**Answer:** B. 53 : 16
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 53 : 16 (Correct):** In the other pairs, the second number is the product of the digits of the first number; for 53, 5 x 3 = 15, not 16.
- **A — 24 : 8:** In 24 : 8, multiplying the digits 2 and 4 gives 8.
- **C — 36 : 18:** In 36 : 18, multiplying the digits 3 and 6 gives 18.
- **D — 45 : 20:** In 45 : 20, multiplying the digits 4 and 5 gives 20.
- **Exam Trick:** Digit-level operations (sum of digits, product of digits, difference of squares of digits) are standard SSC CGL Tier-1 patterns.

### Q355 · Number Classification

Select the odd number from the given alternatives.

A. (3, 9, 27)
B. (4, 16, 64)
C. (6, 36, 210)
D. (5, 25, 125)

**Answer:** C. (6, 36, 210)
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — (6, 36, 210) (Correct):** Each triad follows (n, n^2, n^3); for n = 6, 6^3 is 216, so (6, 36, 210) breaks the pattern.
- **A — (3, 9, 27):** The triad (3, 9, 27) correctly represents 3, 3 squared, and 3 cubed.
- **B — (4, 16, 64):** The triad (4, 16, 64) correctly represents 4, 4 squared, and 4 cubed.
- **D — (5, 25, 125):** The triad (5, 25, 125) correctly represents 5, 5 squared, and 5 cubed.
- **Exam Trick:** In number-triad questions, check both (a, a^2, a^3) and linear combinations like c = a + b or c = 2a + b.

### Q356 · Number Classification

Select the odd number-pair from the given alternatives.

A. 212
B. 133
C. 161
D. 189

**Answer:** A. 212
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 212 (Correct):** 212 is not divisible by 7 (212 = 7 x 30 + 2), whereas 133 (7 x 19), 161 (7 x 23), and 189 (7 x 27) are all multiples of 7.
- **B — 133:** The number 133 is divisible by 7 (7 x 19 = 133).
- **C — 161:** The number 161 is divisible by 7 (7 x 23 = 161).
- **D — 189:** The number 189 is divisible by 7 (7 x 27 = 189).
- **Exam Trick:** To check divisibility by 7, double the last digit and subtract it from the remaining leading number; if the result is divisible by 7, so is the original number.

### Q357 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. 12 : 48
B. 15 : 60
C. 18 : 72
D. 21 : 82

**Answer:** D. 21 : 82
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 21 : 82 (Correct):** The other pairs follow the ratio n : 4n; for 21, 21 x 4 = 84, not 82.
- **A — 12 : 48:** In 12 : 48, the second number is 4 times 12.
- **B — 15 : 60:** In 15 : 60, the second number is 4 times 15.
- **C — 18 : 72:** In 18 : 72, the second number is 4 times 18.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check simple integer ratios (2n, 3n, 4n, 5n) before looking for complex multi-step formulas.

### Q358 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. ACEG
B. BDFH
C. PRTW
D. IKMO

**Answer:** C. PRTW
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PRTW (Correct):** PRTW has gaps of +2, +2, +3 (P=16, R=18, T=20, W=23), whereas ACEG, BDFH, and IKMO all follow +2, +2, +2.
- **A — ACEG:** ACEG (1, 3, 5, 7) has a uniform +2 step between consecutive letters.
- **B — BDFH:** BDFH (2, 4, 6, 8) has a uniform +2 step between consecutive letters.
- **D — IKMO:** IKMO (9, 11, 13, 15) has a uniform +2 step between consecutive letters.
- **Exam Trick:** Write down the alphabetical positions (A=1 to Z=26) immediately using EJOTY (5, 10, 15, 20, 25) as anchor points.

### Q359 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. ADGJ
B. DGJN
C. BEHK
D. CFIL

**Answer:** B. DGJN
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — DGJN (Correct):** DGJN has gaps of +3, +3, +4 (D=4, G=7, J=10, N=14), whereas ADGJ, BEHK, and CFIL all follow +3, +3, +3.
- **A — ADGJ:** ADGJ (1, 4, 7, 10) increases by +3 at every step.
- **C — BEHK:** BEHK (2, 5, 8, 11) increases by +3 at every step.
- **D — CFIL:** CFIL (3, 6, 9, 12) increases by +3 at every step.
- **Exam Trick:** Always verify the gap between the third and fourth letters, as SSC often hides the mismatch in the final letter.

### Q360 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. KIF
B. ZXV
C. TRP
D. NLJ

**Answer:** A. KIF
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — KIF (Correct):** KIF decreases by -2, -3 (K=11, I=9, F=6), whereas ZXV, TRP, and NLJ all decrease uniformly by -2, -2.
- **B — ZXV:** ZXV (26, 24, 22) decreases by -2 at each step.
- **C — TRP:** TRP (20, 18, 16) decreases by -2 at each step.
- **D — NLJ:** NLJ (14, 12, 10) decreases by -2 at each step.
- **Exam Trick:** For reverse-order clusters, converting letters to numbers makes subtracting gaps much less error-prone than counting backwards.

### Q361 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. AZ
B. BY
C. CX
D. DV

**Answer:** D. DV
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — DV (Correct):** DV is not an opposite-letter pair (4 + 22 = 26, whereas D's opposite is W = 23); AZ, BY, and CX are opposite-letter pairs summing to 27.
- **A — AZ:** AZ is a valid opposite-letter pair (1 + 26 = 27).
- **B — BY:** BY is a valid opposite-letter pair (2 + 25 = 27).
- **C — CX:** CX is a valid opposite-letter pair (3 + 24 = 27).
- **Exam Trick:** Opposite letter pairs always have position values summing to 27 (e.g., A+Z = 1+26 = 27, D+W = 4+23 = 27).

### Q362 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. AZB
B. DVE
C. BYC
D. CXD

**Answer:** B. DVE
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — DVE (Correct):** In AZB, BYC, and CXD, the first two letters are opposite pairs and the third is +1 from the first; in DVE, D(4) and V(22) are not opposite letters (D's opposite is W).
- **A — AZB:** AZB pairs opposite letters A-Z and follows A with B (+1).
- **C — BYC:** BYC pairs opposite letters B-Y and follows B with C (+1).
- **D — CXD:** CXD pairs opposite letters C-X and follows C with D (+1).
- **Exam Trick:** 3-letter clusters that combine an opposite pair with a shifted letter are a classic SSC CGL pattern.

### Q363 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. BECD
B. FIGH
C. PQRS
D. KULM

**Answer:** C. PQRS
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — PQRS (Correct):** PQRS contains only consonants, whereas BECD (E), FIGH (I), and KULM (U) each contain one vowel.
- **A — BECD:** BECD contains the vowel E among its four letters.
- **B — FIGH:** FIGH contains the vowel I among its four letters.
- **D — KULM:** KULM contains the vowel U among its four letters.
- **Exam Trick:** If alphabetical gap patterns look identical across all four options, immediately check for vowel/consonant counts.

### Q364 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. DEGK
B. ABDG
C. BCEH
D. CDFI

**Answer:** A. DEGK
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — DEGK (Correct):** DEGK follows +1, +2, +4 (D=4, E=5, G=7, K=11), whereas ABDG, BCEH, and CDFI all follow +1, +2, +3.
- **B — ABDG:** ABDG (1, 2, 4, 7) follows the progressive +1, +2, +3 pattern.
- **C — BCEH:** BCEH (2, 3, 5, 8) follows the progressive +1, +2, +3 pattern.
- **D — CDFI:** CDFI (3, 4, 6, 9) follows the progressive +1, +2, +3 pattern.
- **Exam Trick:** Progressive gap patterns (+1, +2, +3) are among the most common 4-letter cluster rules in SSC CHSL.

### Q365 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. AEI
B. BFJ
C. CGK
D. DHM

**Answer:** D. DHM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — DHM (Correct):** DHM follows +4, +5 (D=4, H=8, M=13), whereas AEI, BFJ, and CGK all follow +4, +4.
- **A — AEI:** AEI (1, 5, 9) has a constant +4 step between consecutive letters.
- **B — BFJ:** BFJ (2, 6, 10) has a constant +4 step between consecutive letters.
- **C — CGK:** CGK (3, 7, 11) has a constant +4 step between consecutive letters.
- **Exam Trick:** Note that AEI (1, 5, 9) consists entirely of vowels, while BFJ and CGK share the exact same +4, +4 numerical step.

### Q366 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. AFD
B. DIH
C. BGE
D. CHF

**Answer:** B. DIH
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — DIH (Correct):** DIH follows +5, -1 (D=4, I=9, H=8), whereas AFD, BGE, and CHF all follow +5, -2.
- **A — AFD:** AFD (1, 6, 4) follows the +5, -2 pattern.
- **C — BGE:** BGE (2, 7, 5) follows the +5, -2 pattern.
- **D — CHF:** CHF (3, 8, 6) follows the +5, -2 pattern.
- **Exam Trick:** Alternating sign patterns (+a, -b) become obvious once you write the numeric values above the letters.

### Q367 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. ZWT
B. TQN
C. HEC
D. NKH

**Answer:** C. HEC
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — HEC (Correct):** HEC follows -3, -2 (H=8, E=5, C=3), whereas ZWT, TQN, and NKH all follow -3, -3.
- **A — ZWT:** ZWT (26, 23, 20) decreases by -3 at each step.
- **B — TQN:** TQN (20, 17, 14) decreases by -3 at each step.
- **D — NKH:** NKH (14, 11, 8) decreases by -3 at each step.
- **Exam Trick:** Keep track of exact subtraction: 5 - 3 = 2 (B), not 3 (C).

### Q368 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. MOQN
B. ACBD
C. EGFH
D. IKJL

**Answer:** A. MOQN
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — MOQN (Correct):** MOQN follows +2, +2, -3 (13, 15, 17, 14), whereas ACBD, EGFH, and IKJL all follow +2, -1, +2.
- **B — ACBD:** ACBD (1, 3, 2, 4) follows the +2, -1, +2 pattern.
- **C — EGFH:** EGFH (5, 7, 6, 8) follows the +2, -1, +2 pattern.
- **D — IKJL:** IKJL (9, 11, 10, 12) follows the +2, -1, +2 pattern.
- **Exam Trick:** Alternating +2, -1, +2 is equivalent to swapping the middle two letters of four consecutive letters (e.g., ABCD -> ACBD).

### Q369 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-cluster from the given alternatives.

A. ABBA
B. CDDC
C. EFFE
D. GHHJ

**Answer:** D. GHHJ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — GHHJ (Correct):** GHHJ is not a palindrome and its outer letters do not match (G vs J), whereas ABBA, CDDC, and EFFE are palindromic clusters of the form XYYX.
- **A — ABBA:** ABBA is a symmetric palindrome with matching outer letters (A) and doubled inner letters (BB).
- **B — CDDC:** CDDC is a symmetric palindrome with matching outer letters (C) and doubled inner letters (DD).
- **C — EFFE:** EFFE is a symmetric palindrome with matching outer letters (E) and doubled inner letters (FF).
- **Exam Trick:** Look for structural symmetry (palindromes like XYYX or repeated letters) before computing numerical gaps.

### Q370 · Letter-Cluster Classification

Select the odd letter-pair from the given alternatives.

A. ACFJ
B. DFJN
C. BDGK
D. CEHL

**Answer:** B. DFJN
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — DFJN (Correct):** DFJN follows +2, +4, +4 (D=4, F=6, J=10, N=14), whereas ACFJ, BDGK, and CEHL all follow +2, +3, +4.
- **A — ACFJ:** ACFJ (1, 3, 6, 10) follows the +2, +3, +4 pattern.
- **C — BDGK:** BDGK (2, 4, 7, 11) follows the +2, +3, +4 pattern.
- **D — CEHL:** CEHL (3, 5, 8, 12) follows the +2, +3, +4 pattern.
- **Exam Trick:** Check each consecutive difference carefully; here the middle difference (+3 vs +4) breaks the sequence.

### Q371 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Apple  2. Apply  3. Application  4. Appreciate  5. Approach

A. AD
B. EH
C. MQ
D. IL

**Answer:** C. MQ
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — MQ (Correct):** MQ has a gap of +4 (M=13, Q=17), whereas AD (1, 4), EH (5, 8), and IL (9, 12) all have a gap of +3.
- **A — AD:** AD (1, 4) has a difference of +3 between the two letters.
- **B — EH:** EH (5, 8) has a difference of +3 between the two letters.
- **D — IL:** IL (9, 12) has a difference of +3 between the two letters.
- **Exam Trick:** For 2-letter pairs, only two relations are common: constant letter difference (+k) or opposite letters (sum = 27).

### Q372 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Mountain  2. Mouse  3. Mouth  4. Mould  5. Mourn

A. 1, 3, 2, 4, 5
B. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
C. 1, 3, 2, 5, 4
D. 3, 1, 2, 4, 5

**Answer:** A. 1, 3, 2, 4, 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1, 3, 2, 4, 5 (Correct):** All five words start with 'App'. Comparing the 4th letter puts 'l' (1, 2, 3) before 'r' (4, 5). Among 'Appl-', Apple (e) comes before Application (i), which comes before Apply (y). Among 'Appr-', Appreciate (e) comes before Approach (o). Thus the order is 1, 3, 2, 4, 5.
- **B — 1, 2, 3, 4, 5:** Putting 2 before 3 is incorrect because 'i' in Application precedes 'y' in Apply.
- **C — 1, 3, 2, 5, 4:** Putting 5 before 4 is incorrect because 'e' in Appreciate precedes 'o' in Approach.
- **D — 3, 1, 2, 4, 5:** Putting 4 before 2 is incorrect because 'l' in Apply precedes 'r' in Appreciate.
- **Exam Trick:** Cancel out common prefixes first, then compare only the first differing letter at each position.

### Q373 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Direction  2. Direct  3. Director  4. Directory  5. Directly

A. 1, 4, 5, 2, 3
B. 4, 1, 5, 2, 3
C. 4, 1, 2, 5, 3
D. 4, 5, 1, 2, 3

**Answer:** B. 4, 1, 5, 2, 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 4, 1, 5, 2, 3 (Correct):** All five words start with 'Mou'. Comparing the 4th letter in alphabetical order: 'l' (Mould - 4), 'n' (Mountain - 1), 'r' (Mourn - 5), 's' (Mouse - 2), and 't' (Mouth - 3). Thus the order is 4, 1, 5, 2, 3.
- **A — 1, 4, 5, 2, 3:** Starting with 1 is incorrect because 'l' in Mould precedes 'n' in Mountain.
- **C — 4, 1, 2, 5, 3:** Putting 2 before 5 is incorrect because 'r' in Mourn precedes 's' in Mouse.
- **D — 4, 5, 1, 2, 3:** Putting 3 before 2 is incorrect because 's' in Mouse precedes 't' in Mouth.
- **Exam Trick:** Once you find the first and last words (here 4 and 3), eliminate options that do not start with 4 and end with 3.

### Q374 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Chemistry  2. Chemical  3. Chemist  4. Cherish  5. Cherry

A. 1, 2, 5, 3, 4
B. 2, 1, 3, 5, 4
C. 2, 1, 5, 3, 4
D. 2, 1, 5, 4, 3

**Answer:** C. 2, 1, 5, 3, 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 2, 1, 5, 3, 4 (Correct):** All words share the prefix 'Direct'. The shortest word Direct (2) comes first. Next, comparing the 7th letter: 'i' (Direction - 1), 'l' (Directly - 5), and 'o' (3 and 4). Between Director (3) and Directory (4), the shorter word Director comes first. Thus the order is 2, 1, 5, 3, 4.
- **A — 1, 2, 5, 3, 4:** Starting with 1 is incorrect because the shorter root word Direct (2) precedes Direction.
- **B — 2, 1, 3, 5, 4:** Putting 3 before 5 is incorrect because 'l' in Directly precedes 'o' in Director.
- **D — 2, 1, 5, 4, 3:** Putting 4 before 3 is incorrect because Director is a prefix of Directory and must precede it.
- **Exam Trick:** When one word is an exact prefix of another (e.g., Direct vs Direction, Director vs Directory), the shorter word always comes first in a dictionary.

### Q375 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Praise  2. Practice  3. Practical  4. Pray  5. Prank

A. 2, 1, 3, 4, 5
B. 3, 2, 1, 4, 5
C. 2, 3, 1, 5, 4
D. 2, 3, 1, 4, 5

**Answer:** D. 2, 3, 1, 4, 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 2, 3, 1, 4, 5 (Correct):** All words start with 'Che'. Comparing the 4th letter places 'm' (1, 2, 3) before 'r' (4, 5). Within 'Chem-', Chemical (2) precedes Chemist (3), which precedes Chemistry (1). Within 'Cher-', Cherish (4) precedes Cherry (5). Thus the order is 2, 3, 1, 4, 5.
- **A — 2, 1, 3, 4, 5:** Putting 1 before 3 is incorrect because Chemist is a prefix of Chemistry and comes first.
- **B — 3, 2, 1, 4, 5:** Putting 3 before 2 is incorrect because 'c' in Chemical precedes 's' in Chemist.
- **C — 2, 3, 1, 5, 4:** Putting 5 before 4 is incorrect because 'i' in Cherish precedes 'r' in Cherry.
- **Exam Trick:** In SSC exams, comparing just the first two words and the last two words often narrows down the options to a single choice.

### Q376 · Dictionary Order

If the following five words are arranged in dictionary order, which word will come THIRD?
1. Banquet  2. Banner  3. Banish  4. Bandit  5. Bank

A. 3, 2, 1, 5, 4
B. 2, 3, 1, 5, 4
C. 3, 2, 1, 4, 5
D. 1, 3, 2, 5, 4

**Answer:** A. 3, 2, 1, 5, 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 3, 2, 1, 5, 4 (Correct):** All words start with 'Pra'. Comparing the 4th letter gives 'c' (2, 3), 'i' (1), 'n' (5), and 'y' (4). Between Practical (3) and Practice (2), the 9th letter 'a' precedes 'e'. Thus the order is 3, 2, 1, 5, 4.
- **B — 2, 3, 1, 5, 4:** Putting 2 before 3 is incorrect because 'a' in Practical precedes 'e' in Practice.
- **C — 3, 2, 1, 4, 5:** Putting 4 before 5 is incorrect because 'n' in Prank precedes 'y' in Pray.
- **D — 1, 3, 2, 5, 4:** Putting 1 before 3 is incorrect because 'c' in Practical precedes 'i' in Praise.
- **Exam Trick:** Be careful with long shared prefixes like 'Practi-'; scan letter by letter without skipping characters.

### Q377 · Dictionary Order

If the following five words are arranged in dictionary order, which word will come LAST?
1. Magnet  2. Magic  3. Magnificent  4. Magazine  5. Magistrate

A. Banish
B. Bank
C. Banner
D. Banquet

**Answer:** B. Bank
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Bank (Correct):** Arranging in dictionary order by the 4th letter: Bandit (d), Banish (i), Bank (k), Banner (n), Banquet (q). Therefore, Bank comes third.
- **A — Banish:** Banish comes second in dictionary order because 'i' follows 'd' (Bandit) and precedes 'k' (Bank).
- **C — Banner:** Banner comes fourth in dictionary order because 'n' follows 'k' (Bank).
- **D — Banquet:** Banquet comes fifth (last) in dictionary order because 'q' follows 'n' (Banner).
- **Exam Trick:** Read the question carefully to check whether it asks for the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, or last word, or reverse dictionary order.

### Q378 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Select  2. Seldom  3. Selfish  4. Seller  5. Sentence

A. Magnet
B. Magistrate
C. Magnificent
D. Magic

**Answer:** C. Magnificent
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Magnificent (Correct):** Arranging in dictionary order: Magazine (Maga-), Magic (Magic), Magistrate (Magis-), Magnet (Magne-), Magnificent (Magni-). Hence, Magnificent comes last.
- **A — Magnet:** Magnet comes fourth because 'e' in Magnet precedes 'i' in Magnificent.
- **B — Magistrate:** Magistrate comes third because 'i' at the 4th position precedes 'n' in Magnet and Magnificent.
- **D — Magic:** Magic comes second because it is shorter than Magistrate and precedes Magnet.
- **Exam Trick:** To find the last word quickly, only track the words with the alphabetically largest letters at each differing position.

### Q379 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Trigger  2. Triangle  3. Triumph  4. Trick  5. Tribute

A. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
B. 2, 1, 4, 3, 5
C. 2, 3, 1, 4, 5
D. 2, 1, 3, 4, 5

**Answer:** D. 2, 1, 3, 4, 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 2, 1, 3, 4, 5 (Correct):** Comparing the 3rd letter places 'l' (1, 2, 3, 4) before 'n' (Sentence - 5). Among 'Sel-', comparing the 4th letter gives: 'd' (Seldom - 2), 'e' (Select - 1), 'f' (Selfish - 3), and 'l' (Seller - 4). Thus the order is 2, 1, 3, 4, 5.
- **A — 1, 2, 3, 4, 5:** Starting with 1 is incorrect because 'd' in Seldom precedes 'e' in Select.
- **B — 2, 1, 4, 3, 5:** Putting 4 before 3 is incorrect because 'f' in Selfish precedes 'l' in Seller.
- **C — 2, 3, 1, 4, 5:** Putting 3 before 1 is incorrect because 'e' in Select precedes 'f' in Selfish.
- **Exam Trick:** When all words share only two letters, the 3rd or 4th letter immediately settles most of the order.

### Q380 · Dictionary Order

Arrange the given words in the sequence in which they occur in the dictionary:
1. Knowledge  2. Knight  3. Knit  4. Knock  5. Knot

A. 2, 5, 4, 1, 3
B. 2, 4, 5, 1, 3
C. 2, 5, 4, 3, 1
D. 5, 2, 4, 1, 3

**Answer:** A. 2, 5, 4, 1, 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2, 5, 4, 1, 3 (Correct):** All five words start with 'Tri'. Comparing the 4th letter in alphabetical order: 'a' (Triangle - 2), 'b' (Tribute - 5), 'c' (Trick - 4), 'g' (Trigger - 1), and 'u' (Triumph - 3). Thus the order is 2, 5, 4, 1, 3.
- **B — 2, 4, 5, 1, 3:** Putting 4 before 5 is incorrect because 'b' in Tribute precedes 'c' in Trick.
- **C — 2, 5, 4, 3, 1:** Putting 3 before 1 is incorrect because 'g' in Trigger precedes 'u' in Triumph.
- **D — 5, 2, 4, 1, 3:** Starting with 5 is incorrect because 'a' in Triangle precedes 'b' in Tribute.
- **Exam Trick:** Do not get distracted by the original numbering; write the 4th letter next to each number (1:g, 2:a, 3:u, 4:c, 5:b) and sort.

### Q381 · Logical Word Sequence

Arrange the following words in a logical and meaningful order:
1. Seed  2. Fruit  3. Plant  4. Flower  5. Sapling

A. 3, 2, 4, 5, 1
B. 2, 3, 4, 5, 1
C. 2, 3, 4, 1, 5
D. 2, 3, 5, 4, 1

**Answer:** B. 2, 3, 4, 5, 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2, 3, 4, 5, 1 (Correct):** All words start with 'Kn'. Comparing the 3rd letter places 'i' (2, 3) before 'o' (1, 4, 5). Between Knight (2) and Knit (3), 'g' precedes 't'. Among 'Kno-', comparing the 4th letter gives 'c' (Knock - 4), 't' (Knot - 5), and 'w' (Knowledge - 1). Thus the order is 2, 3, 4, 5, 1.
- **A — 3, 2, 4, 5, 1:** Putting 3 before 2 is incorrect because 'g' in Knight precedes 't' in Knit.
- **C — 2, 3, 4, 1, 5:** Putting 1 before 5 is incorrect because 't' in Knot precedes 'w' in Knowledge.
- **D — 2, 3, 5, 4, 1:** Putting 5 before 4 is incorrect because 'c' in Knock precedes 't' in Knot.
- **Exam Trick:** In SSC questions, always check whether the 3rd letter splits the words into two clear groups before sorting within each group.

### Q382 · Logical Word Sequence

Arrange the following words in a logical and meaningful order:
1. Job  2. School  3. College  4. Salary  5. Interview

A. 1, 3, 5, 4, 2
B. 1, 5, 3, 2, 4
C. 1, 5, 3, 4, 2
D. 5, 1, 3, 4, 2

**Answer:** C. 1, 5, 3, 4, 2
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1, 5, 3, 4, 2 (Correct):** The natural growth sequence of a plant is: Seed (1) -> Sapling (5) -> Plant (3) -> Flower (4) -> Fruit (2).
- **A — 1, 3, 5, 4, 2:** Placing Plant (3) before Sapling (5) reverses the natural growth stage from young shoot to mature plant.
- **B — 1, 5, 3, 2, 4:** Placing Fruit (2) before Flower (4) is biologically reversed, as flowers develop into fruits.
- **D — 5, 1, 3, 4, 2:** Starting with Sapling (5) skips the initial Seed (1) stage.
- **Exam Trick:** For biological lifecycle sequences, trace the progression from origin/birth through intermediate growth to final maturity or output.

### Q383 · Logical Word Sequence

Arrange the following words in a logical and meaningful order:
1. State  2. District  3. Country  4. Village  5. Continent

A. 2, 3, 5, 1, 4
B. 2, 3, 1, 5, 4
C. 2, 3, 5, 4, 1
D. 3, 2, 5, 1, 4

**Answer:** A. 2, 3, 5, 1, 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2, 3, 5, 1, 4 (Correct):** The chronological career progression is: School (2) -> College (3) -> Interview (5) -> Job (1) -> Salary (4).
- **B — 2, 3, 1, 5, 4:** Placing Job (1) before Interview (5) reverses the standard hiring process.
- **C — 2, 3, 5, 4, 1:** Placing Salary (4) before Job (1) is illogical because salary is earned after securing a job.
- **D — 3, 2, 5, 1, 4:** Placing College (3) before School (2) reverses the educational hierarchy.
- **Exam Trick:** Career and recruitment sequences always move chronologically: Education -> Application/Interview -> Selection/Job -> Remuneration.

### Q384 · Logical Word Sequence

Arrange the following words in a logical and meaningful order:
1. Printing  2. Writing  3. Reading  4. Publishing  5. Proofreading

A. 4, 1, 2, 3, 5
B. 2, 4, 1, 3, 5
C. 4, 2, 1, 5, 3
D. 4, 2, 1, 3, 5

**Answer:** D. 4, 2, 1, 3, 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4, 2, 1, 3, 5 (Correct):** Arranging from smallest to largest geographical/administrative unit: Village (4) -> District (2) -> State (1) -> Country (3) -> Continent (5).
- **A — 4, 1, 2, 3, 5:** Placing State (1) before District (2) mixes up the ascending administrative hierarchy.
- **B — 2, 4, 1, 3, 5:** Starting with District (2) before Village (4) breaks the smallest-to-largest order.
- **C — 4, 2, 1, 5, 3:** Placing Continent (5) before Country (3) reverses the ascending geographical scale.
- **Exam Trick:** When options allow either ascending or descending order, check whether the choices start with the smallest (4) or largest (5) unit.

### Q385 · Logical Word Sequence

Arrange the following words in a logical and meaningful order:
1. Rain  2. Cloud  3. Evaporation  4. Ocean  5. Condensation

A. 2, 1, 5, 4, 3
B. 2, 5, 1, 4, 3
C. 2, 5, 4, 1, 3
D. 2, 5, 1, 3, 4

**Answer:** B. 2, 5, 1, 4, 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2, 5, 1, 4, 3 (Correct):** The stages of creating and consuming a book are: Writing (2) -> Proofreading (5) -> Printing (1) -> Publishing (4) -> Reading (3).
- **A — 2, 1, 5, 4, 3:** Placing Printing (1) before Proofreading (5) would mean printing an unedited manuscript.
- **C — 2, 5, 4, 1, 3:** Placing Publishing (4) before Printing (1) reverses the physical production sequence.
- **D — 2, 5, 1, 3, 4:** Placing Reading (3) before Publishing (4) skips the release of the book to readers.
