Six persons A, B, C, D, E, and F are sitting around a circular table facing outward. A is sitting opposite D. B is sitting to the immediate left of A. C is sitting second to the left of A. E is sitting to the immediate right of F. Who is sitting to the immediate right of A?

A. B
B. F
C. E
D. C

**Answer:** B. F
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — F (Correct):** Facing outward reverses the directions: counter-clockwise (+1) is LEFT and clockwise (-1) is RIGHT. Place A at 1 and D at 4 (opposite). B is at 2 (immediate left of A) and C is at 3 (2nd left of A). For E to be to the immediate right (-1) of F using seats 5 and 6, F must be at 6 and E at 5. Thus F(6) is to the immediate right (-1) of A(1).
- **A — B:** B is to the immediate left of A, not immediate right.
- **C — E:** E is two places to the right of A.
- **D — C:** C is two places to the left of A.
- **Exam Trick:** When facing outward (away from centre): Left = Counter-Clockwise (CCW), Right = Clockwise (CW) — the exact opposite of facing the centre.

# Section III: Directions, Clocks, Calendars & Logic

## Chapter 7: Direction Sense & Distance

### Q476 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

A hiker walks 3 km north and then turns east to walk 4 km. What is the straight-line distance from the starting point?

A. 5.00 km
B. 6.00 km
C. 5.50 km
D. 7.00 km

**Answer:** A. 5.00 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 5.00 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 4 km East and 3 km North. Using Pythagoras' theorem, √(3² + 4²) = √(9 + 16) = √25 = 5.00 km.
- **B — 6.00 km:** Adding the two distances directly (3 + 4) gives the total path length, not the shortest straight-line distance.
- **C — 5.50 km:** 6.00 km is an incorrect estimate that neither equals the hypotenuse nor the total distance walked.
- **D — 7.00 km:** 5.50 km miscalculates √25 by averaging or rounding errors.
- **Exam Trick:** 3-4-5 is the most fundamental Pythagorean triple frequently tested in SSC CHSL and CGL.

### Q477 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Rohan cycles 6 km east and then 8 km north to reach his school. What is the shortest distance between his home and school?

A. 14.00 km
B. 10.00 km
C. 12.00 km
D. 9.50 km

**Answer:** B. 10.00 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 10.00 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 6 km East and 8 km North. Shortest distance = √(6² + 8²) = √(36 + 64) = √100 = 10.00 km.
- **A — 14.00 km:** 14.00 km is the total distance cycled (6 + 8), whereas shortest distance requires the hypotenuse.
- **C — 12.00 km:** 12.00 km overestimates the hypotenuse by treating the legs closer to a linear sum.
- **D — 9.50 km:** 9.50 km underestimates √100, which is an exact square.
- **Exam Trick:** Scaling the 3-4-5 triple by 2 immediately gives 6-8-10 without computing squares.

### Q478 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Priya drives 5 km west and then 12 km south. How far is she in a straight line from her starting point?

A. 15 km
B. 17 km
C. 13 km
D. 11 km

**Answer:** C. 13 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 13 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 5 km West and 12 km South. Straight-line distance = √(5² + 12²) = √(25 + 144) = √169 = 13 km.
- **A — 15 km:** 17 km is the total distance driven (5 + 12) rather than the straight-line displacement.
- **B — 17 km:** 15 km confuses the 5-12-13 triple with the 9-12-15 triple.
- **D — 11 km:** 11 km results from subtracting or miscalculating √(144 - 25).
- **Exam Trick:** 5-12-13 is a standard primitive Pythagorean triple; memorizing it saves calculation time.

### Q479 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

A delivery van travels 8 km north and then 15 km west. Find the straight-line distance from its starting point.

A. 23 km
B. 16 km
C. 19 km
D. 17 km

**Answer:** D. 17 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 17 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 8 km North and 15 km West. Distance = √(8² + 15²) = √(64 + 225) = √289 = 17 km.
- **A — 23 km:** 23 km is the sum of both legs (8 + 15), not the hypotenuse.
- **B — 16 km:** 19 km overestimates √289; note that 19² = 361.
- **C — 19 km:** 16 km underestimates the hypotenuse; 16² = 256 is less than 289.
- **Exam Trick:** Recognize 8-15-17 (8² + 15² = 289 = 17²) as a classic SSC CGL Tier-1 triple.

### Q480 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Ankit runs 7 km south and then 24 km east. What is his straight-line distance from the starting point?

A. 25 km
B. 26 km
C. 23 km
D. 31 km

**Answer:** A. 25 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 25 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 7 km South and 24 km East. Distance = √(7² + 24²) = √(49 + 576) = √625 = 25 km.
- **B — 26 km:** 31 km adds both path segments (7 + 24) instead of finding the hypotenuse.
- **C — 23 km:** 26 km confuses 7-24-25 with the 10-24-26 triple.
- **D — 31 km:** 23 km is smaller than the longer leg (24 km), which is impossible for a hypotenuse.
- **Exam Trick:** The hypotenuse of a right triangle is always strictly greater than either perpendicular leg.

### Q481 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Meera walks 9 km east and then 12 km south. What is the shortest distance back to her starting point?

A. 21 km
B. 15 km
C. 16 km
D. 14 km

**Answer:** B. 15 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 15 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 9 km East and 12 km South. Shortest distance = √(9² + 12²) = √(81 + 144) = √225 = 15 km.
- **A — 21 km:** 21 km is the total distance walked (9 + 12) instead of the straight-line distance.
- **C — 16 km:** 16 km overestimates √225; 16² = 256.
- **D — 14 km:** 14 km underestimates √225; 14² = 196.
- **Exam Trick:** 9-12-15 is simply 3 times the 3-4-5 Pythagorean triple.

### Q482 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

A cyclist rides 12 km north and then 16 km east. How far is the cyclist from the starting point?

A. 28 km
B. 22 km
C. 20 km
D. 18 km

**Answer:** C. 20 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 20 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 12 km North and 16 km East. Distance = √(12² + 16²) = √(144 + 256) = √400 = 20 km.
- **A — 28 km:** 28 km is the total path length (12 + 16), not the shortest distance.
- **B — 22 km:** 22 km overestimates √400; 22² = 484.
- **D — 18 km:** 18 km underestimates √400; 18² = 324.
- **Exam Trick:** 12-16-20 is 4 times the 3-4-5 Pythagorean triple.

### Q483 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

A drone flies 9 km west and then 40 km north. What is its straight-line distance from the launch pad?

A. 49 km
B. 45 km
C. 43 km
D. 41 km

**Answer:** D. 41 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 41 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 9 km West and 40 km North. Distance = √(9² + 40²) = √(81 + 1600) = √1681 = 41 km.
- **A — 49 km:** 49 km is the sum of the two legs (9 + 40), not the hypotenuse.
- **B — 45 km:** 45 km overestimates √1681 considerably.
- **C — 43 km:** 43 km is an incorrect value; 43² = 1849.
- **Exam Trick:** 9-40-41 is a less common but high-yield primitive Pythagorean triple in CGL.

### Q484 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Suresh walks 10 km south and then 24 km west. Find the straight-line distance from his starting point.

A. 26 km
B. 34 km
C. 28 km
D. 25 km

**Answer:** A. 26 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 26 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 10 km South and 24 km West. Distance = √(10² + 24²) = √(100 + 576) = √676 = 26 km.
- **B — 34 km:** 34 km adds the two perpendicular segments (10 + 24) instead of using Pythagoras' theorem.
- **C — 28 km:** 28 km overestimates √676; 28² = 784.
- **D — 25 km:** 25 km confuses 10-24-26 with the 7-24-25 triple.
- **Exam Trick:** 10-24-26 is double the 5-12-13 Pythagorean triple.

### Q485 · Pythagorean Triples & Exact Distance

Kavita drives 15 km east and then 20 km north. What is the shortest distance from her starting point?

A. 35 km
B. 25 km
C. 27 km
D. 30 km

**Answer:** B. 25 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 25 km (Correct):** Net displacement is 15 km East and 20 km North. Distance = √(15² + 20²) = √(225 + 400) = √625 = 25 km.
- **A — 35 km:** 35 km is the total distance driven (15 + 20) rather than the displacement.
- **C — 27 km:** 30 km overestimates √625; 30² = 900.
- **D — 30 km:** 27 km is incorrect because 27² = 729.
- **Exam Trick:** 15-20-25 is 5 times the 3-4-5 Pythagorean triple.

### Q486 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Aarav walks 10 km east and then 8 km north. What is the straight-line distance from his starting point?

A. 18.00 km
B. 13.45 km
C. 12.81 km
D. 11.66 km

**Answer:** C. 12.81 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 12.81 km (Correct):** Displacement is 10 km East and 8 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(10² + 8²) = √(100 + 64) = √164 ≈ 12.81 km.
- **A — 18.00 km:** Adding 10 and 8 gives total distance walked, not straight-line displacement.
- **B — 13.45 km:** 13.45 km corresponds to √(10² + 9²) rather than √(10² + 8²).
- **D — 11.66 km:** 11.66 km corresponds to √(10² + 6²) rather than √(10² + 8²).
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q487 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Neha cycles 10 km east and then 6 km north. What is the straight-line distance from her starting point?

A. 16.00 km
B. 12.81 km
C. 10.77 km
D. 11.66 km

**Answer:** D. 11.66 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 11.66 km (Correct):** Displacement is 10 km East and 6 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(10² + 6²) = √(100 + 36) = √136 ≈ 11.66 km.
- **A — 16.00 km:** 16.00 km is the total path length (10 + 6), not the hypotenuse.
- **B — 12.81 km:** 12.81 km comes from legs of 10 km and 8 km.
- **C — 10.77 km:** 10.77 km comes from legs of 10 km and 4 km.
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q488 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Vikram drives 10 km east and then 4 km north. What is the straight-line distance from his starting point?

A. 10.77 km
B. 14.00 km
C. 11.18 km
D. 9.85 km

**Answer:** A. 10.77 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10.77 km (Correct):** Displacement is 10 km East and 4 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(10² + 4²) = √(100 + 16) = √116 ≈ 10.77 km.
- **B — 14.00 km:** 14.00 km adds the two legs (10 + 4) instead of applying Pythagoras' theorem.
- **C — 11.18 km:** 11.18 km is √(10² + 5²), not √(10² + 4²).
- **D — 9.85 km:** 9.85 km is smaller than the 10 km leg, which is impossible for a hypotenuse.
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q489 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Pooja walks 6 km east and then 6 km north. What is the straight-line distance from her starting point?

A. 12.00 km
B. 8.49 km
C. 7.81 km
D. 9.22 km

**Answer:** B. 8.49 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8.49 km (Correct):** Displacement is 6 km East and 6 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(6² + 6²) = √(36 + 36) = √72 ≈ 8.49 km.
- **A — 12.00 km:** 12.00 km is the sum of both legs (6 + 6).
- **C — 7.81 km:** 7.81 km corresponds to √(6² + 5²).
- **D — 9.22 km:** 9.22 km corresponds to √(6² + 7²).
- **Exam Trick:** When two perpendicular legs are equal (a = b), the hypotenuse is simply a√2 ≈ 1.414 × a.

Deepak jogs 6 km east and then 4 km north. What is the straight-line distance from his starting point?

A. 10.00 km
B. 8.06 km
C. 7.21 km
D. 6.40 km

**Answer:** C. 7.21 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 7.21 km (Correct):** Displacement is 6 km East and 4 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(6² + 4²) = √(36 + 16) = √52 ≈ 7.21 km.
- **A — 10.00 km:** 10.00 km is the total distance jogged (6 + 4), not the shortest distance.
- **B — 8.06 km:** 8.06 km corresponds to √(4² + 7²).
- **D — 6.40 km:** 6.40 km corresponds to √(5² + 4²).
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

D. 12.53 km

**Answer:** D. 12.53 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**

- **A — 17.00 km:** 17.00 km is the sum of the two segments (6 + 11).
- **B — 11.66 km:** 13.42 km corresponds to √(6² + 12²).
- **C — 13.42 km:** 11.66 km corresponds to √(6² + 10²).

- **C — 15.62 km:** 15.62 km corresponds to √(10² + 12²).

### Q498 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Amit jogs 6 km east and then 5 km north. What is the straight-line distance from his starting point?

A. 7.21 km
B. 11.00 km
C. 7.81 km
D. 8.49 km

**Answer:** C. 7.81 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 7.81 km (Correct):** Displacement is 6 km East and 5 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(6² + 5²) = √(36 + 25) = √61 ≈ 7.81 km.
- **A — 7.21 km:** 11.00 km is the sum of the two distances (6 + 5).
- **B — 11.00 km:** 7.21 km corresponds to √(6² + 4²).
- **D — 8.49 km:** 8.49 km corresponds to √(6² + 6²).
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q499 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Sneha drives 6 km east and then 12 km north. What is the straight-line distance from her starting point?

A. 14.14 km
B. 18.00 km
C. 12.53 km
D. 13.42 km

**Answer:** D. 13.42 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 13.42 km (Correct):** Displacement is 6 km East and 12 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(6² + 12²) = √(36 + 144) = √180 ≈ 13.42 km.
- **A — 14.14 km:** 18.00 km adds the two legs (6 + 12) instead of finding the shortest distance.
- **B — 18.00 km:** 12.53 km corresponds to √(6² + 11²).
- **C — 12.53 km:** 14.14 km corresponds to √(10² + 10²).
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q500 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Varun travels 10 km east and then 12 km north. What is the straight-line distance from his starting point?

A. 15.62 km
B. 22.00 km
C. 14.87 km
D. 16.40 km

**Answer:** A. 15.62 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 15.62 km (Correct):** Displacement is 10 km East and 12 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(10² + 12²) = √(100 + 144) = √244 ≈ 15.62 km.
- **B — 22.00 km:** 22.00 km is the total distance travelled (10 + 12).
- **C — 14.87 km:** 14.87 km corresponds to √(10² + 11²).
- **D — 16.40 km:** 16.40 km overestimates √244; 16.4² = 268.96.
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q501 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

Sonal walks 10 km east and then 10 km north. What is the straight-line distance from her starting point?

A. 20.00 km
B. 14.14 km
C. 13.45 km
D. 15.62 km

**Answer:** B. 14.14 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 14.14 km (Correct):** Displacement is 10 km East and 10 km North. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(10² + 10²) = √(100 + 100) = √200 ≈ 14.14 km.
- **A — 20.00 km:** 20.00 km is the total path length (10 + 10).
- **C — 13.45 km:** 13.45 km corresponds to √(10² + 9²).
- **D — 15.62 km:** 15.62 km corresponds to √(10² + 12²).
- **Exam Trick:** When two perpendicular legs are equal (a = b), the hypotenuse is simply a√2 ≈ 1.414 × a.

### Q502 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

A cyclist rides 4 km east and then 7 km south. Find the straight-line distance from the start.

A. 11.00 km
B. 7.62 km
C. 8.06 km
D. 8.60 km

**Answer:** C. 8.06 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 8.06 km (Correct):** Displacement is 4 km East and 7 km South. By Pythagoras' theorem, distance = √(4² + 7²) = √(16 + 49) = √65 ≈ 8.06 km.
- **A — 11.00 km:** 11.00 km is the total distance ridden (4 + 7).
- **B — 7.62 km:** 7.62 km corresponds to √(3² + 7²).
- **D — 8.60 km:** 8.60 km corresponds to √(5² + 7²).
- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q503 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

A runner covers 7 km north and then 7 km east. Find the straight-line distance from the start.

A. 14.00 km
B. 10.63 km
C. 9.22 km
D. 9.90 km

**Answer:** D. 9.90 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**

- **A — 14.00 km:** 14.00 km adds both legs (7 + 7) instead of computing 7√2.
- **B — 10.63 km:** 9.22 km corresponds to √(6² + 7²).
- **C — 9.22 km:** 10.63 km corresponds to √(8² + 7²).

- **C — 13.00 km:** 9.22 km corresponds to √(6² + 7²).

### Q505 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

A hiker treks 3 km north and then 10 km east. Find the straight-line distance from the start.

- **A — 13.00 km:** 13.00 km is the sum of both legs (3 + 10).
- **C — 10.77 km:** 10.77 km corresponds to √(4² + 10²).

### Q506 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

**Notes & Option Breakdown:**

- **B — 8.00 km:** 6.40 km corresponds to √(5² + 4²).

### Q507 · 2-Leg Straight-Line Distance

A van drives 5 km south and then 7 km west. Find the straight-line distance from the start.

**Answer:** D. 8.60 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**

- **A — 12.00 km:** 12.00 km adds the two legs (5 + 7).
- **B — 8.06 km:** 8.06 km corresponds to √(4² + 7²).

- **Exam Trick:** For non-triple legs, compute √(a² + b²) and bound it between the nearest perfect squares.

### Q508 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A person walks 10 km east, turns right to walk 7 km, and then turns right again to walk 2 km. What is the straight-line distance from the starting point?

A. 10.63 km
B. 19.00 km
C. 11.40 km
D. 9.85 km

**Answer:** A. 10.63 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10.63 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 8 East and 7 South. Distance = √(8² + 7²) = 10.63 km.
- **B — 19.00 km:** 19.00 km is the total distance walked (10 + 7 + 2).
- **C — 11.40 km:** 11.40 km corresponds to √(9² + 7²).
- **D — 9.85 km:** 9.85 km corresponds to √(9² + 4²).
- **Exam Trick:** Always cancel opposite directions first: 10 km East − 2 km West = 8 km East.

### Q509 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Anjali walks 5 km north, turns right to walk 7 km, and then turns right to walk 2 km. How far is she in a straight line from her starting point?

A. 14.00 km
B. 7.62 km
C. 8.60 km
D. 7.00 km

**Answer:** B. 7.62 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 7.62 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 7 East and 3 North. Distance = √(7² + 3²) = 7.62 km.
- **A — 14.00 km:** 14.00 km is the total path length (5 + 7 + 2).
- **C — 8.60 km:** 8.60 km corresponds to √(5² + 7²), forgetting to subtract the 2 km South.
- **D — 7.00 km:** 7.00 km ignores the net 3 km North displacement.
- **Exam Trick:** North and South displacements subtract directly; here 5 km North − 2 km South = 3 km North.

### Q510 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Gaurav cycles 8 km south, turns right to cycle 4 km, and then turns right to cycle 3 km. What is his shortest distance from the start?

A. 15.00 km
B. 8.94 km
C. 6.40 km
D. 7.21 km

**Answer:** C. 6.40 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 6.40 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 4 West and 5 South. Distance = √(4² + 5²) = 6.40 km.
- **A — 15.00 km:** 15.00 km adds all three segments (8 + 4 + 3).
- **B — 8.94 km:** 8.94 km comes from √(8² + 4²), ignoring the 3 km return leg.
- **D — 7.21 km:** 7.21 km corresponds to √(6² + 4²).
- **Exam Trick:** A right turn from South leads West, and another right turn leads North, opposing the initial South leg.

### Q511 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A car travels 12 km east, turns left for 4 km, and then turns left again for 4 km. What is the straight-line distance from its starting point?

A. 20.00 km
B. 12.65 km
C. 10.77 km
D. 8.94 km

**Answer:** D. 8.94 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 8.94 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 8 East and 4 North. Distance = √(8² + 4²) = 8.94 km.
- **A — 20.00 km:** 20.00 km is the total distance driven (12 + 4 + 4).
- **B — 12.65 km:** 12.65 km comes from √(12² + 4²), forgetting to subtract the 4 km West.
- **C — 10.77 km:** 10.77 km corresponds to √(10² + 4²).
- **Exam Trick:** Two consecutive left turns reverse the direction of travel by 180°.

### Q512 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Suman walks 9 km south, turns right to walk 10 km, and then turns right to walk 16 km. What is the straight-line distance from her starting point?

A. 12.21 km
B. 35.00 km
C. 11.40 km
D. 13.45 km

**Answer:** A. 12.21 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 12.21 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 10 West and 7 North. Distance = √(10² + 7²) = 12.21 km.
- **B — 35.00 km:** 11.40 km comes from √(9² + 7²), which miscomputes the net North-South displacement.
- **C — 11.40 km:** 35.00 km is the total distance walked (9 + 10 + 16).
- **D — 13.45 km:** 13.45 km corresponds to √(9² + 10²).
- **Exam Trick:** Wait: let's check Net N/S and E/W carefully—never assume the larger leg isn't exceeded by the return leg!

### Q513 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A postman walks 14 km west, turns left to walk 9 km, and then turns left to walk 7 km. What is his straight-line distance from the starting point?

A. 30.00 km
B. 11.40 km
C. 16.64 km
D. 10.63 km

**Answer:** B. 11.40 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 11.40 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 7 West and 9 South. Distance = √(7² + 9²) = 11.40 km.
- **A — 30.00 km:** 30.00 km is the total path length (14 + 9 + 7).
- **C — 16.64 km:** 16.64 km comes from √(14² + 9²), ignoring the 7 km East leg.
- **D — 10.63 km:** 10.63 km corresponds to √(8² + 7²).
- **Exam Trick:** Left from West is South; left from South is East, which cancels 7 km of the 14 km West.

### Q514 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Ramesh walks 10 km north, turns right and walks 6 km, turns right and walks 10 km, and finally turns left and walks 5 km. How far and in which direction is he from the start?

A. 1 km, East
B. 11 km, West
C. 11 km, East
D. 21 km, North-East

**Answer:** C. 11 km, East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 11 km, East (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 11 East and 0 North. Result is 11 km, East.
- **A — 1 km, East:** 11 km, West reverses the Eastward direction.
- **B — 11 km, West:** 1 km, East subtracts 5 km from 6 km instead of adding both Eastward legs.
- **D — 21 km, North-East:** 21 km, North-East fails to cancel the 10 km North and 10 km South legs.
- **Exam Trick:** When North and South legs are equal, the net vertical displacement is zero, leaving only horizontal displacement.

### Q515 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A boy cycles 5 km north, turns right and cycles 12 km, turns right and cycles 5 km, and then turns right again to cycle 4 km. How far is he from the starting point?

A. 16 km
B. 12 km
C. 13 km
D. 8 km

**Answer:** D. 8 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 8 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 8 East and 0 North. Result is 8 km.
- **A — 16 km:** 16 km adds the 12 km East and 4 km West legs instead of subtracting them.
- **B — 12 km:** 13 km comes from √(5² + 12²), ignoring the return legs.
- **C — 13 km:** 12 km ignores the final 4 km Westward movement.
- **Exam Trick:** Four consecutive 90° turns form a rectangle/loop; track net E/W and N/S separately.

### Q516 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Priya starts from home, walks 4 km east, turns left and walks 6 km, turns left and walks 12 km, and finally turns left and walks 6 km. In which direction is she from her home?

A. West
B. North
C. East
D. South

**Answer:** A. West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — West (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 8 West and 0 North. Result is West.
- **B — North:** East would occur if the initial 4 km East exceeded the 12 km West leg.
- **C — East:** North ignores that the 6 km North and 6 km South legs cancel each other.
- **D — South:** South is incorrect because North and South displacements are equal and opposite.
- **Exam Trick:** Net East-West is 4 − 12 = −8 km (8 km West), and Net North-South is 6 − 6 = 0 km.

### Q517 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A tourist drives 15 km south, turns left to drive 8 km, turns left to drive 15 km, and then turns right to drive 7 km. What is the shortest distance from the starting point?

A. 1 km
B. 15 km
C. 17 km
D. 23 km

**Answer:** B. 15 km
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 15 km (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 15 East and 0 North. Result is 15 km.
- **A — 1 km:** 1 km subtracts 7 km from 8 km instead of adding both Eastward segments.
- **C — 17 km:** 17 km comes from √(15² + 8²), forgetting the 15 km North leg.
- **D — 23 km:** 23 km adds an unrelated 15 km vertical component.
- **Exam Trick:** Tracing each turn carefully: South → left (East) → left (North) → right (East).

### Q518 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Kunal walks 8 km north, turns right and walks 6 km, turns left and walks 4 km, and then turns left and walks 6 km. How far and in which direction is he from the start?

A. 12 km, South
B. 4 km, North
C. 12 km, North
D. 10 km, North-East

**Answer:** C. 12 km, North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 12 km, North (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 0 East and 12 North. Result is 12 km, North.
- **A — 12 km, South:** 4 km, North subtracts 4 km from 8 km instead of adding them.
- **B — 4 km, North:** 12 km, South reverses the Northward net displacement.
- **D — 10 km, North-East:** 10 km, North-East fails to cancel the 6 km East and 6 km West legs.
- **Exam Trick:** Equal East and West movements (6 km each) cancel out completely.

### Q519 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A delivery rider goes 10 km west, turns right and goes 15 km, turns right and goes 18 km, and then turns right and goes 9 km. What is the shortest distance and direction from the start?

A. 10 km, South-East
B. 10 km, North-West
C. 14 km, North-East
D. 10 km, North-East

**Answer:** D. 10 km, North-East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 10 km, North-East (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 8 East and 6 North. Distance = √(8² + 6²) = 10 km, North-East.
- **A — 10 km, South-East:** 10 km, South-East reverses the North-South sign (15 km North − 9 km South = +6 km North).
- **B — 10 km, North-West:** 14 km, North-East adds 8 + 6 instead of applying √(8² + 6²).
- **C — 14 km, North-East:** 10 km, North-West reverses the East-West sign (18 km East − 10 km West = +8 km East).
- **Exam Trick:** Net displacement is 8 km East and 6 km North, forming a 6-8-10 right triangle in the North-East quadrant.

### Q520 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Neha walks 6 km south, turns left and walks 5 km, turns right and walks 6 km, and then turns right and walks 10 km. What is the shortest distance and direction from her starting point?

A. 13 km, South-West
B. 17 km, South-West
C. 13 km, South-East
D. 12 km, West

**Answer:** A. 13 km, South-West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 13 km, South-West (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 5 West and 12 South. Distance = √(5² + 12²) = 13 km, South-West.
- **B — 17 km, South-West:** 13 km, South-East reverses the East-West direction (5 km East − 10 km West = 5 km West).
- **C — 13 km, South-East:** 17 km, South-West adds 12 + 5 instead of computing √(12² + 5²).
- **D — 12 km, West:** 12 km, West ignores the 5 km Westward net displacement.
- **Exam Trick:** Net displacement is 5 km West and 12 km South, giving a 5-12-13 triangle in the South-West.

### Q521 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

A man walks 20 m north, turns right and walks 30 m, turns right and walks 35 m, turns left and walks 15 m, and finally turns left and walks 15 m. How far and in which direction is he from the start?

A. 30 m, East
B. 45 m, East
C. 45 m, West
D. 50 m, North-East

**Answer:** B. 45 m, East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 45 m, East (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 45 East and 0 North. Result is 45 m, East.
- **A — 30 m, East:** 30 m, East omits the extra 15 m Eastward segment.
- **C — 45 m, West:** 45 m, West reverses the direction of the net horizontal movement.
- **D — 50 m, North-East:** 50 m, North-East fails to cancel 20 m + 15 m North against 35 m South.
- **Exam Trick:** Vertical segments: 20 m North − 35 m South + 15 m North = 0 m; Horizontal: 30 m East + 15 m East = 45 m East.

### Q522 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Aanya walks 12 km east, turns left and walks 9 km, turns left and walks 12 km, and then turns left and walks 4 km. How far and in which direction is she from the starting point?

A. 13 km, North
B. 5 km, South
C. 5 km, North
D. 9 km, North

**Answer:** C. 5 km, North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 5 km, North (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 0 East and 5 North. Result is 5 km, North.
- **A — 13 km, North:** 5 km, South reverses the net Northward displacement (9 km North − 4 km South).
- **B — 5 km, South:** 13 km, North adds 9 + 4 instead of subtracting opposite directions.
- **D — 9 km, North:** 9 km, North ignores the final 4 km Southward walk.
- **Exam Trick:** Horizontal legs (12 km East and 12 km West) cancel, leaving 9 − 4 = 5 km North.

### Q523 · Multi-Turn Distance & Direction

Rohit starts walking towards North. After walking 7 km, he turns left and walks 24 km. What is his shortest distance and direction from the starting point?

A. 25 km, North-East
B. 24 km, West
C. 31 km, North-West
D. 25 km, North-West

**Answer:** D. 25 km, North-West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 25 km, North-West (Correct):** Tracking net displacements gives 24 West and 7 North. Distance = √(24² + 7²) = 25 km, North-West.
- **A — 25 km, North-East:** 25 km, North-East results from turning right (East) instead of left (West) from North.
- **B — 24 km, West:** 31 km, North-West adds the two legs (7 + 24) instead of applying Pythagoras' theorem.
- **C — 31 km, North-West:** 24 km, West ignores the initial 7 km Northward walk.
- **Exam Trick:** Turning left from North points West, so net displacement is 7 km North and 24 km West (7-24-25 triple).

### Q524 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A person faces North, turns 180° and then turns 90° clockwise. Which direction are they facing now?

A. West
B. South
C. North
D. East

**Answer:** A. West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — West (Correct):** Starting from North, the net rotation is 270° (clockwise), which lands on West.
- **B — South:** East results from turning 90° clockwise from North instead of 270° clockwise.
- **C — North:** South is the direction after only the 180° turn, before the 90° clockwise turn.
- **D — East:** North is the starting direction, not the final direction.
- **Exam Trick:** Turning 180° + 90° clockwise = 270° clockwise, which is equivalent to 90° anticlockwise.

### Q525 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A person faces East, turns 180° and then turns 90° clockwise. Which direction are they facing now?

A. South
B. North
C. East
D. West

**Answer:** B. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — North (Correct):** Starting from East, the net rotation is 270° (clockwise), which lands on North.
- **A — South:** South is 90° clockwise from East, ignoring the 180° reversal.
- **C — East:** West is the direction after turning 180°, before the 90° clockwise turn.
- **D — West:** East is the initial direction.
- **Exam Trick:** A 180° turn always reverses the direction; 90° clockwise from West points North.

### Q526 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A person faces South, turns 180° and then turns 90° clockwise. Which direction are they facing now?

A. West
B. North
C. East
D. South

**Answer:** C. East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — East (Correct):** Starting from South, the net rotation is 270° (clockwise), which lands on East.
- **A — West:** West is 90° clockwise from South, omitting the 180° turn.
- **B — North:** North is the intermediate direction after turning 180°.
- **D — South:** South is the starting direction.
- **Exam Trick:** From South, 180° leads to North, and 90° clockwise from North leads to East.

### Q527 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A person faces West, turns 180° and then turns 90° clockwise. Which direction are they facing now?

A. North
B. East
C. West
D. South

**Answer:** D. South
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — South (Correct):** Starting from West, the net rotation is 270° (clockwise), which lands on South.
- **A — North:** North is 90° clockwise from West, ignoring the 180° turn.
- **B — East:** East is the direction after the 180° turn alone.
- **C — West:** West is the initial facing direction.
- **Exam Trick:** 270° clockwise from West is identical to 90° anticlockwise from West, which is South.

### Q528 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

Aman is facing North-East. He turns 90° clockwise and then 135° anticlockwise. Which direction is he facing now?

A. North
B. North-West
C. East
D. South-East

**Answer:** A. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — North (Correct):** Starting from North-East, the net rotation is -45° (anticlockwise), which lands on North.
- **B — North-West:** North-West results from turning 90° anticlockwise instead of 45° anticlockwise net.
- **C — East:** East is 45° clockwise from North-East, reversing the net turn sign.
- **D — South-East:** South-East is the intermediate direction after the 90° clockwise turn.
- **Exam Trick:** Net turn = 90° CW − 135° ACW = 45° ACW. From North-East, 45° ACW is North.

### Q529 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

Shreya is facing South-West. She turns 270° anticlockwise and then 45° clockwise. Which direction is she facing now?

A. South
B. North
C. East
D. South-East

**Answer:** B. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — North (Correct):** Starting from South-West, the net rotation is -225° (anticlockwise), which lands on North.
- **A — South:** South is 45° anticlockwise from South-West, not 225° anticlockwise.
- **C — East:** East is 135° anticlockwise from South-West, forgetting the 45° clockwise turn.
- **D — South-East:** South-East is the intermediate direction after 270° anticlockwise from South-West.
- **Exam Trick:** Net turn = 270° ACW − 45° CW = 225° ACW (or 135° CW). From South-West, 135° CW is North.

### Q530 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A soldier faces North, turns 45° clockwise, then turns 180°, and finally turns 45° anticlockwise. Which direction is he facing?

A. South-West
B. North-East
C. South
D. South-East

**Answer:** C. South
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — South (Correct):** Starting from North, the net rotation is 180° (clockwise), which lands on South.
- **A — South-West:** North-East is only the first 45° clockwise turn.
- **B — North-East:** South-West is 180° + 45° CW, failing to subtract the 45° anticlockwise turn.
- **D — South-East:** South-East results from adding 45° CW instead of cancelling it with 45° ACW.
- **Exam Trick:** Equal clockwise and anticlockwise turns (45° CW and 45° ACW) cancel out, leaving just 180°.

### Q531 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A guard faces East. He turns 135° clockwise and then 90° anticlockwise. Which direction is he facing now?

A. North-East
B. South-West
C. South
D. South-East

**Answer:** D. South-East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — South-East (Correct):** Starting from East, the net rotation is 45° (clockwise), which lands on South-East.
- **A — North-East:** North-East is 45° anticlockwise from East, reversing the net turn direction.
- **B — South-West:** South is 90° clockwise from East, ignoring the 45° difference.
- **C — South:** South-West is the intermediate direction after turning 135° clockwise from East.
- **Exam Trick:** Net turn = 135° CW − 90° ACW = 45° CW. 45° clockwise from East is South-East.

### Q532 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A hiker faces South-East and turns 225° clockwise. Which direction is the hiker facing now?

A. North
B. West
C. North-West
D. North-East

**Answer:** A. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — North (Correct):** Starting from South-East, the net rotation is 225° (clockwise), which lands on North.
- **B — West:** West is 135° clockwise from South-East, falling 90° short.
- **C — North-West:** North-West is 180° from South-East, falling 45° short.
- **D — North-East:** North-East is 270° clockwise (or 90° ACW) from South-East.
- **Exam Trick:** Break 225° CW into 180° + 45° CW: South-East → 180° → North-West → +45° CW → North.

### Q533 · Degree Turns (CW / ACW)

A cyclist faces North-West, turns 135° anticlockwise, and then turns 180°. Which direction is the cyclist facing?

A. South
B. North
C. East
D. North-East

**Answer:** B. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — North (Correct):** Starting from North-West, the net rotation is 45° (clockwise), which lands on North.
- **A — South:** South is the intermediate direction after turning 135° anticlockwise from North-West.
- **C — East:** East is 135° clockwise from North-West.
- **D — North-East:** North-East is 90° clockwise from North-West.
- **Exam Trick:** Net turn = 180° − 135° = 45° clockwise. From North-West, 45° clockwise is North.

### Q534 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One morning after sunrise, Rahul stood facing a pole. The shadow of the pole fell exactly to his right. Which direction was Rahul facing?

A. North
B. East
C. South
D. West

**Answer:** C. South
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — South (Correct):** In the morning, the sun is in the East, so all shadows fall towards the West. Since West is to Rahul's right, he must be facing South.
- **A — North:** North has West to the left, so the shadow would fall to his left.
- **B — East:** East has West directly behind him, so the shadow would fall behind him.
- **D — West:** West has West directly in front of him, so the shadow would fall in front.
- **Exam Trick:** Morning rule: Sun in East → Shadow in West. Facing South puts West on your right.

### Q535 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One evening before sunset, Sneha and Amit were talking to each other face-to-face. If Amit's shadow was exactly to Sneha's right, which direction was Sneha facing?

A. West
B. South
C. East
D. North

**Answer:** D. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — North (Correct):** In the evening, the sun is in the West, so shadows fall towards the East. Since East is to Sneha's right, Sneha must be facing North.
- **A — West:** South has East to the left, not to the right.
- **B — South:** East has East directly in front, not to the right.
- **C — East:** West has East directly behind, not to the right.
- **Exam Trick:** Evening rule: Sun in West → Shadow in East. Facing North puts East on your right.

### Q536 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One morning after sunrise, two friends P and Q stood back-to-back. P's shadow fell to P's left. Which direction was Q facing?

A. South
B. North
C. East
D. West

**Answer:** A. South
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — South (Correct):** In the morning, shadows fall West. Since West is to P's left, P faces North. Standing back-to-back, Q faces the opposite direction: South.
- **B — North:** North is the direction P is facing, not Q.
- **C — East:** East would place the shadow behind or in front, not to the left.
- **D — West:** West would place the shadow in front of P.
- **Exam Trick:** Read carefully whether two people are face-to-face or back-to-back, and whose left/right is referenced.

### Q537 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One evening before sunset, a man walks such that his shadow falls directly in front of him. Which direction is he walking?

A. North
B. East
C. South
D. West

**Answer:** B. East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — East (Correct):** In the evening, the sun is in the West and shadows fall towards the East. Since his shadow falls in front of him, he is walking East.
- **A — North:** North would place the shadow to his right.
- **C — South:** South would place the shadow to his left.
- **D — West:** West would mean walking towards the sun, casting his shadow behind him.
- **Exam Trick:** If a shadow falls in front of you, you are walking away from the sun.

### Q538 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One morning after sunrise, Vikram stood facing a flagpost. The shadow of the flagpost fell exactly to his left. Which direction was Vikram facing?

A. East
B. South
C. North
D. West

**Answer:** C. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — North (Correct):** In the morning, shadows fall towards the West. Since West is to Vikram's left, he is facing North.
- **A — East:** South puts West to the right.
- **B — South:** East puts West directly behind.
- **D — West:** West puts West directly in front.
- **Exam Trick:** Facing North always puts East on your right and West on your left.

### Q539 · Morning & Evening Shadows

One evening before sunset, Deepak starts walking and notices his shadow falling to his right. If he turns 90° clockwise, which direction will he walk?

A. North
B. South
C. West
D. East

**Answer:** D. East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — East (Correct):** In the evening, shadows fall East. Since East is to Deepak's right, he is initially walking North. Turning 90° clockwise from North leads to East.
- **A — North:** North is his initial walking direction before the 90° clockwise turn.
- **B — South:** South is 180° opposite to his initial direction.
- **C — West:** West would result from turning 90° anticlockwise instead of clockwise.
- **Exam Trick:** Solve shadow problems in two steps: first find the initial direction, then apply any turns.

### Q540 · Morning & Evening Shadows

At 8:00 AM, a tree's shadow falls to the left of Ravi who is walking straight. If he turns 90° anticlockwise, which direction will he walk?

A. West
B. South
C. East
D. North

**Answer:** A. West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — West (Correct):** At 8:00 AM (morning), shadows fall West. West is to Ravi's left, so he is walking North. Turning 90° anticlockwise from North points West.
- **B — South:** North is his initial direction before turning left.
- **C — East:** South is opposite to North.
- **D — North:** East would result from turning 90° clockwise instead of anticlockwise.
- **Exam Trick:** Time cues like 8:00 AM mean morning (sun in East), while 5:00 PM means evening (sun in West).

### Q541 · Morning & Evening Shadows

At 5:00 PM, Meena and Tina are sitting face-to-face. If Meena's shadow falls to her own left, which direction is Tina facing?

A. South
B. North
C. East
D. West

**Answer:** B. North
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — North (Correct):** At 5:00 PM (evening), shadows fall East. Since East is to Meena's left, Meena faces South. Facing her, Tina must face North.
- **A — South:** South is the direction Meena is facing, not Tina.
- **C — East:** East puts the shadow in front, not to the left.
- **D — West:** West puts the shadow behind, not to the left.
- **Exam Trick:** When two people sit face-to-face, they always face exact opposite directions.

### Q542 · Rotated Compass & Relative Position

If South-East becomes North, North-East becomes West, and so on, what will West become?

A. North-East
B. South-West
C. South-East
D. North-West

**Answer:** C. South-East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — South-East (Correct):** South-East (135° CW from North) shifts 135° anticlockwise to become North; similarly North-East shifts 135° anticlockwise to West. Shifting West by 135° anticlockwise gives South-East.
- **A — North-East:** North-East is 135° clockwise from West, using the wrong rotation direction.
- **B — South-West:** South-West is only 45° anticlockwise from West.
- **D — North-West:** North-West is 45° clockwise from West.
- **Exam Trick:** Find the exact angular shift (here 135° ACW) from the given pair and apply the same shift to the target.

### Q543 · Rotated Compass & Relative Position

If North becomes North-East, and East becomes South-East, what will South-West become?

A. South-East
B. South
C. North-West
D. West

**Answer:** D. West
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — West (Correct):** Each direction rotates 45° clockwise (North → North-East, East → South-East). Rotating South-West by 45° clockwise gives West.
- **A — South-East:** South is 45° anticlockwise from South-West, reversing the rotation.
- **B — South:** North-West is 90° clockwise from South-West.
- **C — North-West:** South-East is 90° anticlockwise from South-West.
- **Exam Trick:** Check that both given examples share the exact same rotation angle before applying it.

### Q544 · Rotated Compass & Relative Position

If East becomes South-West, what will North-West become under the same rotation?

A. East
B. North
C. South-East
D. North-East

**Answer:** A. East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — East (Correct):** East to South-West is a 135° clockwise rotation. Applying 135° clockwise to North-West lands on East.
- **B — North:** North is only 45° clockwise from North-West.
- **C — South-East:** South-East is 180° from North-West.
- **D — North-East:** North-East is 90° clockwise from North-West.
- **Exam Trick:** On an 8-point compass, every step is 45°, so 135° is simply 3 steps.

### Q545 · Rotated Compass & Relative Position

A clock shows 4:30. If the minute hand points East, in which direction does the hour hand point?

A. South-East
B. North-East
C. North-West
D. South

**Answer:** B. North-East
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — North-East (Correct):** At 4:30, the minute hand is at 6 (180°) and the hour hand is halfway between 4 and 5 (135°), which is 45° anticlockwise from the minute hand. Since the minute hand points East, 45° anticlockwise from East is North-East.
- **A — South-East:** South-East is 45° clockwise from East, reversing the relative position of the hour hand.
- **C — North-West:** North-West is 135° anticlockwise from East.
- **D — South:** South is 90° clockwise from East.
- **Exam Trick:** In clock-direction questions, first find the angle between the two hands at the given time.

---

## Chapter 8: Syllogism & Logical Venn Diagrams

### Q546 · Syllogism - Basic

Statements: All metals conduct electricity. Copper is a metal. Which conclusion follows logically?

A. Copper is an insulator.
B. Copper conducts only heat.
C. Copper does not conduct electricity.
D. Copper conducts electricity.

**Answer:** D. Copper conducts electricity.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Copper conducts electricity. (Correct):** Since copper is a metal and all metals conduct electricity, copper necessarily lies inside the set of things that conduct electricity.
- **A — Copper is an insulator.:** Insulators are not mentioned in the statements, so no conclusion about copper being an insulator can be drawn.
- **B — Copper conducts only heat.:** Being a metal does not restrict copper to only conducting heat; the statement specifically links metals to electricity.
- **C — Copper does not conduct electricity.:** The statements imply copper conducts electricity, directly contradicting the claim that it does not.
- **Exam Trick:** In syllogisms, a singular subject like 'Copper' acts as a universal affirmative ('All copper is metal'), so it inherits every property of the class 'metal'.

### Q547 · Syllogism - Basic

Statements: All clerks are employees. No employee is a visitor. Which conclusion follows logically?

A. Some clerks are visitors.
B. All visitors are clerks.
C. No clerk is a visitor.
D. All employees are clerks.

**Answer:** C. No clerk is a visitor.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — No clerk is a visitor. (Correct):** Because all clerks are inside the set of employees, and employees have zero overlap with visitors, no clerk can be a visitor.
- **A — Some clerks are visitors.:** There is no overlap between employees and visitors, so no clerk can possibly be a visitor.
- **B — All visitors are clerks.:** The statement 'No employee is a visitor' rules out any clerk being a visitor, making 'Some clerks are visitors' false.
- **D — All employees are clerks.:** The universal affirmative 'All clerks are employees' does not mean 'All employees are clerks'; other employees may exist.
- **Exam Trick:** The pattern 'All A are B' and 'No B is C' always yields the definite conclusion 'No A is C' (and its converse 'No C is A').

### Q548 · Syllogism - Basic

Statements: All roses are flowers. All flowers need water. Which conclusion follows logically?

A. Some roses do not need water.
B. All roses need water.
C. All things that need water are roses.
D. All things that need water are flowers.

**Answer:** B. All roses need water.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — All roses need water. (Correct):** The set of roses is completely inside flowers, and flowers are completely inside things that need water, so all roses need water.
- **A — Some roses do not need water.:** All roses are flowers and all flowers need water, so it is false that some roses do not need water.
- **C — All things that need water are roses.:** While all roses need water, other things that need water may not be roses, so 'All things that need water are roses' is invalid.
- **D — All things that need water are flowers.:** Flowers are a subset of things that need water, not the other way around, so not all water-needing things are flowers.
- **Exam Trick:** In a chain of two universal affirmatives ('All A are B' and 'All B are C'), 'All A are C' and 'Some C are A' both follow.

### Q549 · Syllogism - Basic

Statements: Some books are novels. All novels are fiction. Which conclusion follows logically?

A. Some books are fiction.
B. All books are fiction.
C. No book is fiction.
D. All fiction are novels.

**Answer:** A. Some books are fiction.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Some books are fiction. (Correct):** Since some books are novels and every novel is fiction, the books that are novels must also be fiction.
- **B — All books are fiction.:** Only some books are stated to be novels, so we cannot conclude that all books are fiction.
- **C — No book is fiction.:** Since some books are definitely fiction, 'No book is fiction' is false.
- **D — All fiction are novels.:** All novels are fiction, which does not mean all fiction works are novels.
- **Exam Trick:** The combination 'Some A are B' followed by 'All B are C' always gives the definite conclusion 'Some A are C'.

### Q550 · Syllogism - Basic

Statements: No poets are careless. Some teachers are poets. Which conclusion follows logically?

A. No teacher is careless.
B. All teachers are careless.
C. Some teachers are not careless.
D. All poets are teachers.

**Answer:** C. Some teachers are not careless.
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Some teachers are not careless. (Correct):** The teachers who are poets cannot be careless because no poet is careless; therefore, some teachers are not careless.
- **A — No teacher is careless.:** The statements only tell us about the teachers who are poets; the remaining teachers could be careless or not.
- **B — All teachers are careless.:** Teachers who are poets are definitely not careless, so 'All teachers are careless' is impossible.
- **D — All poets are teachers.:** We only know that some teachers are poets; there is no information that all poets are teachers.
- **Exam Trick:** When 'No A is B' and 'Some C are A' are given, the definite conclusion is 'Some C are not B'.

### Q551 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All laptops are gadgets. All gadgets are devices. Conclusions: I. All laptops are devices. II. All devices are laptops.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** From 'All laptops are gadgets' and 'All gadgets are devices', all laptops are devices (I follows). Conclusion II ('All devices are laptops') does not follow because devices can be a larger set than laptops.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I definitely follows from the chain rule, so 'Only conclusion II follows' is incorrect.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Conclusion II is an invalid converse of 'All laptops are devices', so both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is valid, ruling out 'Neither conclusion I nor II follows'.
- **Exam Trick:** 'All A are B' implies 'Some B are A', but never 'All B are A'.

### Q552 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All pens are stationery. No stationery is paper. Conclusions: I. Some pens are paper. II. Some stationery are pens.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'All pens are stationery' and 'No stationery is paper' give 'No pen is paper', so I ('Some pens are paper') is false. However, since all pens are stationery, the converse 'Some stationery are pens' is definitely true (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since no stationery is paper and all pens are stationery, no pen can be paper, making I false.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is false and II is true, both cannot follow together.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is a valid converse of the first statement, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** A universal affirmative 'All A are B' always yields the valid particular converse 'Some B are A'.

### Q553 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some chairs are tables. All tables are wood. Conclusions: I. Some chairs are wood. II. Some wood are chairs.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** Since 'Some chairs are tables' and 'All tables are wood', the chairs that are tables are wood, so 'Some chairs are wood' (I follows). Its converse 'Some wood are chairs' also follows (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II is the valid converse of conclusion I, so I alone cannot be the answer.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I follows directly from the statements, so II alone is incomplete.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are logically valid, so 'Neither' is false.
- **Exam Trick:** In 'Some A are B', the relation is symmetric, so 'Some B are A' is always true.

### Q554 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some fruits are apples. Some apples are sweet. Conclusions: I. Some fruits are sweet. II. Some fruits are not sweet.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some fruits are apples' and 'Some apples are sweet' share only a particular middle term ('apples'), which cannot establish any definite link between fruits and sweet things. Thus neither I nor II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** The apples that are sweet might not be the same apples that are fruits, so I is not definite.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** There is no negative statement to guarantee that any fruit is not sweet, so II is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Neither I nor II is definitely true, so both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** Two particular affirmative statements ('Some A are B' + 'Some B are C') never yield a definite conclusion between A and C.

### Q555 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: No river is a lake. All oceans are rivers. Conclusions: I. No ocean is a lake. II. Some lakes are rivers.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** From 'No river is a lake' and 'All oceans are rivers', oceans lie completely inside rivers, which are disjoint from lakes. Thus 'No ocean is a lake' (I follows). Conclusion II ('Some lakes are rivers') directly contradicts 'No river is a lake'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion II directly contradicts the first statement, so it cannot follow.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II is false, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** 'No A is B' is symmetric: it implies 'No B is A' and rules out 'Some B are A'.

### Q556 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some painters are sculptors. No sculptor is a dancer. Conclusions: I. All painters are dancers. II. Some painters are not dancers.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some painters are sculptors' and 'No sculptor is a dancer' mean the painters who are sculptors are not dancers, so 'Some painters are not dancers' (II follows). Conclusion I ('All painters are dancers') is false because at least those painters who are sculptors cannot be dancers.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Painters who are sculptors cannot be dancers, making 'All painters are dancers' impossible.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Conclusion I is false, so both conclusions cannot follow together.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely valid, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** The combination 'Some A are B' and 'No B is C' always yields 'Some A are not C', never 'Some C are not A'.

### Q557 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: No bus is a train. No train is a ship. Conclusions: I. Some buses are ships. II. No bus is a ship.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** Two universal negative statements ('No bus is a train' and 'No train is a ship') cannot establish any definite relation between buses and ships; they could be disjoint or overlapping. Hence neither I nor II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Buses and ships may overlap completely or partially while still being disjoint from trains, so I is not definite.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Buses and ships could be completely disjoint from each other, so II is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since neither I nor II is guaranteed, both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** Two negative premises ('No + No', 'No + Some Not', or 'Some Not + Some Not') never yield a valid definite conclusion between the extreme terms.

### Q558 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All gold is silver. No silver is bronze. Conclusions: I. Some gold is silver. II. Some gold is not bronze.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** In SSC syllogism, 'All gold is silver' implies 'Some gold is silver' (I follows). Combined with 'No silver is bronze', 'No gold is bronze' follows, which in turn implies 'Some gold is not bronze' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II also follows because 'No gold is bronze' guarantees 'Some gold is not bronze'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I is a valid particular deduction from 'All gold is silver', so II alone is incomplete.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are logically valid, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** A universal statement ('All' or 'No') always implies its corresponding particular statement ('Some' or 'Some not') in standard SSC logic.

### Q559 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: No tiger is a deer. All deer are herbivores. Conclusions: I. No herbivore is a tiger. II. Some herbivores are not tigers.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'No tiger is a deer' and 'All deer are herbivores' mean the part of herbivores that are deer are definitely not tigers. Therefore, 'Some herbivores are not tigers' (II follows). Conclusion I ('No herbivore is a tiger') is too strong because other herbivores could be tigers.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Herbivores outside the set of deer could still be tigers, so 'No herbivore is a tiger' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is not definite, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true from the deer that are herbivores, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** 'No A is B' followed by 'All B are C' gives 'Some C are not A', not 'No C is A'.

### Q560 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All apples are mangoes. Some mangoes are bananas. Conclusions: I. Some apples are bananas. II. No apple is a banana.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'All apples are mangoes' and 'Some mangoes are bananas' leaves the relation between apples and bananas completely open (apples may or may not touch bananas). Thus neither I nor II definitely follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** The mangoes that are bananas could lie entirely outside the circle of apples, making I uncertain.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** The mangoes that are bananas could overlap with apples, making 'No apple is a banana' uncertain.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since neither conclusion is definite, both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** When the middle term is the predicate of 'All' and the subject of 'Some' ('All A are B, Some B are C'), no definite conclusion links A and C.

### Q561 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All stars are planets. All stars are moons. Conclusions: I. Some planets are moons. II. All moons are planets.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** From 'All stars are planets' and 'All stars are moons', the entire set of stars lies in the intersection of planets and moons. Therefore, 'Some planets are moons' (I follows). Conclusion II ('All moons are planets') does not follow as moons may extend beyond planets.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Moons outside the set of stars need not be planets, so conclusion II is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II does not follow, both conclusions cannot be true together.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is guaranteed by the common subset of stars, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** If 'All A are B' and 'All A are C', then 'Some B are C' and 'Some C are B' always follow.

### Q562 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All circles are squares. All triangles are squares. Conclusions: I. Some squares are circles. II. Some squares are triangles.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** 'All circles are squares' implies 'Some squares are circles' (I follows), and 'All triangles are squares' directly implies 'Some squares are triangles' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II is a direct valid converse of 'All triangles are squares', so I alone is incomplete.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I is a direct valid converse of 'All circles are squares', so II alone is incomplete.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are valid converses of the given statements, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** Each 'All X are Y' premise independently guarantees its particular converse 'Some Y are X'.

### Q563 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some windows are doors. No door is a wall. Conclusions: I. Some walls are not windows. II. Some windows are not walls.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some windows are doors' and 'No door is a wall' mean the windows that are doors cannot be walls, so 'Some windows are not walls' (II follows). Conclusion I ('Some walls are not windows') is the reverse and is not guaranteed because all walls could lie inside windows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** The entire set of walls could lie inside the set of windows (outside doors), so I is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is uncertain, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II definitely follows from the windows that are doors, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** 'Some A are not B' does not imply 'Some B are not A'; the 'Some Not' relation is one-directional.

### Q564 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some clouds are not rain. All rain is water. Conclusions: I. Some clouds are not water. II. Some clouds are water.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some clouds are not rain' and 'All rain is water' give no definite link between clouds and water—all clouds could be water, or some clouds might not be water. Neither I nor II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Clouds that are not rain could still be part of water, so 'Some clouds are not water' is not definite.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** There is no positive overlap stated between clouds and water, so 'Some clouds are water' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since neither conclusion is guaranteed, both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** A 'Some Not' statement followed by an 'All' statement ('Some A are not B, All B are C') gives no definite conclusion about A and C.

### Q565 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: No bottle is a cup. Some cups are plates. Conclusions: I. Some plates are not bottles. II. All plates are bottles.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'No bottle is a cup' means no cup is a bottle. Since 'Some cups are plates', the plates that are cups cannot be bottles, so 'Some plates are not bottles' (I follows). Conclusion II ('All plates are bottles') is false because the plates that are cups are not bottles.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** The plates that are cups cannot be bottles, making 'All plates are bottles' impossible.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion II is false, both conclusions cannot follow together.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** 'No A is B' + 'Some B are C' always gives 'Some C are not A', which rules out 'All C are A'.

### Q566 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All keys are locks. All locks are doors. Conclusions: I. Some doors are keys. II. Some doors are locks.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** Since 'All keys are locks' and 'All locks are doors', all keys are doors, so 'Some doors are keys' (I follows). Also, 'All locks are doors' directly yields 'Some doors are locks' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II also follows directly from 'All locks are doors'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I also follows because all keys are inside doors.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are logically valid, so 'Neither' is false.
- **Exam Trick:** When A is inside B and B is inside C, 'Some C are A' and 'Some C are B' both hold true.

### Q567 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some pens are pencils. No pencil is an eraser. Conclusions: I. All pencils are pens. II. Some pencils are pens.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some pens are pencils' means 'Some pencils are pens' (II follows). Conclusion I ('All pencils are pens') is not guaranteed from a 'Some' statement.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** 'Some pens are pencils' does not mean all pencils must be pens, so I is uncertain.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since I is not definite, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is the direct valid converse of the given statement, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** In standard syllogisms, 'Some A are B' does not rule out 'All A are B', nor does it prove it.

### Q568 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All singers are actors. No singer is a writer. Conclusions: I. All actors are writers. II. No actor is a writer.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'All singers are actors' and 'No singer is a writer' mean singers are inside actors and disjoint from writers. Conclusion I ('All actors are writers') is false because singers are actors who are not writers, and Conclusion II ('No actor is a writer') is uncertain because non-singer actors could be writers. Thus neither follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since singers are actors and no singer is a writer, at least some actors are not writers, making 'All actors are writers' false.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Actors who are not singers may still be writers, so 'No actor is a writer' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Neither I nor II is valid, so both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** When 'All A are B' and 'No A is C', we can conclude 'Some B are not C', but not 'No B is C'.

### Q569 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: All coats are jackets. No coat is a shirt. Conclusions: I. Some jackets are not shirts. II. Some shirts are not jackets.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** From 'All coats are jackets' and 'No coat is a shirt', the coats inside jackets cannot be shirts, so 'Some jackets are not shirts' (I follows). Conclusion II ('Some shirts are not jackets') is uncertain because all shirts could be inside non-coat jackets.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** All shirts could be jackets that are not coats, so 'Some shirts are not jackets' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II is not guaranteed, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true from the coats inside jackets, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** Always draw the minimal overlap Venn diagram: if all C can fit inside B without touching A, then 'Some C are not B' is false.

### Q570 · Syllogism - Two Statements

Statements: Some roads are lanes. All lanes are streets. Conclusions: I. No road is a street. II. Some streets are roads.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some roads are lanes' and 'All lanes are streets' give 'Some roads are streets', which rules out 'No road is a street' (I is false). The converse 'Some streets are roads' definitely follows (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since roads that are lanes are also streets, 'No road is a street' is false.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is false, both conclusions cannot follow together.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** If 'Some A are C' is definitely true, then 'No A is C' is definitely false.

### Q571 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All rings are bangles. All bangles are gold. No gold is silver. Conclusions: I. No ring is silver. II. Some silver are bangles.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** From 'All rings are bangles' and 'All bangles are gold', 'All rings are gold' follows. Combined with 'No gold is silver', 'No ring is silver' follows (I follows). Conclusion II ('Some silver are bangles') is false because 'No gold is silver' and 'All bangles are gold' imply 'No bangle is silver'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Since all bangles are gold and no gold is silver, no bangle can be silver, making 'Some silver are bangles' false.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion II is false, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** In a 3-statement chain 'All A are B, All B are C, No C is D', neither A nor B can have any overlap with D.

### Q572 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: Some phones are tablets. All tablets are screens. All screens are glass. Conclusions: I. Some phones are screens. II. All tablets are glass.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** 'Some phones are tablets' + 'All tablets are screens' -> 'Some phones are screens' (I follows). 'All tablets are screens' + 'All screens are glass' -> 'All tablets are glass' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II also follows directly from the second and third statements.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I also follows from the first two statements.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are logically valid, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** In 3-statement syllogisms, check each conclusion using only the relevant subset of premises first.

### Q573 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: No cat is a dog. All dogs are pets. Some pets are brown. Conclusions: I. Some brown are cats. II. Some brown are dogs.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'No cat is a dog' + 'All dogs are pets' -> 'Some pets are not cats'. 'Some pets are brown' does not guarantee that brown pets are cats (I uncertain) or dogs (II uncertain). Neither follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** The pets that are brown may be completely outside cats, so I is not definite.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** The pets that are brown may not be dogs at all, so II is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since neither conclusion is guaranteed, both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** A 'Some' link at the end of a chain ('All B are C, Some C are D') does not pass properties of B on to D.

### Q574 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: Some bags are boxes. No box is a crate. All crates are heavy. Conclusions: I. Some bags are heavy. II. Some heavy are not boxes.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some bags are boxes' and 'No box is a crate' -> 'Some bags are not crates'. With 'All crates are heavy', 'Some bags are heavy' is uncertain (I does not follow). However, 'No box is a crate' implies 'No crate is a box', and 'All crates are heavy' means the crates inside heavy are not boxes -> 'Some heavy are not boxes' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** The bags that are not boxes might or might not be heavy, so 'Some bags are heavy' is uncertain.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is uncertain, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true from the crates inside heavy, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** When 'No B is C' and 'All C are D', 'Some D are not B' always follows.

### Q575 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All ants are insects. All insects are small. Some small are red. Conclusions: I. Some small are ants. II. Some red are insects.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'All ants are insects' + 'All insects are small' -> 'All ants are small', so 'Some small are ants' (I follows). 'Some small are red' does not guarantee any red is an insect (II does not follow).
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** The small things that are red could be completely outside insects, so II is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II is uncertain, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Check whether the middle term ('small' here) is distributed; in 'All insects are small' and 'Some small are red', 'small' is undistributed in both, so no link forms between insects and red.

### Q576 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All buses are vans. No van is a jeep. Some jeeps are cars. Conclusions: I. No bus is a jeep. II. Some cars are not vans.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
