- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** 'All buses are vans' + 'No van is a jeep' -> 'No bus is a jeep' (I follows). 'Some jeeps are cars' + 'No van is a jeep' -> 'Some cars are not vans' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II also follows because the jeeps that are cars cannot be vans.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I also follows because buses are inside vans, which are disjoint from jeeps.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are valid, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** Whenever 'No A is B' and 'Some B are C', 'Some C are not A' is a guaranteed deduction.

### Q577 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: Some cups are mugs. Some mugs are bowls. All bowls are steel. Conclusions: I. Some cups are steel. II. All mugs are steel.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some cups are mugs' + 'Some mugs are bowls' give no definite link between cups and bowls, so 'Some cups are steel' (I) is uncertain. Only the mugs that are bowls are guaranteed to be steel, so 'All mugs are steel' (II) is too strong. Thus neither I nor II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since there is no definite link between cups and bowls, 'Some cups are steel' is uncertain.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Only the mugs that are bowls are guaranteed to be steel, so 'All mugs are steel' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Neither I nor II is definitely true, so both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** Watch out for 'All' in a conclusion when the premises only guarantee 'Some'.

### Q578 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: No brick is stone. Some stones are rocks. All rocks are hard. Conclusions: I. No brick is hard. II. Some hard are not bricks.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'No brick is stone' + 'Some stones are rocks' -> 'Some rocks are not bricks'. With 'All rocks are hard', those rocks that are not bricks are hard, so 'Some hard are not bricks' (II follows). Conclusion I ('No brick is hard') is uncertain because bricks could overlap with hard things outside rocks.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Bricks could be hard things that are not rocks, so 'No brick is hard' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is uncertain, both conclusions cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is guaranteed by the stones that are rocks and hence hard, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** 'Some X are not Y' + 'All X are Z' always yields 'Some Z are not Y'.

### Q579 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All trees are plants. All plants are green. Some trees are tall. Conclusions: I. Some tall are green. II. All tall are trees.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'All trees are plants' + 'All plants are green' -> 'All trees are green'. Combined with 'Some trees are tall', the tall trees are green, so 'Some tall are green' (I follows). Conclusion II ('All tall are trees') does not follow from 'Some trees are tall'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** 'Some trees are tall' only guarantees 'Some tall are trees', not 'All tall are trees'.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion II does not follow, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** 'All A are C' and 'Some A are D' always imply 'Some D are C' (and 'Some C are D').

### Q580 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: No pen is ink. All ink is blue. All blue is dark. Conclusions: I. Some dark is not pen. II. All ink is dark.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** 'No pen is ink' + 'All ink is blue' -> 'Some blue is not pen'. 'All blue is dark' -> 'Some dark is not pen' (I follows). Also, 'All ink is blue' + 'All blue is dark' -> 'All ink is dark' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II also follows from 'All ink is blue' and 'All blue is dark'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I also follows because ink (which is not pen) is inside dark.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both conclusions are logically sound, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** Trace each conclusion independently through the Venn diagram before combining them.

### Q581 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: Some coins are notes. Some notes are cards. No card is a bill. Conclusions: I. No coin is a bill. II. No note is a bill.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some coins are notes' + 'Some notes are cards' + 'No card is a bill' gives no definite relation between coins and bills (I uncertain). Also, 'Some notes are cards' + 'No card is a bill' gives 'Some notes are not bills', not 'No note is a bill' (II is too strong). Thus neither follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Coins and bills have no definite relation, so 'No coin is a bill' is uncertain.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Notes outside cards may still be bills, so 'No note is a bill' is not definite.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Neither conclusion is guaranteed, so both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** 'Some A are B' and 'No B is C' only proves 'Some A are not C', never 'No A is C'.

### Q582 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All shirts are cotton. Some cotton is white. All white is bright. Conclusions: I. All shirts are bright. II. Some cotton is bright.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'All shirts are cotton' + 'Some cotton is white' + 'All white is bright' -> 'Some cotton is bright' (II follows). Conclusion I ('All shirts are bright') is uncertain because cotton shirts need not overlap with white cotton.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Shirts are inside cotton, but the white cotton might not include shirts, so I is uncertain.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is uncertain, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true from the white cotton, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** 'Some A are B' + 'All B are C' always gives 'Some A are C'.

### Q583 · Syllogism - Three Statements

Statements: All lakes are ponds. No pond is a sea. All seas are deep. Conclusions: I. No lake is a sea. II. All deep are seas.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'All lakes are ponds' + 'No pond is a sea' -> 'No lake is a sea' (I follows). Conclusion II ('All deep are seas') is an invalid converse of 'All seas are deep'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** 'All seas are deep' only guarantees 'Some deep are seas', not 'All deep are seas'.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II is invalid, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is definitely true, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** An extra third statement does not invalidate a definite conclusion already formed by the first two statements.

### Q584 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: All bells are clocks. Some clocks are watches. Conclusions: I. All bells being watches is a possibility. II. All clocks being watches is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** Since 'All bells are clocks' and 'Some clocks are watches', there is no restriction preventing all bells from being inside watches, so 'All bells being watches is a possibility' (I follows). Also, 'Some clocks are watches' leaves open the possibility that all clocks are watches (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II is also a valid possibility because 'Some clocks are watches' does not forbid 'All clocks are watches'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I is also a valid possibility because bells are inside clocks and can lie inside watches.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both possibilities are consistent with the statements, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Whenever there is no negative relation blocking an overlap, 'All X being Y is a possibility' is true unless 'Some X are not Y' or 'No X is Y' is definite.

### Q585 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: Some pens are markers. No marker is a chalk. Conclusions: I. All pens being markers is a possibility. II. All pens being chalk is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'Some pens are markers' allows 'All pens being markers is a possibility' (I follows). However, 'No marker is a chalk' and 'Some pens are markers' mean at least some pens are not chalk, so 'All pens being chalk is a possibility' is impossible (II does not follow).
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** The pens that are markers can never be chalk, so all pens can never be chalk.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since conclusion II is impossible, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is a valid possibility, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** If 'Some A are not B' is definitely true, then 'All A being B is a possibility' is always false.

### Q586 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: All stars are bright. No bright is dull. Conclusions: I. Some stars being dull is a possibility. II. All bright being stars is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'All stars are bright' and 'No bright is dull' -> 'No star is dull', so 'Some stars being dull is a possibility' is false (I does not follow). However, 'All stars are bright' allows the two sets to be identical, so 'All bright being stars is a possibility' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since all stars are bright and no bright is dull, no star can ever be dull, even as a possibility.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I is impossible, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is a valid possibility, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** If 'All A are B' is given, then 'All B being A is a possibility' is always true.

### Q587 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: Some fruits are not ripe. All ripe are sweet. Conclusions: I. All fruits being ripe is a possibility. II. No ripe is a fruit.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some fruits are not ripe' makes 'All fruits being ripe is a possibility' impossible (I false). Also, ripe things could still be part of fruits, so 'No ripe is a fruit' (II) does not definitely follow. Thus neither follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Since some fruits are definitely not ripe, all fruits can never be ripe.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Ripe things could still be part of fruits, so 'No ripe is a fruit' does not definitely follow.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Since neither conclusion holds, both cannot follow.
- **Exam Trick:** A definite conclusion is never true if a counterexample diagram exists, and a possibility is never true if it violates a given premise.

### Q588 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: Some wires are cables. Some cables are ropes. Conclusions: I. All wires being ropes is a possibility. II. No wire being a rope is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Both conclusions I and II follow
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow (Correct):** 'Some wires are cables' and 'Some cables are ropes' leave wires and ropes unrestricted: all wires can be ropes (I follows), and no wire can be a rope (II follows, since ropes can be completely disjoint from wires).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion II is also a valid possibility because ropes need not touch wires.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion I is also a valid possibility because wires and ropes can overlap completely.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Both possibilities are consistent with the premises, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** When two terms have no definite relation, both positive ('All/Some') and negative ('No/Some not') possibilities between them are valid.

### Q589 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: No lion is a fox. All foxes are wild. Conclusions: I. All lions being wild is a possibility. II. Some foxes being lions is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Only conclusion I follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Only conclusion I follows (Correct):** 'No lion is a fox' and 'All foxes are wild' allow all lions to lie inside wild (outside foxes), so 'All lions being wild is a possibility' (I follows). Conclusion II ('Some foxes being lions is a possibility') is false because 'No lion is a fox'.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Since no lion is a fox, no fox can ever be a lion, even as a possibility.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion II is impossible, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion I is a valid possibility, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** When 'No A is B' and 'All B are C', 'All A being C is a possibility' is true, while 'All C being A is a possibility' is false.

### Q590 · Syllogism - Possibility

Statements: All notes are pages. No page is a book. All books are heavy. Conclusions: I. Some pages being notes is a possibility. II. All notes being heavy is a possibility.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Both conclusions I and II follow
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** B. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'All notes are pages' means 'Some pages are notes' is already definitely true; in SSC syllogism, a definite truth restated as a possibility does not follow (I is false). Meanwhile, 'No page is a book' + 'All books are heavy' allows notes to sit inside heavy outside books, so 'All notes being heavy is a possibility' (II follows).
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** 'Some pages are notes' is already definitely true, so stating it as a mere possibility does not follow in SSC logic.
- **C — Both conclusions I and II follow:** Because conclusion I fails the possibility rule, both cannot follow.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is a valid possibility (notes can be heavy without being books), so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **Exam Trick:** In SSC exams, if a statement is already 100% definitely true, calling it 'a possibility' makes that conclusion false.

### Q591 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: Some hats are caps. Some caps are gloves. Conclusions: I. Some hats are gloves. II. No hat is a glove.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Only conclusion II follows
C. Either conclusion I or II follows
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Either conclusion I or II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Either conclusion I or II follows (Correct):** 'Some hats are caps' and 'Some caps are gloves' give no definite relation between hats and gloves. Neither I ('Some hats are gloves') nor II ('No hat is a glove') is definitely true alone, and together they form a complementary 'Some + No' pair: one of them must be true.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion I is not definitely true because hats and gloves might not overlap.
- **B — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion II is not definitely true because hats and gloves might overlap.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Since I and II are mutually exhaustive ('Some' and 'No'), one of them must hold, ruling out 'Neither'.
- **Exam Trick:** For 'Either I or II' to hold: (1) both conclusions must be individually uncertain, (2) both must share the same two terms, and (3) they must form a complementary pair ('Some + No' or 'All + Some not').

### Q592 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: All desks are wood. Some wood is polish. Conclusions: I. All desks are polish. II. Some desks are not polish.

A. Either conclusion I or II follows
B. Only conclusion I follows
C. Only conclusion II follows
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Either conclusion I or II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Either conclusion I or II follows (Correct):** 'All desks are wood' and 'Some wood is polish' give no definite link between desks and polish. Individually both I ('All desks are polish') and II ('Some desks are not polish') are uncertain, and together 'All + Some not' with the same subject and predicate form a complementary Either-Or pair.
- **B — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion I is uncertain because desks may lie outside polish.
- **C — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion II is uncertain because all desks could lie inside polish.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Because 'All desks are polish' and 'Some desks are not polish' cover all cases, one must be true, ruling out 'Neither'.
- **Exam Trick:** In the 'All A are B' + 'Some A are not B' complementary pair, the subject and predicate order must match in both conclusions.

### Q593 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: Some rivers are streams. Some streams are ponds. Conclusions: I. All rivers are ponds. II. No river is a pond.

A. Either conclusion I or II follows
B. Only conclusion I follows
C. Only conclusion II follows
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows (Correct):** 'Some rivers are streams' and 'Some streams are ponds' give no definite link between rivers and ponds. Both 'All rivers are ponds' and 'No river is a pond' are individually uncertain, and they do not form a complementary pair because if only some rivers are ponds, both 'All' and 'No' are false simultaneously. Hence neither follows.
- **A — Either conclusion I or II follows:** Both conclusions can be false simultaneously (if only some rivers are ponds), so Either-Or does not apply.
- **B — Only conclusion I follows:** 'All rivers are ponds' is not guaranteed by the statements.
- **C — Only conclusion II follows:** 'No river is a pond' is not guaranteed by the statements.
- **Exam Trick:** 'All A are B' + 'No A is B' is a classic trap: it is never a valid Either-Or pair because both can be false at the same time.

### Q594 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: No coin is a medal. No medal is a trophy. Conclusions: I. Some trophies are coins. II. No coin is a trophy.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Either conclusion I or II follows
C. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
D. Only conclusion II follows

**Answer:** B. Either conclusion I or II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Either conclusion I or II follows (Correct):** 'No coin is a medal' and 'No medal is a trophy' give no definite relation between coins and trophies. 'Some trophies are coins' and 'No coin is a trophy' (equivalent to 'No trophy is a coin') are individually uncertain and form a valid 'Some + No' complementary pair. Thus Either I or II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Conclusion I is uncertain because coins and trophies could be disjoint.
- **C — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Either I or II must be true since they are complementary, so 'Neither' is wrong.
- **D — Only conclusion II follows:** Conclusion II is uncertain because coins and trophies could overlap.
- **Exam Trick:** For the 'Some + No' complementary pair, term order can be reversed because both 'Some A are B' and 'No A is B' are symmetric.

### Q595 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: All keys are metal. No metal is plastic. Conclusions: I. Some keys are plastic. II. No key is plastic.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Either conclusion I or II follows
C. Only conclusion II follows
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** C. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** From 'All keys are metal' and 'No metal is plastic', we get 'No key is plastic' definitely. Thus II ('No key is plastic') is definitely true and I ('Some keys are plastic') is definitely false. Since one conclusion is definitely true, Either-Or does not apply; only II follows.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** 'No key is plastic' is definitely true, so 'Some keys are plastic' is false.
- **B — Either conclusion I or II follows:** Either-Or only applies when both conclusions are individually uncertain, which is not the case here.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Never mark 'Either I or II' if one of the two conclusions is already definitely true or definitely false from the statements.

### Q596 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: Some trains are metros. All metros are fast. Conclusions: I. All trains are fast. II. Some trains are not fast.

A. Either conclusion I or II follows
B. Only conclusion I follows
C. Only conclusion II follows
D. Neither conclusion I nor II follows

**Answer:** A. Either conclusion I or II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Either conclusion I or II follows (Correct):** 'Some trains are metros' and 'All metros are fast' give 'Some trains are fast', which tells us nothing about whether 'All trains are fast' or 'Some trains are not fast'. Both I and II are individually uncertain, and 'All + Some not' with matching subject-predicate forms a valid Either-Or pair.
- **B — Only conclusion I follows:** 'Some trains are fast' does not guarantee 'All trains are fast'.
- **C — Only conclusion II follows:** 'Some trains are fast' does not rule out 'All trains are fast', so 'Some trains are not fast' is uncertain.
- **D — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** One of 'All trains are fast' or 'Some trains are not fast' must be true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Even when 'Some A are C' is true, 'All A are C' and 'Some A are not C' remain uncertain and form a valid Either-Or pair.

### Q597 · Syllogism - Either-Or

Statements: Some jars are lids. No lid is a cap. Conclusions: I. All jars are caps. II. Some jars are not caps.

A. Only conclusion I follows
B. Either conclusion I or II follows
C. Neither conclusion I nor II follows
D. Only conclusion II follows

**Answer:** D. Only conclusion II follows
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Only conclusion II follows (Correct):** 'Some jars are lids' and 'No lid is a cap' give 'Some jars are not caps'. Conclusion I ('All jars are caps') is definitely false, and Conclusion II ('Some jars are not caps') is definitely true. Since II is definitely true, only II follows—not Either-Or.
- **A — Only conclusion I follows:** Some jars (the ones that are lids) are definitely not caps, so 'All jars are caps' is false.
- **B — Either conclusion I or II follows:** Since conclusion II is definitely true and I is definitely false, Either-Or cannot apply.
- **C — Neither conclusion I nor II follows:** Conclusion II is definitely true, so 'Neither' is incorrect.
- **Exam Trick:** Always verify both conclusions independently first before checking for an Either-Or pair.

### Q598 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Seconds, Minutes, Hours?

A. Three mutually disjoint circles
B. Three concentric circles, one inside the other
C. Three mutually intersecting circles
D. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle

**Answer:** B. Three concentric circles, one inside the other
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Three concentric circles, one inside the other (Correct):** All seconds are part of minutes, and all minutes are part of hours. Therefore, the three classes form three concentric circles (Seconds inside Minutes inside Hours).
- **A — Three mutually disjoint circles:** Seconds, minutes, and hours are nested inside one another, not three separate disjoint circles.
- **C — Three mutually intersecting circles:** Seconds are strictly inside minutes and minutes are strictly inside hours, rather than merely intersecting.
- **D — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** Hours contain both minutes and seconds, so they cannot be two disjoint circles inside a third.
- **Exam Trick:** When every member of A is in B and every member of B is in C, the Venn diagram is three concentric circles.

### Q599 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Apples, Mangoes, Fruits?

A. Three concentric circles
B. Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle
C. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle
D. Three mutually disjoint circles

**Answer:** C. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle (Correct):** All apples are fruits and all mangoes are fruits, while no apple is a mango. Hence, Apples and Mangoes are two disjoint circles completely inside the larger circle of Fruits.
- **A — Three concentric circles:** Apples and mangoes are distinct fruits and do not nest one inside the other.
- **B — Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle:** Apples and mangoes do not overlap with each other; they are completely separate types of fruit.
- **D — Three mutually disjoint circles:** Apples and mangoes are not outside fruits; both belong entirely within the Fruits circle.
- **Exam Trick:** Two mutually exclusive subcategories of a single parent category are always drawn as two disjoint circles inside one larger circle.

### Q600 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Fathers, Males, Doctors?

A. One circle completely inside a second, and the third partially overlapping both
B. Three concentric circles
C. Three mutually disjoint circles
D. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle

**Answer:** A. One circle completely inside a second, and the third partially overlapping both
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — One circle completely inside a second, and the third partially overlapping both (Correct):** All fathers are males (Fathers circle completely inside Males circle). However, some doctors are fathers, some doctors are non-father males, and some doctors are females (outside Males). Thus Doctors partially overlaps both Fathers and Males.
- **B — Three concentric circles:** Not all doctors are males or fathers, so Doctors cannot be inside Males.
- **C — Three mutually disjoint circles:** Fathers are strictly a subset of Males, not completely disjoint from them.
- **D — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** Fathers and Doctors can overlap (a father can be a doctor), so they are not disjoint.
- **Exam Trick:** Gender-role relationships like 'Fathers, Males' or 'Mothers, Females' always place the role completely inside the gender circle.

### Q601 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Mothers, Sisters, Females?

A. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle
B. Three concentric circles
C. Three mutually intersecting circles
D. Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle

**Answer:** D. Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle (Correct):** All mothers are females and all sisters are females, so both circles lie completely inside Females. Furthermore, some mothers are also sisters, so Mothers and Sisters intersect each other inside Females.
- **A — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** Some mothers are sisters, so the two inner circles must intersect rather than be disjoint.
- **B — Three concentric circles:** A mother is not necessarily a sister (and vice versa), so they are not concentric.
- **C — Three mutually intersecting circles:** Both mothers and sisters are completely inside Females, not extending outside it.
- **Exam Trick:** When two roles belong to the same gender or category and can coexist in one person, draw them as two intersecting circles inside the parent circle.

### Q602 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Teachers, Writers, Musicians?

A. Three concentric circles
B. Three mutually intersecting circles
C. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle
D. One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint

**Answer:** B. Three mutually intersecting circles
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Three mutually intersecting circles (Correct):** A person can be a teacher and a writer, a writer and a musician, a teacher and a musician, or all three, while none of these professions is a strict subset of another. Thus they form three mutually intersecting circles.
- **A — Three concentric circles:** None of the three professions is completely contained inside another, so concentric circles do not apply.
- **C — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** All three can overlap with one another, so none of the circles should be disjoint.
- **D — One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint:** No single profession here encompasses the other two, so two circles cannot sit inside the third.
- **Exam Trick:** Independent professions or hobbies (e.g., Singers, Dancers, Teachers) are almost always represented by three mutually intersecting circles.

### Q603 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Sparrows, Birds, Dogs?

A. Three concentric circles
B. Three mutually intersecting circles
C. One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint
D. Three mutually disjoint circles

**Answer:** C. One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint (Correct):** Every sparrow is a bird (Sparrows completely inside Birds), while dogs are mammals and have zero overlap with either sparrows or birds. Thus one circle is inside another, and the third is completely disjoint.
- **A — Three concentric circles:** Dogs are not birds, so all three cannot be concentric.
- **B — Three mutually intersecting circles:** Dogs do not overlap with birds or sparrows, so there is no triple intersection.
- **D — Three mutually disjoint circles:** Sparrows are strictly inside Birds, not disjoint from them.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check if one class belongs to a completely different biological or physical category—if so, its circle is completely disjoint.

### Q604 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Copper, Paper, Oxygen?

A. Three mutually disjoint circles
B. Three concentric circles
C. Three mutually intersecting circles
D. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle

**Answer:** A. Three mutually disjoint circles
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Three mutually disjoint circles (Correct):** Copper is a metal, Paper is a cellulose product, and Oxygen is a gas—none of the three belongs to or overlaps with any other. Thus they are three mutually disjoint circles.
- **B — Three concentric circles:** None of these three is a subset of another, so they cannot be concentric.
- **C — Three mutually intersecting circles:** None of the three shares any member with another, so they cannot intersect.
- **D — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** No single class here contains the other two.
- **Exam Trick:** Distinct substances or unrelated everyday objects (e.g., Copper, Paper, Oxygen) form three completely separate circles.

### Q605 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which Venn diagram relationship best represents: Men, Doctors, Roses?

A. Three mutually intersecting circles
B. Three mutually disjoint circles
C. One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint
D. Two overlapping circles, with the third completely disjoint

**Answer:** D. Two overlapping circles, with the third completely disjoint
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Two overlapping circles, with the third completely disjoint (Correct):** Some men are doctors and some doctors are women, so Men and Doctors partially overlap. Roses are flowers and have no relation to Men or Doctors, so Roses is a third completely disjoint circle.
- **A — Three mutually intersecting circles:** Roses have no overlap with Men or Doctors, so all three cannot mutually intersect.
- **B — Three mutually disjoint circles:** Men and Doctors overlap (some men are doctors), so they cannot be three disjoint circles.
- **C — One circle inside another, with the third completely disjoint:** Not all doctors are men, so Doctors is not completely inside Men.
- **Exam Trick:** When two classes partially overlap and the third is unrelated to both, only two circles intersect while the third stands apart.

### Q606 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is best represented by three concentric circles, one inside the other?

A. Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle
B. Three concentric circles
C. Three mutually intersecting circles
D. Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle

**Answer:** B. Three concentric circles
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Three concentric circles (Correct):** Every square is a rectangle (Squares inside Rectangles), and every rectangle is a polygon (Rectangles inside Polygons). Hence Squares, Rectangles, and Polygons form three concentric circles.
- **A — Two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle:** Squares and rectangles are not disjoint; every square is a special type of rectangle.
- **C — Three mutually intersecting circles:** Squares are strictly inside Rectangles, not merely partially overlapping.
- **D — Two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle:** Squares are not outside Polygons; every square is a 4-sided polygon.
- **Exam Trick:** Geometric hierarchies like 'Squares, Rectangles, Polygons' or 'Triangles, Polygons, Closed Figures' are classic SSC concentric-circle questions.

### Q607 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is best represented by two disjoint circles inside a third larger circle?

A. Fathers, Brothers, Males
B. Pigeons, Birds, Dogs
C. Dogs, Cats, Mammals
D. Earth, Planets, Sun

**Answer:** C. Dogs, Cats, Mammals
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Dogs, Cats, Mammals (Correct):** Dogs and Cats are two completely distinct species, and both belong entirely to the class of Mammals. Therefore, Dogs and Cats are two disjoint circles inside the larger circle of Mammals.
- **A — Fathers, Brothers, Males:** Some fathers are brothers, so those two inner circles would intersect rather than be disjoint.
- **B — Pigeons, Birds, Dogs:** Pigeons are inside Birds while Dogs are completely outside Birds, which is one circle inside another plus one disjoint.
- **D — Earth, Planets, Sun:** The Sun is a star, not a planet, so it lies outside Planets rather than inside it.
- **Exam Trick:** Verify both conditions: the two inner classes must have zero overlap and both must be 100% inside the outer class.

### Q608 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is represented by one circle completely inside a second, and a third circle partially overlapping both?

A. Nephews, Males, Cooks
B. Mars, Venus, Planets
C. Tables, Chairs, Desks
D. Gold, Silver, Metals

**Answer:** A. Nephews, Males, Cooks
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Nephews, Males, Cooks (Correct):** All nephews are males (Nephews inside Males), and Cooks partially overlaps both Nephews and Males (since some cooks are nephews, some are non-nephew males, and some are females).
- **B — Mars, Venus, Planets:** Mars and Venus are disjoint inside Planets, with no overlapping third circle.
- **C — Tables, Chairs, Desks:** Tables, Chairs, and Desks are three distinct types of furniture (three disjoint circles).
- **D — Gold, Silver, Metals:** Gold and Silver are disjoint metals inside Metals, with no partially overlapping third circle.
- **Exam Trick:** Check whether the third class can be both inside and outside the outer circle; Cooks can be male or female, so it crosses the boundary of Males.

### Q609 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is best represented by two intersecting circles inside a third larger circle?

A. Apples, Oranges, Fruits
B. Physics, Chemistry, Science
C. Buses, Cars, Vehicles
D. Aunts, Nieces, Females

**Answer:** D. Aunts, Nieces, Females
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Aunts, Nieces, Females (Correct):** All aunts are females and all nieces are females, and a woman can be both an aunt and a niece at the same time. Thus Aunts and Nieces are two intersecting circles inside Females.
- **A — Apples, Oranges, Fruits:** Apples and Oranges do not overlap with each other inside Fruits.
- **B — Physics, Chemistry, Science:** Physics and Chemistry are distinct subjects inside Science without overlapping as subsets of each other.
- **C — Buses, Cars, Vehicles:** Buses and Cars are distinct vehicles without overlapping inside Vehicles.
- **Exam Trick:** Female family roles like 'Aunts, Nieces, Females' or 'Mothers, Daughters, Females' always intersect inside Females.

### Q610 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is best represented by one circle inside another, with the third circle completely disjoint?

A. Students, Athletes, Engineers
B. Wheat, Grains, Milk
C. Men, Doctors, Engineers
D. Days, Weeks, Months

**Answer:** B. Wheat, Grains, Milk
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Wheat, Grains, Milk (Correct):** Every wheat item is a grain (Wheat inside Grains), while Milk is a liquid dairy product completely unrelated to grains (disjoint).
- **A — Students, Athletes, Engineers:** Some athletes are students and some are not, so Athletes and Students only partially overlap.
- **C — Men, Doctors, Engineers:** Doctors and Engineers overlap with Men and with each other, rather than forming a nested pair plus a disjoint circle.
- **D — Days, Weeks, Months:** All three—Days, Weeks, Months—form three concentric circles.
- **Exam Trick:** In 'one inside another + one disjoint', ensure the two nested classes have no overlap whatsoever with the third class.

### Q611 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Class Relationships

Which set of classes is best represented by two overlapping circles and a third completely disjoint circle?

A. Lilies, Flowers, Stones
B. Iron, Copper, Zinc
C. Students, Players, Mountains
D. Sisters, Females, Teachers

**Answer:** C. Students, Players, Mountains
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Students, Players, Mountains (Correct):** Some students are players and some players are not students (overlapping circles), while Mountains have zero relation to students or players (disjoint circle).
- **A — Lilies, Flowers, Stones:** All lilies are flowers, so Lilies is completely inside Flowers rather than just partially overlapping.
- **B — Iron, Copper, Zinc:** Iron, Copper, and Zinc are three completely disjoint metals.
- **D — Sisters, Females, Teachers:** All sisters are females, so Sisters is completely inside Females.
- **Exam Trick:** Look for two human or role categories that overlap, paired with one inanimate or unrelated category.

### Q612 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a survey of 100 students, 60 like Maths, 50 like Science, and 20 like both. How many students like neither Maths nor Science?

A. 10
B. 20
C. 30
D. 40

**Answer:** A. 10
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 (Correct):** Using the inclusion-exclusion formula: Students who like at least one = 60 + 50 - 20 = 90. Therefore, students who like neither = 100 - 90 = 10.
- **B — 20:** 20 is the number of students who like both subjects, not neither.
- **C — 30:** 30 is the number of students who like only Science (50 - 20), not neither.
- **D — 40:** 40 is the number of students who like only Maths (60 - 20), not neither.
- **Exam Trick:** For two sets A and B: Total = n(A) + n(B) - n(A ∩ B) + Neither.

### Q613 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a class of 80 students, 45 play Cricket, 35 play Football, and 10 play neither sport. How many students play both Cricket and Football?

A. 5
B. 10
C. 15
D. 20

**Answer:** B. 10
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 10 (Correct):** Total playing at least one sport = 80 - 10 = 70. Using n(C ∪ F) = n(C) + n(F) - n(C ∩ F): 70 = 45 + 35 - Both, so Both = 80 - 70 = 10.
- **A — 5:** 5 undercounts the overlap; 45 + 35 - 5 + 10 would give 85, exceeding 80.
- **C — 15:** 15 overcounts the overlap; 45 + 35 - 15 + 10 would give 75, which is less than 80.
- **D — 20:** 20 would mean only 60 play at least one sport, giving 60 + 10 = 70 instead of 80.
- **Exam Trick:** Always subtract the 'Neither' count from the Total first to get n(A ∪ B).

### Q614 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

Out of 120 people in an office, 75 drink Tea, 25 drink both Tea and Coffee, and 15 drink neither. How many people drink Coffee only?

A. 25
B. 35
C. 30
D. 55

**Answer:** C. 30
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 30 (Correct):** Total drinking at least one = 120 - 15 = 105. Since 75 drink Tea, the remaining people who drink Coffee only = 105 - 75 = 30.
- **A — 25:** 25 is the number who drink both Tea and Coffee, not Coffee only.
- **B — 35:** 35 would give 75 + 35 + 15 = 125, exceeding the total of 120 people.
- **D — 55:** 55 is the total number who drink Coffee (including those who also drink Tea), not Coffee only.
- **Exam Trick:** n(Only B) = n(A ∪ B) - n(A), which saves a step when n(B) is not given.

### Q615 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a group of 60 people, everyone speaks at least one of English or Hindi. If 35 speak English and 15 speak both, how many speak only one language?

A. 15
B. 25
C. 35
D. 45

**Answer:** D. 45
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 45 (Correct):** Total = 60. Only English = 35 - 15 = 20. Only Hindi = 60 - 35 = 25. Thus those speaking only one language = 20 + 25 = 45.
- **A — 15:** 15 is the number of people who speak both languages, not exactly one.
- **B — 25:** 25 is the number who speak Hindi only, forgetting the 20 who speak English only.
- **C — 35:** 35 is the total who speak English (including bilinguals), not those who speak only one language.
- **Exam Trick:** 'Exactly one' of two sets equals Total - Both - Neither (here 60 - 15 - 0 = 45).

### Q616 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In an exam, 70% students passed in Reasoning, 65% passed in English, and 50% passed in both. If 30 students failed in both subjects, what is the total number of students?

A. 200
B. 150
C. 250
D. 300

**Answer:** A. 200
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 200 (Correct):** Passed at least one = 70% + 65% - 50% = 85%. Therefore, failed in both = 100% - 85% = 15%. Since 15% = 30 students, 100% = (30 / 15) × 100 = 200 students.
- **B — 150:** 150 students would mean 15% is 22.5, which does not match 30.
- **C — 250:** 250 students would mean 15% is 37.5, not 30.
- **D — 300:** 300 students would mean 15% is 45, not 30.
- **Exam Trick:** In percentage Venn problems, compute the 'Neither' percentage first, then equate it to the given raw count.

### Q617 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a Venn diagram, a Circle represents Doctors, a Triangle represents Singers, and a Square represents Dancers. If 12 lies only in the Circle, 8 lies in the intersection of Circle and Triangle only, 5 lies in all three shapes, and 9 lies only in the Square, how many Doctors are Singers but not Dancers?

A. 5
B. 8
C. 12
D. 13

**Answer:** B. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 (Correct):** In the Venn diagram, the region inside the Circle (Doctors) and Triangle (Singers) but outside the Square (Dancers) represents Doctors who are Singers but not Dancers. That region has value 8.
- **A — 5:** 5 represents those who are Doctors, Singers, and Dancers (all three).
- **C — 12:** 12 represents Doctors only (neither Singers nor Dancers).
- **D — 13:** 13 is the sum of 8 and 5, which includes Dancers as well.
- **Exam Trick:** When a question says 'A and B but not C', look only at the overlap of A and B that excludes C.

### Q618 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a Venn diagram, Square represents Athletes, Circle represents Graduates, and Triangle represents Musicians. The region inside Square only has 14, Square and Circle only has 6, Square and Triangle only has 7, and all three shapes have 3. What is the total number of Athletes?

A. 14
B. 20
C. 30
D. 27

**Answer:** C. 30
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 30 (Correct):** People who are Athletes (Square) include: Square-only (14), Square & Circle only (6), Square & Triangle only (7), and all three (3). Total Athletes = 14 + 6 + 7 + 3 = 30.
- **A — 14:** 14 counts only those Athletes who are neither Graduates nor Musicians.
- **B — 20:** 20 misses the 7 (Square & Triangle only) and 3 (all three) regions.
- **D — 27:** 27 forgets to include the 3 who belong to all three shapes.
- **Exam Trick:** To find the total count of one shape in a 3-set Venn diagram, sum all disjoint regions inside that shape.

### Q619 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a group of 50 people, everyone reads at least one newspaper among A, B, and C. If 25 read A, 22 read B, 20 read C, 8 read A and B, 7 read B and C, and 6 read A and C, how many read all three newspapers?

A. 2
B. 3
C. 5
D. 4

**Answer:** D. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4 (Correct):** Using the 3-set inclusion-exclusion formula: 50 = 25 + 22 + 20 - 8 - 7 - 6 + All three -> 50 = 46 + All three -> All three = 4.
- **A — 2:** 2 would give a union of 46 + 2 = 48, leaving 2 people unassigned.
- **B — 3:** 3 would give a union of 46 + 3 = 49 instead of 50.
- **C — 5:** 5 would give a union of 46 + 5 = 51, exceeding 50.
- **Exam Trick:** In a 3-set union: Total = Sum of singles - Sum of pairwise overlaps + All three (when none are outside).

### Q620 · Logical Venn Diagrams - Set Counts

In a survey, 8 people read newspapers A and B, 7 read B and C, 6 read A and C, and 4 read all three. How many people read exactly two newspapers?

A. 9
B. 13
C. 17
D. 21

**Answer:** A. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 9 (Correct):** 'A and B only' = 8 - 4 = 4, 'B and C only' = 7 - 4 = 3, and 'A and C only' = 6 - 4 = 2. Total reading exactly two newspapers = 4 + 3 + 2 = 9.
- **B — 13:** 13 subtracts 'All three' only twice instead of three times.
- **C — 17:** 17 subtracts 'All three' only once.
- **D — 21:** 21 is the sum of pairwise overlaps without subtracting the triple overlap.
- **Exam Trick:** To find 'exactly two' from pairwise totals, subtract 3 × (All three) from the sum of pairwise overlaps: (8 + 7 + 6) - 3(4) = 9.

---

# Section IV: Clocks, Calendars, Math Ops & Cube-Dice

## Chapter 9: Clocks & Calendars

### Q621 · Clocks - Angle Between Hands

What is the smaller angle between the hour hand and the minute hand of a clock at 3:40?

A. 120°
B. 130°
C. 140°
D. 150°

**Answer:** B. 130°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 130° (Correct):** Using the angle formula |30H − 11M/2| for H = 3 and M = 40: |30(3) − 11(40)/2| = |90 − 220| = 130°.
- **A — 120°:** 120° is the angle between 3 and 7 on the dial (4 × 30°), which ignores the 20° forward shift of the hour hand over 40 minutes.
- **C — 140°:** 140° results from miscalculating 5.5 × 40 or subtracting the hour hand's 20° movement in the wrong direction.
- **D — 150°:** 150° is the angle at 3:45 if the hour hand were stationary, not the actual angle at 3:40.
- **Exam Trick:** Always apply |30H − 5.5M|; if the result exceeds 180°, subtract it from 360° to get the smaller (interior) angle.

### Q622 · Clocks - Angle Between Hands

Find the angle between the minute hand and the hour hand of a clock at 8:25.

A. 97.5°
B. 100°
C. 102.5°
D. 115°

**Answer:** C. 102.5°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 102.5° (Correct):** For H = 8 and M = 25, |30(8) − 5.5(25)| = |240 − 137.5| = 102.5° (or 102°30').
- **A — 97.5°:** 97.5° comes from using 5° per minute instead of 5.5° per minute for the relative movement.
- **B — 100°:** 100° ignores the 2.5° half-minute fractional shift of the hour hand at 25 minutes past the hour.
- **D — 115°:** 115° corresponds to the angle between the 8 and 5 marks (90°) plus a wrong adjustment of 25°.
- **Exam Trick:** The minute hand moves 6° per minute while the hour hand moves 0.5° per minute, giving a relative speed of 5.5° per minute.

### Q623 · Clocks - Reflex Angle

What is the reflex angle between the hands of a clock at 10:25?

A. 162.5°
B. 182.5°
C. 192.5°
D. 197.5°

**Answer:** D. 197.5°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 197.5° (Correct):** First find the interior angle at 10:25: |30(10) − 5.5(25)| = |300 − 137.5| = 162.5°. The reflex angle is 360° − 162.5° = 197.5°.
- **A — 162.5°:** 162.5° is the smaller interior angle between the hands at 10:25, not the reflex angle.
- **B — 182.5°:** 182.5° comes from an arithmetic error when subtracting 162.5° from 345° instead of 360°.
- **C — 192.5°:** 192.5° results from miscalculating 5.5 × 25 as 142.5° instead of 137.5°.
- **Exam Trick:** A reflex angle is strictly greater than 180° and less than 360°; always subtract the acute/obtuse angle from 360°.

### Q624 · Clocks - Reflex Angle

Find the reflex angle between the hour hand and the minute hand of a clock at 9:15.

A. 187.5°
B. 172.5°
C. 180°
D. 195°

**Answer:** A. 187.5°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 187.5° (Correct):** At 9:15, |30(9) − 5.5(15)| = |270 − 82.5| = 187.5°. Since 187.5° is already greater than 180°, it is itself the reflex angle.
- **B — 172.5°:** 172.5° is the smaller interior angle (360° − 187.5°), which is obtuse rather than reflex.
- **C — 180°:** 180° ignores the 7.5° movement of the hour hand past the 9 mark in 15 minutes.
- **D — 195°:** 195° results from adding 7.5° to 187.5° or miscalculating 5.5 × 15 as 75°.
- **Exam Trick:** If |30H − 5.5M| already yields a value between 180° and 360°, do not subtract it from 360° when asked for the reflex angle.

### Q625 · Clocks - Angle Between Hands

What is the angle between the two hands of a clock at 4:12?

A. 48°
B. 52°
C. 54°
D. 60°

**Answer:** C. 54°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 54° (Correct):** For H = 4 and M = 12, |30(4) − 5.5(12)| = |120 − 66| = 54°.
- **A — 48°:** 48° is the angle between 12 minutes and 20 minutes (8 × 6°) if the hour hand stayed fixed at 4.
- **B — 52°:** 52° comes from approximating the hour hand's 6° shift as 4°.
- **D — 60°:** 60° is the angle at 4:10 if the hour hand remained stationary at 4.
- **Exam Trick:** At any time H:M, each minute division on the clock face equals 6°, and the hour hand advances 0.5° per minute.

### Q626 · Clocks - Angle Between Hands

What is the smaller angle between the hour hand and the minute hand at 12:20?

A. 100°
B. 110°
C. 120°
D. 130°

**Answer:** B. 110°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 110° (Correct):** At 12:20, take H = 0 (since 12 represents 0 hours). Angle = |30(0) − 5.5(20)| = 110°.
- **A — 100°:** 100° results from subtracting 20° instead of 10° for the hour hand's movement from 120°.
- **C — 120°:** 120° is the angle between the 12 and 4 marks, ignoring that the hour hand has moved 10° past 12.
- **D — 130°:** 130° incorrectly adds the hour hand's 10° movement to 120° instead of subtracting it.
- **Exam Trick:** For 12 o'clock times (12:M), substitute H = 0 in |30H − 5.5M| to avoid unnecessary subtraction from 360°.

### Q627 · Clocks - Angular Speed

Through how many degrees does the hour hand move from 2:00 PM to 5:20 PM on the same day?

A. 90°
B. 95°
C. 110°
D. 100°

**Answer:** D. 100°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 100° (Correct):** From 2:00 PM to 5:20 PM is 3 hours 20 minutes = 200 minutes. The hour hand moves at 0.5° per minute, so it covers 200 × 0.5° = 100°.
- **A — 90°:** 90° accounts only for the 3 full hours (3 × 30°) and omits the extra 20 minutes.
- **B — 95°:** 95° miscalculates 20 minutes of hour-hand movement as 5° instead of 10°.
- **C — 110°:** 110° overcounts the 20 minutes as 20° (1° per minute) instead of 10°.
- **Exam Trick:** An hour hand sweeps 360° in 12 hours, which is 30° per hour or 0.5° per minute.

### Q628 · Clocks - Angular Speed

How many degrees does the minute hand gain over the hour hand in 16 minutes?

A. 88°
B. 96°
C. 80°
D. 90°

**Answer:** A. 88°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 88° (Correct):** The minute hand gains 5.5° (or 11/2°) over the hour hand every minute. In 16 minutes, it gains 16 × 5.5° = 88°.
- **B — 96°:** 96° is the total angle moved by the minute hand alone (16 × 6°), without subtracting the hour hand's 8° movement.
- **C — 80°:** 80° uses a relative speed of 5° per minute instead of 5.5° per minute.
- **D — 90°:** 90° rounds 16 × 5.5° up instead of computing the exact product.
- **Exam Trick:** Relative speed of minute hand over hour hand = 6° − 0.5° = 5.5° per minute.

### Q629 · Clocks - Coinciding Hands (0°)

At what time between 4 and 5 o'clock do the hands of a clock coincide?

A. 20 minutes past 4
B. 22 9/11 minutes past 4
C. 21 9/11 minutes past 4
D. 21 7/11 minutes past 4

**Answer:** C. 21 9/11 minutes past 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 21 9/11 minutes past 4 (Correct):** Hands coincide when M = (60/11) × H. For H = 4, M = 240/11 = 21 9/11 minutes past 4.
- **A — 20 minutes past 4:** 20 minutes past 4 is when the minute hand reaches 4, by which time the hour hand has already moved ahead by 10°.
- **B — 22 9/11 minutes past 4:** 22 9/11 minutes past 4 comes from dividing 240 by 11 as 22 instead of 21.
- **D — 21 7/11 minutes past 4:** 21 7/11 minutes past 4 has an incorrect remainder (240 = 11 × 21 + 9, not 7).
- **Exam Trick:** Between H and H+1 o'clock, the hands coincide at (60H)/11 minutes past H.

### Q630 · Clocks - Coinciding Hands (0°)

At what time between 7 and 8 o'clock will the hands of a clock be together?

A. 35 minutes past 7
B. 38 2/11 minutes past 7
C. 38 5/11 minutes past 7
D. 39 2/11 minutes past 7

**Answer:** B. 38 2/11 minutes past 7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 38 2/11 minutes past 7 (Correct):** For H = 7, M = (60/11) × 7 = 420/11 = 38 2/11 minutes past 7.
- **A — 35 minutes past 7:** 35 minutes past 7 places the minute hand at 7, ignoring the hour hand's movement past 7.
- **C — 38 5/11 minutes past 7:** 38 5/11 minutes past 7 has the wrong remainder from dividing 420 by 11 (420 = 11 × 38 + 2).
- **D — 39 2/11 minutes past 7:** 39 2/11 minutes past 7 uses quotient 39 instead of 38 (11 × 39 = 429 > 420).
- **Exam Trick:** Notice that the whole number part plus the numerator of the fraction always ends in 0 (e.g., 38 + 2 = 40), a quick check for 11-denominator clock answers.

### Q631 · Clocks - Coinciding Hands (0°)

How many times do the hands of a clock coincide in 24 hours?

A. 24 times
B. 20 times
C. 11 times
D. 22 times

**Answer:** D. 22 times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 22 times (Correct):** The hands of a clock coincide 11 times in every 12 hours (only once between 11 and 1, at 12:00). Therefore, in 24 hours they coincide 11 × 2 = 22 times.
- **A — 24 times:** 24 times wrongly assumes the hands coincide once in every single hour without exception.
- **B — 20 times:** 20 times subtracts 2 coincides per 12 hours instead of 1.
- **C — 11 times:** 11 times is the count for 12 hours, not 24 hours.
- **Exam Trick:** Between 11:00 and 1:00, the hands coincide only once (at 12:00), reducing the 12-hour count from 12 to 11.

### Q632 · Clocks - Straight Line (180°)

At what time between 2 and 3 o'clock will the hands of a clock be in opposite directions?

A. 43 7/11 minutes past 2
B. 40 minutes past 2
C. 43 5/11 minutes past 2
D. 44 7/11 minutes past 2

**Answer:** A. 43 7/11 minutes past 2
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 43 7/11 minutes past 2 (Correct):** For 180° (opposite directions) between H and H+1, use M = (60/11) × (H ± 6). For H = 2 (< 6), M = (60/11) × (2 + 6) = 480/11 = 43 7/11 minutes past 2.
- **B — 40 minutes past 2:** 40 minutes past 2 puts the minute hand at 8, ignoring the hour hand's forward shift past 2.
- **C — 43 5/11 minutes past 2:** 43 5/11 minutes past 2 gives an incorrect remainder for 480 ÷ 11 (480 = 11 × 43 + 7).
- **D — 44 7/11 minutes past 2:** 44 7/11 minutes past 2 uses quotient 44 instead of 43 (11 × 44 = 484).
- **Exam Trick:** Use (H + 6) when H < 6 and (H − 6) when H ≥ 6 to find when the hands are 180° apart.

### Q633 · Clocks - Straight Line (180°)

At what time between 9 and 10 o'clock are the hands of a clock in a straight line, but not together?

A. 15 minutes past 9
B. 16 6/11 minutes past 9
C. 16 4/11 minutes past 9
D. 49 1/11 minutes past 9

**Answer:** C. 16 4/11 minutes past 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 16 4/11 minutes past 9 (Correct):** "In a straight line but not together" means 180° apart. For H = 9 (≥ 6), M = (60/11) × (9 − 6) = 180/11 = 16 4/11 minutes past 9.
- **A — 15 minutes past 9:** 15 minutes past 9 is when the minute hand is at 3, whereas the hour hand has moved 7.5° past 9.
- **B — 16 6/11 minutes past 9:** 16 6/11 minutes past 9 has the wrong remainder for 180 ÷ 11 (180 = 11 × 16 + 4).
- **D — 49 1/11 minutes past 9:** 49 1/11 minutes past 9 is the time when the hands coincide (0°), not when they are opposite (180°).
- **Exam Trick:** "Straight line" can mean either 0° or 180°, so read carefully for phrases like "not together" or "opposite directions".

### Q634 · Clocks - Straight Line (180°)

How many times in a day (24 hours) are the hands of a clock in a straight line?

A. 22 times
B. 44 times
C. 48 times
D. 46 times

**Answer:** B. 44 times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 44 times (Correct):** Hands are in a straight line when they are either 0° apart (22 times in 24 hours) or 180° apart (22 times in 24 hours). Total = 22 + 22 = 44 times.
- **A — 22 times:** 22 times counts only one type of straight line (either 0° or 180°), or gives the 12-hour total instead of 24 hours.
- **C — 48 times:** 48 times assumes 2 straight-line alignments in every hour without accounting for the 12:00 and 6:00 exceptions.
- **D — 46 times:** 46 times miscounts the exceptions at 12:00 and 6:00 as occurring only once a day.
- **Exam Trick:** In 12 hours, hands are at 0° 11 times and at 180° 11 times (missing 180° between 5 and 7 except at 6:00), making 22 straight-line alignments per 12 hours.

### Q635 · Clocks - Right Angle (90°)

At what time between 5 and 6 o'clock are the hands of a clock at right angles for the first time?

A. 10 minutes past 5
B. 11 9/11 minutes past 5
C. 43 7/11 minutes past 5
D. 10 10/11 minutes past 5

**Answer:** D. 10 10/11 minutes past 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 10 10/11 minutes past 5 (Correct):** Right angles occur when M = (60/11) × (H ± 3). For the first time between 5 and 6, use H − 3 = 5 − 3 = 2: M = 120/11 = 10 10/11 minutes past 5.
- **A — 10 minutes past 5:** 10 minutes past 5 ignores the 5° movement of the hour hand past 5.
- **B — 11 9/11 minutes past 5:** 11 9/11 minutes past 5 miscalculates 120 ÷ 11 as 11 instead of 10.
- **C — 43 7/11 minutes past 5:** 43 7/11 minutes past 5 is the second time the hands are at right angles between 5 and 6, not the first.
- **Exam Trick:** For 90° between H and H+1, (H − 3) gives the earlier right angle and (H + 3) gives the later one (wrapping mod 12).

### Q636 · Clocks - Right Angle (90°)

At what time between 7 and 8 o'clock are the hands of a clock perpendicular for the second time?

A. 54 6/11 minutes past 7
B. 21 9/11 minutes past 7
C. 54 4/11 minutes past 7
D. 55 minutes past 7

**Answer:** A. 54 6/11 minutes past 7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 54 6/11 minutes past 7 (Correct):** For the second right angle between 7 and 8, use (H + 3) = 7 + 3 = 10: M = (60/11) × 10 = 600/11 = 54 6/11 minutes past 7.
- **B — 21 9/11 minutes past 7:** 21 9/11 minutes past 7 is the first time the hands are at right angles between 7 and 8.
- **C — 54 4/11 minutes past 7:** 54 4/11 minutes past 7 has the wrong remainder for 600 ÷ 11 (600 = 11 × 54 + 6).
- **D — 55 minutes past 7:** 55 minutes past 7 places the minute hand at 11, ignoring the hour hand's 27.5° advance past 7.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check both (H − 3) and (H + 3) mod 12; the smaller positive value gives the first 90° and the larger gives the second.

### Q637 · Clocks - Right Angle (90°)

How many times are the hands of a clock at right angles between 1:00 PM and 10:00 PM on the same day?

A. 18 times
B. 14 times
C. 16 times
D. 17 times

**Answer:** C. 16 times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 16 times (Correct):** Normally there are 2 right angles per hour (9 × 2 = 18 in 9 hours). However, between 2 and 4 there are 3 (not 4), and between 8 and 10 there are 3 (not 4). Thus, 18 − 2 = 16 times.
- **A — 18 times:** 18 times ignores the two lost right angles at 3:00 (shared between 2–3 and 3–4) and 9:00 (shared between 8–9 and 9–10).
- **B — 14 times:** 14 times subtracts too many exceptions from the 9-hour span.
- **D — 17 times:** 17 times accounts for only one of the two 3-o'clock / 9-o'clock shared boundaries.
- **Exam Trick:** In any 12 hours, hands are at 90° 22 times (losing 1 each in 2–4 and 8–10); in 24 hours, 44 times.

### Q638 · Clocks - Relative Position

At what time between 3 and 4 o'clock is the minute hand 7 minutes ahead of the hour hand?

A. 3:22
B. 3:24
C. 3:25
D. 3:18

**Answer:** B. 3:24
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 3:24 (Correct):** At 3:00, the minute hand is 15 minute-spaces behind the hour hand. To be 7 minutes ahead, it must gain 15 + 7 = 22 minute-spaces. Time = 22 × (12/11) = 24 minutes past 3, i.e., 3:24.
- **A — 3:22:** 3:22 is the total number of minute-spaces to gain (22), forgetting to multiply by 12/11 for the hour hand's movement.
- **C — 3:25:** 3:25 is obtained by rough estimation rather than the exact 12/11 factor.
- **D — 3:18:** 3:18 results from subtracting 7 from 25 or misapplying the relative speed factor.
- **Exam Trick:** To gain x minute-spaces over the hour hand, the minute hand takes (12/11)x minutes.

### Q639 · Clocks - Mirror Image

If a clock shows 3:25, what time does its mirror image show?

A. 9:35
B. 8:25
C. 9:25
D. 8:35

**Answer:** D. 8:35
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 8:35 (Correct):** Subtract the given time from 11:60: 11:60 − 3:25 = 8:35.
- **A — 9:35:** 9:35 results from subtracting 3 from 12 instead of 11 when borrowing 60 minutes.
- **B — 8:25:** 8:25 only reflects the hour (12 − 3 = 9 or 11 − 3 = 8) while leaving the minutes unchanged.
- **C — 9:25:** 9:25 fails to reflect the minutes from 60.
- **Exam Trick:** For mirror image of any 12-hour time H:M (where H is 1 to 11), subtract from 11:60.

### Q640 · Clocks - Mirror Image

What is the mirror image of the time 12:42?

A. 11:18
B. 12:18
C. 11:42
D. 10:18

**Answer:** A. 11:18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 11:18 (Correct):** For times in the 12 o'clock hour, subtract from 23:60: 23:60 − 12:42 = 11:18.
- **B — 12:18:** 12:18 subtracts 12 from 24 instead of 23 after borrowing 60 minutes.
- **C — 11:42:** 11:42 reflects the hour correctly but fails to subtract 42 minutes from 60.
- **D — 10:18:** 10:18 subtracts 1 from 11 unnecessarily.
- **Exam Trick:** When the hour is 12 (or 00), use 23:60 − T instead of 11:60 − T to avoid negative hours.

### Q641 · Clocks - Mirror Image

Find the mirror image of a clock showing 7 hours 46 minutes 24 seconds.

A. 4:14:36
B. 5:13:36
C. 4:13:36
D. 4:13:24

**Answer:** C. 4:13:36
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 4:13:36 (Correct):** Subtract from 11:59:60: 11:59:60 − 7:46:24 = 4:13:36.
- **A — 4:14:36:** 4:14:36 borrows 60 seconds into the minutes column without reducing 60 minutes to 59.
- **B — 5:13:36:** 5:13:36 forgets to reduce 12 hours to 11 hours after borrowing.
- **D — 4:13:24:** 4:13:24 leaves the seconds unchanged instead of subtracting from 60.
- **Exam Trick:** When seconds are included in a mirror-image question, subtract from 11:59:60 (or 23:59:60 for 12:xx).

### Q642 · Clocks - Water Image

What will be the water image of a clock showing 4:15?

A. 7:45
B. 2:15
C. 2:45
D. 1:15

**Answer:** B. 2:15
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2:15 (Correct):** For water image (vertical reflection), subtract from 18:30 (when M ≤ 30): 18:30 − 4:15 = 14:15, which is 2:15.
- **A — 7:45:** 7:45 is the mirror image (11:60 − 4:15), not the water image.
- **C — 2:45:** 2:45 subtracts minutes from 60 instead of 30.
- **D — 1:15:** 1:15 comes from subtracting from 17:30 instead of 18:30.
- **Exam Trick:** For water image of time H:M, subtract from 18:30 if M ≤ 30, and from 17:90 if M > 30.

### Q643 · Clocks - Water Image

What is the water image time of a clock showing 8:50?

A. 3:10
B. 10:40
C. 9:10
D. 9:40

**Answer:** D. 9:40
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 9:40 (Correct):** Since minutes (50) > 30, subtract from 17:90: 17:90 − 8:50 = 9:40.
- **A — 3:10:** 3:10 is the mirror image (11:60 − 8:50), not the water image.
- **B — 10:40:** 10:40 forgets to reduce 18 to 17 when borrowing 60 minutes to make 90.
- **C — 9:10:** 9:10 subtracts 50 from 60 instead of 90.
- **Exam Trick:** 17:90 is simply 18:30 with 1 hour converted into 60 minutes so you can subtract M > 30 cleanly.

### Q644 · Clocks - Faulty Clocks

A clock is set right at 8:00 AM. It gains 10 minutes in 24 hours. What will be the true time when the clock indicates 1:00 PM on the following day?

A. 12:48 PM
B. 12:50 PM
C. 1:12 PM
D. 12:40 PM

**Answer:** A. 12:48 PM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 12:48 PM (Correct):** From 8 AM today to 1 PM tomorrow is 29 hours on the faulty clock. 24 hours 10 minutes (145/6 hours) of the faulty clock equals 24 true hours. Thus, 29 faulty hours = 29 × 24 × (6/145) = 28.8 true hours = 28 hours 48 minutes. Adding 28h 48m to 8:00 AM gives 12:48 PM on the following day.
- **B — 12:50 PM:** 12:50 PM approximates the gain as 10 minutes instead of computing the proportional gain over 28.8 true hours.
- **C — 1:12 PM:** 1:12 PM adds 12 minutes to 1:00 PM instead of subtracting, treating a fast clock as a slow clock.
- **D — 12:40 PM:** 12:40 PM subtracts 20 minutes from 1:00 PM, overestimating the clock's gain.
- **Exam Trick:** Always convert faulty clock elapsed time to true time using the ratio (True base / Faulty base) × Faulty elapsed.

### Q645 · Clocks - Faulty Clocks

A watch which gains uniformly is 2 minutes slow at noon on Monday and is 4 minutes 48 seconds fast at 2:00 PM on the following Monday. When was it correct?

A. Tuesday 2:00 PM
B. Wednesday 12:00 PM
C. Wednesday 2:00 PM
D. Thursday 2:00 PM

**Answer:** C. Wednesday 2:00 PM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Wednesday 2:00 PM (Correct):** Total duration from Monday 12:00 PM to next Monday 2:00 PM is 7 days 2 hours = 170 hours. Total gain = 2 + 4.8 = 6.8 minutes. Time to gain the initial 2 minutes = (2 / 6.8) × 170 = 50 hours. Adding 50 hours (2 days 2 hours) to Monday 12:00 PM gives Wednesday 2:00 PM.
- **A — Tuesday 2:00 PM:** Tuesday 2:00 PM is only 26 hours after Monday noon, during which the clock would still be slow.
- **B — Wednesday 12:00 PM:** Wednesday 12:00 PM is 48 hours after Monday noon, leaving the clock slightly short of catching up the full 2 minutes.
- **D — Thursday 2:00 PM:** Thursday 2:00 PM is 74 hours later, by which time the clock is already fast.
- **Exam Trick:** When a clock goes from x minutes slow to y minutes fast over T hours, it shows the exact time after [x / (x + y)] × T hours.

### Q646 · Clocks - Faulty Clocks

A clock loses 5 minutes every hour and is set right at 6:00 AM. What time will it show when the actual time is 4:00 PM on the same day?

A. 3:15 PM
B. 3:10 PM
C. 3:20 PM
D. 4:50 PM

**Answer:** B. 3:10 PM
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 3:10 PM (Correct):** From 6:00 AM to 4:00 PM is 10 actual hours. Since the clock loses 5 minutes per hour, total loss = 10 × 5 = 50 minutes. Thus, the clock shows 4:00 PM − 50 minutes = 3:10 PM.
- **A — 3:15 PM:** 3:15 PM subtracts only 45 minutes (9 hours' loss instead of 10).
- **C — 3:20 PM:** 3:20 PM subtracts 40 minutes (8 hours' loss instead of 10).
- **D — 4:50 PM:** 4:50 PM adds 50 minutes instead of subtracting, treating a slow clock as fast.
- **Exam Trick:** When loss is stated per actual hour, multiply directly by actual hours elapsed and subtract from the true time.

### Q647 · Clocks - Faulty Clocks

The minute hand of a clock overtakes the hour hand at intervals of 65 minutes of correct time. How much does the clock gain or lose in a day?

A. 10 10/143 minutes loss
B. 11 1/143 minutes gain
C. 9 10/143 minutes gain
D. 10 10/143 minutes gain

**Answer:** D. 10 10/143 minutes gain
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 10 10/143 minutes gain (Correct):** In a correct clock, hands coincide every 65 5/11 (720/11) minutes. Since this clock coincides every 65 minutes, it gains (720/11 − 65) = 5/11 minutes in 65 minutes. In 24 hours (1440 minutes), gain = (5/11) × (1440/65) = 1440/143 = 10 10/143 minutes.
- **A — 10 10/143 minutes loss:** 10 10/143 minutes loss misinterprets a shorter coincide interval (65 < 65 5/11) as losing time instead of gaining.
- **B — 11 1/143 minutes gain:** 11 1/143 minutes gain results from an arithmetic error when dividing 1440 by 143.
- **C — 9 10/143 minutes gain:** 9 10/143 minutes gain uses quotient 9 instead of 10 for 1440 ÷ 143.
- **Exam Trick:** If a clock's hands coincide every M minutes, it gains if M < 65 5/11 and loses if M > 65 5/11.

### Q648 · Clocks - Striking Clocks

A wall clock takes 12 seconds to strike 5. How much time will it take to strike 10?

A. 27 seconds
B. 24 seconds
C. 30 seconds
D. 25 seconds

**Answer:** A. 27 seconds
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 27 seconds (Correct):** To strike 5, there are 5 − 1 = 4 intervals between strikes, taking 12 seconds (3 seconds per interval). To strike 10, there are 10 − 1 = 9 intervals, taking 9 × 3 = 27 seconds.
- **B — 24 seconds:** 24 seconds wrongly assumes proportional scaling (12 × 10/5 = 24) instead of counting intervals.
- **C — 30 seconds:** 30 seconds multiplies 10 strikes by 3 seconds instead of 9 intervals by 3 seconds.
- **D — 25 seconds:** 25 seconds adds 12 and 13 arbitrarily without using the 3-second interval rate.
- **Exam Trick:** To strike N times, a clock has (N − 1) time intervals, not N intervals.

### Q649 · Clocks - Angle Between Hands

At what time between 4 and 5 o'clock is the angle between the hands of a clock 30° for the first time?

A. 27 3/11 minutes past 4
B. 15 minutes past 4
C. 16 4/11 minutes past 4
D. 16 6/11 minutes past 4

**Answer:** C. 16 4/11 minutes past 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 16 4/11 minutes past 4 (Correct):** Set |30(4) − 5.5M| = 30. For the first time (minute hand before hour hand), 120 − 5.5M = 30 ⇒ 5.5M = 90 ⇒ M = 180/11 = 16 4/11 minutes past 4.
- **A — 27 3/11 minutes past 4:** 27 3/11 minutes past 4 is the second time between 4 and 5 when the angle is 30° (120 − 5.5M = −30).
- **B — 15 minutes past 4:** 15 minutes past 4 ignores the hour hand's movement past 4.
- **D — 16 6/11 minutes past 4:** 16 6/11 minutes past 4 has the wrong remainder for 180 ÷ 11 (180 = 11 × 16 + 4).
- **Exam Trick:** Solving 30H − 5.5M = +θ gives the earlier occurrence of angle θ, while 30H − 5.5M = −θ gives the later occurrence.

### Q650 · Clocks - Faulty Clocks

Two clocks are set right at 12:00 noon. One gains 2 minutes per hour and the other loses 1 minute per hour. After how many hours will they differ by 1 hour?

A. 30 hours
B. 20 hours
C. 60 hours
D. 15 hours

**Answer:** B. 20 hours
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 20 hours (Correct):** One clock gains 2 min/hr and the other loses 1 min/hr, so their relative divergence is 2 + 1 = 3 minutes per hour. To differ by 1 hour (60 minutes), it takes 60 / 3 = 20 hours.
- **A — 30 hours:** 30 hours divides 60 by 2 (ignoring the second clock's loss of 1 min/hr).
- **C — 60 hours:** 60 hours divides 60 by 1 (subtracting 2 − 1 instead of adding 2 + 1).
- **D — 15 hours:** 15 hours divides 60 by 4 instead of 3.
- **Exam Trick:** When one clock gains a min/hr and another loses b min/hr, their readings diverge at (a + b) min/hr.

### Q651 · Clocks - Mirror Image

If the mirror image of a clock shows 9:27, what is the actual time?

A. 3:33
B. 2:27
C. 3:27
D. 2:33

**Answer:** D. 2:33
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 2:33 (Correct):** Mirror reflection is symmetric: actual time = 11:60 − 9:27 = 2:33.
- **A — 3:33:** 3:33 forgets to reduce 12 to 11 after borrowing 60 minutes.
- **B — 2:27:** 2:27 reflects the hour correctly but leaves the minutes unchanged.
- **C — 3:27:** 3:27 neither reduces the hour nor reflects the minutes.
- **Exam Trick:** The same 11:60 − T formula converts mirror time back to actual time and actual time to mirror time.

### Q652 · Clocks - Angular Speed

What is the ratio of the angular speed of the minute hand to that of the hour hand of a clock?

A. 12 : 1
B. 6 : 1
C. 24 : 1
D. 1 : 12

**Answer:** A. 12 : 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 12 : 1 (Correct):** The minute hand moves 6° per minute and the hour hand moves 0.5° per minute. Their speed ratio is 6 : 0.5 = 12 : 1.
- **B — 6 : 1:** 6 : 1 compares 6°/min to 1°/min instead of 0.5°/min.
- **C — 24 : 1:** 24 : 1 confuses a 12-hour dial with a 24-hour day.
- **D — 1 : 12:** 1 : 12 reverses the ratio (hour hand to minute hand).
- **Exam Trick:** In 12 hours, the minute hand completes 12 revolutions while the hour hand completes 1 revolution, confirming the 12:1 ratio.

### Q653 · Calendars - Odd Days

If today is Wednesday, what day of the week will it be after 94 days?

A. Friday
B. Sunday
C. Saturday
D. Thursday

**Answer:** C. Saturday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Saturday (Correct):** Divide 94 by 7: 94 = 7 × 13 + 3, giving 3 odd days. Wednesday + 3 days = Saturday.
- **A — Friday:** Friday adds only 2 odd days instead of 3.
- **B — Sunday:** Sunday adds 4 odd days instead of 3.
- **D — Thursday:** Thursday adds 1 odd day instead of 3.
- **Exam Trick:** To find the day after N days, simply add (N mod 7) days to the given day.

### Q654 · Calendars - Odd Days

How many odd days are there in 300 consecutive years from the start of the calendar?

A. 3
B. 1
C. 5
D. 0

**Answer:** B. 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1 (Correct):** 100 years have 5 odd days, 200 years have 10 ≡ 3 odd days, and 300 years have 15 ≡ 1 odd day (400 years have 0 odd days).
- **A — 3:** 3 is the number of odd days in 200 years, not 300 years.
- **C — 5:** 5 is the number of odd days in 100 years, not 300 years.
- **D — 0:** 0 is the number of odd days in 400 years, not 300 years.
- **Exam Trick:** Memorize the century odd-day cycle: 100 → 5, 200 → 3, 300 → 1, 400 → 0.

### Q655 · Calendars - Century Rules

Which of the following days CANNOT be the last day of a century?

A. Monday
B. Wednesday
C. Friday
D. Tuesday

**Answer:** D. Tuesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Tuesday (Correct):** The odd days at the end of 100, 200, 300, and 400 years are 5 (Friday), 3 (Wednesday), 1 (Monday), and 0 (Sunday). Therefore, Tuesday, Thursday, and Saturday can never be the last day of a century.
- **A — Monday:** Monday is the last day of the 3rd century (300 years = 1 odd day).
- **B — Wednesday:** Wednesday is the last day of the 2nd century (200 years = 3 odd days).
- **C — Friday:** Friday is the last day of the 1st century (100 years = 5 odd days).
- **Exam Trick:** A century can only end on Sunday, Monday, Wednesday, or Friday (5, 3, 1, 0 odd days).

### Q656 · Calendars - Century Rules

Which of the following days CANNOT be the first day of a century?

A. Wednesday
B. Monday
C. Tuesday
D. Thursday

**Answer:** A. Wednesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Wednesday (Correct):** Since a century ends on Friday, Wednesday, Monday, or Sunday, the next century must begin on Saturday, Thursday, Tuesday, or Monday. Thus, Wednesday (along with Friday and Sunday) cannot be the first day of a century.
- **B — Monday:** Monday can be the first day of a century (e.g., after a century ending on Sunday, such as 2001).
- **C — Tuesday:** Tuesday can be the first day of a century (after a century ending on Monday).
- **D — Thursday:** Thursday can be the first day of a century (after a century ending on Wednesday).
- **Exam Trick:** First days of a century can only be Monday, Tuesday, Thursday, or Saturday; Monday is the only day that can be both first and last.

### Q657 · Calendars - Historic Dates

What was the day of the week on 15th August 1947?

A. Thursday
B. Saturday
C. Friday
D. Wednesday

**Answer:** C. Friday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Friday (Correct):** Up to 1900: 1600 (0) + 300 (1) = 1 odd day. For 46 years (1901–1946): 11 leap + 35 ordinary = 57 ≡ 1 odd day. In 1947 up to Aug 15: Jan(3)+Feb(0)+Mar(3)+Apr(2)+May(3)+Jun(2)+Jul(3)+15(1) = 17 ≡ 3 odd days. Total = 1 + 1 + 3 = 5 odd days → Friday.
- **A — Thursday:** Thursday corresponds to 4 odd days, missing 1 odd day in the 1947 month count.
- **B — Saturday:** Saturday corresponds to 6 odd days, overcounting 1947 as a leap year.
- **D — Wednesday:** Wednesday corresponds to 3 odd days, omitting the 2 odd days from 1900–1946.
- **Exam Trick:** 15th August 1947 (Indian Independence Day) was a Friday—one of the most frequently tested historic dates in SSC exams.
