### Q658 · Calendars - Historic Dates

What was the day of the week on 26th January 1950?

A. Wednesday
B. Thursday
C. Friday
D. Tuesday

**Answer:** B. Thursday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Thursday (Correct):** Up to 1900: 1 odd day. For 49 years (1901–1949): 12 leap + 37 ordinary = 61 ≡ 5 odd days. In 1950 up to Jan 26: 26 ≡ 5 odd days. Total = 1 + 5 + 5 = 11 ≡ 4 odd days → Thursday.
- **A — Wednesday:** Wednesday corresponds to 3 odd days, often from taking 11 leap years instead of 12 between 1901 and 1949.
- **C — Friday:** Friday corresponds to 5 odd days, wrongly counting 1950 as a leap year or adding 1 extra day.
- **D — Tuesday:** Tuesday corresponds to 2 odd days, miscounting the 1900 century odd day.
- **Exam Trick:** 26th January 1950 (India's first Republic Day) fell on a Thursday.

### Q659 · Calendars - Historic Dates

What was the day of the week on 2nd October 1869?

A. Friday
B. Sunday
C. Monday
D. Saturday

**Answer:** D. Saturday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Saturday (Correct):** Up to 1800: 1600 (0) + 200 (3) = 3 odd days. For 68 years (1801–1868): 17 leap + 51 ordinary = 85 ≡ 1 odd day. In 1869 up to Oct 2: Jan(3)+Feb(0)+Mar(3)+Apr(2)+May(3)+Jun(2)+Jul(3)+Aug(3)+Sep(2)+2 = 23 ≡ 2 odd days. Total = 3 + 1 + 2 = 6 odd days → Saturday.
- **A — Friday:** Friday corresponds to 5 odd days, missing 1 odd day from the 17 leap years in 1801–1868.
- **B — Sunday:** Sunday corresponds to 0 odd days, overcounting 1800 as having 4 odd days instead of 3.
- **C — Monday:** Monday corresponds to 1 odd day, miscalculating the 200-year odd days as 5 instead of 3.
- **Exam Trick:** 2nd October 1869 (Mahatma Gandhi's birthday) was a Saturday.

### Q660 · Calendars - Exact Date

What was the day of the week on 28th May 2006?

A. Sunday
B. Saturday
C. Monday
D. Friday

**Answer:** A. Sunday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Sunday (Correct):** Up to 2000: 0 odd days. For 5 years (2001–2005): 1 leap (2004) + 4 ordinary = 6 odd days. In 2006 up to May 28: Jan(3)+Feb(0)+Mar(3)+Apr(2)+28(0) = 8 ≡ 1 odd day. Total = 0 + 6 + 1 = 7 ≡ 0 odd days → Sunday.
- **B — Saturday:** Saturday corresponds to 6 odd days, forgetting the leap day in 2004.
- **C — Monday:** Monday corresponds to 1 odd day, wrongly treating 2006 as a leap year.
- **D — Friday:** Friday corresponds to 5 odd days, miscounting the 5 years from 2001 to 2005.
- **Exam Trick:** Remember that 2000 is a leap century, so 1600 and 2000 both contribute 0 odd days.

### Q661 · Calendars - Leap Year Rules

Which of the following years is NOT a leap year?

A. 1600
B. 2000
C. 1900
D. 2400

**Answer:** C. 1900
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1900 (Correct):** A century year (ending in 00) is a leap year only if it is divisible by 400. 1600, 2000, and 2400 are divisible by 400, whereas 1900 is not.
- **A — 1600:** 1600 is divisible by 400, so it is a leap year.
- **B — 2000:** 2000 is divisible by 400, so it is a leap year.
- **D — 2400:** 2400 is divisible by 400, so it is a leap year.
- **Exam Trick:** Non-century years are leap years if divisible by 4; century years must be divisible by 400.

### Q662 · Calendars - Leap Year Rules

How many leap years are there in 400 consecutive years?

A. 100
B. 97
C. 96
D. 98

**Answer:** B. 97
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 97 (Correct):** Every 4th year is a leap year, giving 100 leap years in 400 years, except that the 3 century years not divisible by 400 (e.g., 100, 200, 300) are not leap years. Thus, 100 − 3 = 97 leap years.
- **A — 100:** 100 wrongly counts all four century years in a 400-year span as leap years.
- **C — 96:** 96 subtracts all four century years instead of only three.
- **D — 98:** 98 subtracts only two century years instead of three.
- **Exam Trick:** In 400 consecutive years, there are 24 + 24 + 24 + 25 = 97 leap years.

### Q663 · Calendars - Leap Year Rules

How many times does 29th February occur in 400 consecutive years?

A. 100 times
B. 96 times
C. 4497 times
D. 97 times

**Answer:** D. 97 times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 97 times (Correct):** 29th February occurs only in leap years. Since there are 97 leap years in 400 consecutive years, 29th February occurs 97 times.
- **A — 100 times:** 100 ignores the 3 non-leap century years in every 400-year span.
- **B — 96 times:** 96 omits the 400th-year leap year (divisible by 400).
- **C — 4497 times:** 4497 is the total number of times the 29th of ANY month occurs in 400 years, not 29th February alone.
- **Exam Trick:** The frequency of 29th February in any 400-year cycle is always 97, regardless of which year the cycle starts.

### Q664 · Calendars - Leap Year Rules

How many times does the 29th day of a month occur in 400 consecutive years?

A. 4497 times
B. 4400 times
C. 4500 times
D. 4496 times

**Answer:** A. 4497 times
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 4497 times (Correct):** In every year, 11 months (all except February) have a 29th day: 11 × 400 = 4400. February has a 29th day 97 times in 400 years. Total = 4400 + 97 = 4497 times.
- **B — 4400 times:** 4400 forgets to add the 97 occurrences of 29th February.
- **C — 4500 times:** 4500 adds 100 leap years instead of 97 to 4400.
- **D — 4496 times:** 4496 adds only 96 leap years instead of 97.
- **Exam Trick:** Distinguish carefully between "29th February" (97 times) and "29th day of a month" (4497 times) in 400 years.

### Q665 · Calendars - Leap Year Rules

What is the maximum possible gap between two consecutive leap years?

A. 4 years
B. 6 years
C. 8 years
D. 12 years

**Answer:** C. 8 years
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 8 years (Correct):** Normally leap years are 4 years apart, but when a non-leap century year falls in between (e.g., 1896 to 1904, skipping 1900), the gap is 8 years.
- **A — 4 years:** 4 years is the standard gap between leap years, not the maximum possible gap.
- **B — 6 years:** 6 years does not align with the 4-year divisibility rule.
- **D — 12 years:** 12 years never occurs because at most one non-leap century year can fall between two multiples of 4.
- **Exam Trick:** The maximum gap of 8 years happens around century years not divisible by 400 (1700, 1800, 1900, 2100).

### Q666 · Calendars - Repeating Calendar

The calendar for the year 2007 will be the same as for which of the following years?

A. 2013
B. 2018
C. 2019
D. 2016

**Answer:** B. 2018
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2018 (Correct):** 2007 is (leap year + 3), i.e., 3 years after 2004. Adding 11 years gives 2007 + 11 = 2018 (and the odd days from 2007 to 2017 sum to 14 ≡ 0).
- **A — 2013:** 2013 adds 6 years, which works for (leap + 1) years, not (leap + 3) years.
- **C — 2019:** 2019 adds 12 years, which leaves 1 extra odd day.
- **D — 2016:** 2016 is a leap year, so it cannot have the same full-year calendar as an ordinary year like 2007.
- **Exam Trick:** Shortcut for repeating calendars (without crossing a non-leap century): Leap → +28; Leap+1 → +6; Leap+2 or Leap+3 → +11.

### Q667 · Calendars - Repeating Calendar

The calendar for the year 2017 will be the same as for which year?

A. 2028
B. 2022
C. 2024
D. 2023

**Answer:** D. 2023
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 2023 (Correct):** 2017 is (leap year + 1), i.e., 1 year after 2016. Adding 6 years gives 2017 + 6 = 2023.
- **A — 2028:** 2028 adds 11 years and lands on a leap year, which cannot match an ordinary year.
- **B — 2022:** 2022 adds only 5 years (6 odd days, not a multiple of 7).
- **C — 2024:** 2024 is a leap year and 7 years away, so its calendar differs from March onward.
- **Exam Trick:** Always verify that both the original year and the target year are of the same type (both ordinary or both leap).

### Q668 · Calendars - Repeating Calendar

The calendar for the year 2024 will be the same as for which of the following years?

A. 2052
B. 2035
C. 2040
D. 2048

**Answer:** A. 2052
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2052 (Correct):** 2024 is a leap year. A leap year's calendar repeats after 28 years (when no non-leap century year intervenes): 2024 + 28 = 2052.
- **B — 2035:** 2035 adds 11 years, landing on an ordinary year instead of a leap year.
- **C — 2040:** 2040 is 16 years later (20 odd days ≡ 6 mod 7, not 0).
- **D — 2048:** 2048 is 24 years later (30 odd days ≡ 2 mod 7, not 0).
- **Exam Trick:** A leap year calendar can only repeat in another leap year, typically after 28 years.

### Q669 · Calendars - Repeating Calendar

The calendar for the year 1897 was the same as for which of the following years?

A. 1903
B. 1908
C. 1909
D. 1904

**Answer:** C. 1909
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1909 (Correct):** Although 1897 is (leap + 1), the +6 shortcut (1903) fails because 1900 is NOT a leap year! Counting odd days from 1897: 1897(1)+1898(1)+1899(1)+1900(1)+1901(1)+1902(1)+1903(1)+1904(2)+1905(1)+1906(1)+1907(1)+1908(2) = 14 ≡ 0 odd days. Thus, 1909 had the same calendar as 1897.
- **A — 1903:** 1903 blindly applies the +6 rule without noticing that 1900 was not a leap year (giving only 6 odd days instead of 7).
- **B — 1908:** 1908 is a leap year, so it cannot match an ordinary year like 1897.
- **D — 1904:** 1904 is a leap year and only 7 odd days away up to Feb, diverging in March.
- **Exam Trick:** Never blindly apply the +6/+11/+28 shortcuts across a non-leap century year (like 1900 or 2100); count odd days directly.

### Q670 · Calendars - Same Month Calendar

In an ordinary (non-leap) year, which month has the exact same calendar as January?

A. July
B. October
C. August
D. November

**Answer:** B. October
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — October (Correct):** In an ordinary year, odd days from January to September are: Jan(3)+Feb(0)+Mar(3)+Apr(2)+May(3)+Jun(2)+Jul(3)+Aug(3)+Sep(2) = 21 ≡ 0 odd days. Hence, January and October start on the same day and have the exact same calendar.
- **A — July:** July has 3 + 0 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 2 = 13 ≡ 6 odd days after January, so it starts 1 day earlier.
- **C — August:** August has 16 ≡ 2 odd days after January, so it starts 2 days later.
- **D — November:** November has 24 ≡ 3 odd days after January, matching February and March instead.
- **Exam Trick:** In an ordinary year, (Jan, Oct), (Feb, Mar, Nov), (Apr, Jul), and (Sep, Dec) share identical starting days.

### Q671 · Calendars - Same Month Calendar

In a leap year, which of the following months starts on the same day of the week as January?

A. October
B. August
C. November
D. April

**Answer:** D. April
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — April (Correct):** In a leap year, odd days from January to March: Jan(3)+Feb(1)+Mar(3) = 7 ≡ 0 odd days, so January and April start on the same day (and July also starts on the same day, though April and July have 30 and 31 days respectively, they share the same 1st–30th layout).
- **A — October:** October has 22 ≡ 1 odd day after January in a leap year, unlike in an ordinary year.
- **B — August:** August has 17 ≡ 3 odd days after January in a leap year.
- **C — November:** November has 25 ≡ 4 odd days after January in a leap year.
- **Exam Trick:** In a leap year, Jan, Apr, and Jul start on the same day of the week.

### Q672 · Calendars - Relative Day

If 1st March 2023 was a Wednesday, what day of the week was 15th August 2023?

A. Tuesday
B. Monday
C. Wednesday
D. Thursday

**Answer:** A. Tuesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Tuesday (Correct):** Days from 1st March to 15th August 2023: Mar(30) + Apr(30) + May(31) + Jun(30) + Jul(31) + Aug(15) = 167 days. 167 mod 7 = 6 odd days. Wednesday + 6 days = Tuesday.
- **B — Monday:** Monday adds 5 odd days instead of 6.
- **C — Wednesday:** Wednesday adds 7 odd days (0 mod 7) instead of 6.
- **D — Thursday:** Thursday adds 1 odd day instead of 6.
- **Exam Trick:** Counting remaining days in the start month + full intermediate months + target date and taking mod 7 avoids off-by-one errors.

### Q673 · Calendars - Relative Day

If 10th February 2019 was a Sunday, what day of the week was 10th February 2021?

A. Tuesday
B. Thursday
C. Wednesday
D. Monday

**Answer:** C. Wednesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Wednesday (Correct):** From 10th Feb 2019 to 10th Feb 2020 is 1 ordinary year (+1 odd day, since Feb 29, 2020 hasn't occurred yet). From 10th Feb 2020 to 10th Feb 2021 includes 29th Feb 2020 (+2 odd days). Total = 1 + 2 = 3 odd days. Sunday + 3 = Wednesday.
- **A — Tuesday:** Tuesday adds only 2 odd days, missing the leap day of 2020.
- **B — Thursday:** Thursday adds 4 odd days, wrongly counting both 2019 and 2020 as leap years.
- **D — Monday:** Monday adds only 1 odd day.
- **Exam Trick:** A leap year adds 2 odd days only if 29th February lies between the two dates.

### Q674 · Calendars - Relative Day

If 18th October 2006 was a Wednesday, what day of the week was 18th October 2000?

A. Tuesday
B. Wednesday
C. Thursday
D. Monday

**Answer:** B. Wednesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Wednesday (Correct):** Going back from 18th Oct 2006 to 18th Oct 2000 covers 6 years, including only 1 leap day (29th Feb 2004, since 29th Feb 2000 is before Oct 2000). Total odd days = 6 + 1 = 7 ≡ 0. Wednesday − 0 = Wednesday.
- **A — Tuesday:** Tuesday subtracts 1 extra day by mistakenly including 29th Feb 2000 in the interval.
- **C — Thursday:** Thursday moves forward by 1 day instead of staying at 0 mod 7.
- **D — Monday:** Monday subtracts 2 days instead of 0.
- **Exam Trick:** When moving backward in time, subtract the odd days from the given day; if the remainder is 0, the day remains the same.

### Q675 · Calendars - Birthday Interval

Arun's birthday is on Monday, 19th June. On what day of the week will Mahesh's birthday be in the same year if Mahesh was born on 17th November?

A. Thursday
B. Saturday
C. Wednesday
D. Friday

**Answer:** D. Friday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Friday (Correct):** Days from 19th June to 17th November: June(11) + July(31) + Aug(31) + Sep(30) + Oct(31) + Nov(17) = 151 days. 151 mod 7 = 4 odd days. Monday + 4 = Friday.
- **A — Thursday:** Thursday adds 3 odd days instead of 4.
- **B — Saturday:** Saturday adds 5 odd days instead of 4.
- **C — Wednesday:** Wednesday adds 2 odd days instead of 4.
- **Exam Trick:** You can take mod 7 of each month's days first: 4 + 3 + 3 + 2 + 3 + 3 = 18 ≡ 4 odd days.

### Q676 · Calendars - Birthday Interval

Riya is 300 days older than Suman, and Kavya is 50 weeks older than Riya. If Kavya was born on Tuesday, on which day was Suman born?

A. Monday
B. Wednesday
C. Sunday
D. Tuesday

**Answer:** A. Monday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Monday (Correct):** Kavya is 50 weeks (350 days, 0 odd days) older than Riya, so Riya was also born on Tuesday. Riya is 300 days older than Suman, meaning Suman was born 300 days AFTER Riya. 300 mod 7 = 6. Tuesday + 6 = Monday.
- **B — Wednesday:** Wednesday subtracts 6 days (goes backward) instead of adding 6 days for the younger person.
- **C — Sunday:** Sunday adds 5 odd days instead of 6.
- **D — Tuesday:** Tuesday ignores the 300 days difference between Riya and Suman.
- **Exam Trick:** "A is older than B" means A was born BEFORE B, so B's birthday is AFTER A's (add odd days).

### Q677 · Calendars - Year Boundaries

If 1st January 2013 was a Tuesday, what day of the week was 31st December 2013?

A. Wednesday
B. Monday
C. Tuesday
D. Thursday

**Answer:** C. Tuesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Tuesday (Correct):** An ordinary year has 365 days = 52 weeks + 1 day, so its first day (1st Jan) and last day (31st Dec) are always the same day of the week: Tuesday.
- **A — Wednesday:** Wednesday is 1st January 2014, not 31st December 2013.
- **B — Monday:** Monday is 1 day earlier, which never happens when moving forward through a year.
- **D — Thursday:** Thursday would only occur if 2013 were a leap year and you jumped to 1st January 2014.
- **Exam Trick:** In an ordinary year, 1st Jan and 31st Dec fall on the same day; in a leap year, 31st Dec is 1 day ahead of 1st Jan.

### Q678 · Calendars - Year Boundaries

If 1st January 2016 was a Friday, what day of the week was 1st January 2017?

A. Saturday
B. Sunday
C. Monday
D. Friday

**Answer:** B. Sunday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — Sunday (Correct):** 2016 was a leap year (366 days = 52 weeks + 2 odd days). Therefore, 1st January 2017 was Friday + 2 days = Sunday.
- **A — Saturday:** Saturday adds only 1 day, treating 2016 as an ordinary year.
- **C — Monday:** Monday adds 3 days instead of 2.
- **D — Friday:** Friday assumes no shift across a full year.
- **Exam Trick:** Crossing a leap year's February shifts the same date next year forward by 2 days.

### Q679 · Calendars - Odd Days

If the 4th day of a month is Sunday, what will be the 27th day of the same month?

A. Monday
B. Wednesday
C. Sunday
D. Tuesday

**Answer:** D. Tuesday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — Tuesday (Correct):** Difference = 27 − 4 = 23 days. 23 mod 7 = 2 odd days. Sunday + 2 days = Tuesday.
- **A — Monday:** Monday adds only 1 odd day (22 mod 7 instead of 23 mod 7).
- **B — Wednesday:** Wednesday adds 3 odd days (24 mod 7).
- **C — Sunday:** Sunday assumes 27 − 4 is a multiple of 7.
- **Exam Trick:** Any date + 7, + 14, + 21, or + 28 falls on the exact same day of the week.

### Q680 · Calendars - Month Structure

If a month of 30 days has 5 Saturdays, which of the following days can be the 1st of that month?

A. Friday
B. Sunday
C. Wednesday
D. Thursday

**Answer:** A. Friday
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — Friday (Correct):** A 30-day month has 4 full weeks (28 days) plus 2 extra days (the 29th and 30th, which share the same weekdays as the 1st and 2nd). For Saturday to occur 5 times, Saturday must be either the 1st or the 2nd. If Saturday is the 2nd, the 1st is Friday.
- **B — Sunday:** Sunday as the 1st would make Sunday and Monday occur 5 times, leaving only 4 Saturdays.
- **C — Wednesday:** Wednesday as the 1st would make Wednesday and Thursday occur 5 times.
- **D — Thursday:** Thursday as the 1st would make Thursday and Friday occur 5 times.
- **Exam Trick:** In a 30-day month, only the 1st and 2nd weekdays occur 5 times; in a 31-day month, the 1st, 2nd, and 3rd occur 5 times.

---

## Chapter 10: Mathematical Operations, Missing Numbers & Cube/Dice

### Q681 · Mathematical Operations

If '+' means '÷', '−' means '×', '×' means '+', and '÷' means '−', what is the value of: 48 + 6 − 4 ÷ 12 × 8?

A. 24
B. 28
C. 32
D. 16

**Answer:** B. 28
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 28 (Correct):** Replacing the signs gives 48 ÷ 6 × 4 − 12 + 8. By BODMAS: 8 × 4 − 12 + 8 = 32 − 12 + 8 = 28.
- **A — 24:** Obtained by subtracting 8 instead of adding 8 at the final step (32 − 12 − 4).
- **C — 32:** Obtained if 12 and 8 are ignored or miscalculated as 32 − 0.
- **D — 16:** Obtained by mistakenly doing 32 − (12 + 4) or misapplying subtraction before addition.
- **Exam Trick:** Always rewrite the entire expression with the new symbols first before applying BODMAS (Brackets, Orders, Division, Multiplication, Addition, Subtraction).

### Q682 · Mathematical Operations

If '+' denotes '×', '−' denotes '+', '×' denotes '÷', and '÷' denotes '−', find the value of: 72 × 9 − 14 + 3 ÷ 18.

A. 32
B. 36
C. 28
D. 40

**Answer:** A. 32
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 32 (Correct):** Substituting the operators yields 72 ÷ 9 + 14 × 3 − 18. Applying BODMAS: 8 + 42 − 18 = 50 − 18 = 32.
- **B — 36:** Results from an arithmetic slip when subtracting 18 from 54 instead of 50.
- **C — 28:** Results from subtracting 22 instead of 18, or miscomputing 72 ÷ 9 as 4.
- **D — 40:** Results from adding 14 × 3 as 48 instead of 42 (8 + 48 − 16).
- **Exam Trick:** Division and multiplication have equal precedence and are evaluated left to right before addition and subtraction.

### Q683 · Mathematical Operations

If '+' means '−', '−' means '÷', '×' means '+', and '÷' means '×', then what is the value of (36 − 4) × 15 ÷ 3 + 10?

A. 38
B. 42
C. 44
D. 54

**Answer:** C. 44
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 44 (Correct):** After replacing signs: (36 ÷ 4) + 15 × 3 − 10 = 9 + 45 − 10 = 44.
- **A — 38:** Obtained if 15 × 3 is misread or 16 is subtracted instead of 10.
- **B — 42:** Obtained by miscalculating 36 ÷ 4 as 7 instead of 9.
- **D — 54:** Obtained by forgetting to subtract 10 at the end (9 + 45 = 54).
- **Exam Trick:** Brackets are always evaluated first in BODMAS, even after symbol substitution.

### Q684 · Mathematical Operations

If 'A' stands for '+', 'B' stands for '−', 'C' stands for '×', and 'D' stands for '÷', what is the value of: 25 C 4 B 84 D 7 A 12?

A. 88
B. 96
C. 104
D. 100

**Answer:** D. 100
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 100 (Correct):** Converting letters to signs: 25 × 4 − 84 ÷ 7 + 12 = 100 − 12 + 12 = 100.
- **A — 88:** Obtained by subtracting 12 instead of adding it at the end (100 − 12 = 88).
- **B — 96:** Obtained if 84 ÷ 7 is miscalculated as 16 (100 − 16 + 12 = 96).
- **C — 104:** Obtained if 84 ÷ 7 is miscalculated as 8 (100 − 8 + 12 = 104).
- **Exam Trick:** Notice that '− 12 + 12' cancels out to 0, saving calculation time in exams.

### Q685 · Mathematical Operations

If 'P' means '÷', 'Q' means '×', 'R' means '+', and 'S' means '−', then 18 Q 5 R 56 P 8 S 19 = ?

A. 78
B. 74
C. 82
D. 68

**Answer:** A. 78
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 78 (Correct):** Replacing the letters: 18 × 5 + 56 ÷ 8 − 19 = 90 + 7 − 19 = 78.
- **B — 74:** Results from miscalculating 18 × 5 as 86 or subtracting 23 instead of 19.
- **C — 82:** Results from miscalculating 56 ÷ 8 as 11 (90 + 11 − 19 = 82).
- **D — 68:** Results from an arithmetic error of 10 when subtracting 19 from 97.
- **Exam Trick:** Write each letter-to-sign mapping in a quick 2-line legend on your rough sheet to avoid mix-ups.

### Q686 · Mathematical Operations

If 'L' denotes '+', 'M' denotes '−', 'N' denotes '×', and 'P' denotes '÷', what is the value of: (45 P 5) N 6 L 14 M 8?

A. 56
B. 60
C. 64
D. 52

**Answer:** B. 60
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 60 (Correct):** Substituting symbols: (45 ÷ 5) × 6 + 14 − 8 = 9 × 6 + 14 − 8 = 54 + 14 − 8 = 60.
- **A — 56:** Obtained if 14 − 8 is miscalculated as 2 (54 + 2 = 56).
- **C — 64:** Obtained by adding 14 − 4 instead of 14 − 8 (54 + 10 = 64).
- **D — 52:** Obtained if 45 ÷ 5 is taken as 8 (48 + 14 − 8 = 54, or similar slip).
- **Exam Trick:** Evaluating '+ 14 − 8' as '+ 6' directly speeds up the final step.

### Q687 · Mathematical Operations

If '÷' means '+', '−' means '÷', '×' means '−', and '+' means '×', what is the value of: 64 − 8 + 5 × 12 ÷ 7?

A. 21
B. 30
C. 35
D. 45

**Answer:** C. 35
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 35 (Correct):** After substitution: 64 ÷ 8 × 5 − 12 + 7 = 8 × 5 − 12 + 7 = 40 − 12 + 7 = 35.
- **A — 21:** Obtained by wrongly grouping 12 + 7 as 19 and doing 40 − 19 = 21.
- **B — 30:** Obtained by subtracting 5 more than required (40 − 10 = 30).
- **D — 45:** Obtained by adding 12 − 7 instead of subtracting 12 and adding 7 (40 + 5 = 45).
- **Exam Trick:** A classic BODMAS trap is treating '− 12 + 7' as '− (12 + 7)'; always treat signs as attached to the number immediately following them.

### Q688 · Mathematical Operations

If '+' means '×', '−' means '÷', '×' means '−', and '÷' means '+', then 16 + 4 ÷ 36 − 6 × 10 = ?

A. 58
B. 64
C. 54
D. 60

**Answer:** D. 60
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 60 (Correct):** Substituting the signs gives 16 × 4 + 36 ÷ 6 − 10 = 64 + 6 − 10 = 60.
- **A — 58:** Results from miscalculating 36 ÷ 6 as 4 (64 + 4 − 10 = 58).
- **B — 64:** Results from forgetting to subtract 10 after 64 + 6, or subtracting 6.
- **C — 54:** Results from subtracting 16 instead of 10 (70 − 16 = 54).
- **Exam Trick:** Verify that division produces an integer early on—in SSC exams, valid substitutions almost always yield whole-number quotients.

### Q689 · Mathematical Operations

If '@' means '×', '#' means '+', '$' means '÷', and '%' means '−', what is the value of: 14 @ 3 % 48 $ 4 # 9?

A. 39
B. 21
C. 33
D. 45

**Answer:** A. 39
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 39 (Correct):** Replacing the symbols: 14 × 3 − 48 ÷ 4 + 9 = 42 − 12 + 9 = 39.
- **B — 21:** Obtained by grouping 12 + 9 as 21 and computing 42 − 21 = 21.
- **C — 33:** Obtained by miscalculating 48 ÷ 4 as 18 (42 − 18 + 9 = 33).
- **D — 45:** Obtained by adding 12 instead of subtracting it, or miscomputing 14 × 3 as 48.
- **Exam Trick:** Special-symbol questions (@, #, $, %) follow the exact same BODMAS order as standard operator substitutions.

### Q690 · Mathematical Operations

If 'A' denotes '+', 'B' denotes '−', 'C' denotes '×', and 'D' denotes '÷', evaluate: 15 C 3 A 24 D 6 B 9.

A. 36
B. 40
C. 44
D. 48

**Answer:** B. 40
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 40 (Correct):** Substituting the letters gives 15 × 3 + 24 ÷ 6 − 9 = 45 + 4 − 9 = 40.
- **A — 36:** Obtained if 24 ÷ 6 is miscalculated as 0 or 15 × 3 as 41.
- **C — 44:** Obtained if 9 is subtracted as 5 (49 − 5 = 44).
- **D — 48:** Obtained by adding 9 instead of subtracting it (49 − 1 = 48).
- **Exam Trick:** Double-check multiplication of two-digit numbers by single digits (15 × 3 = 45) before proceeding.

### Q691 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the given equation correct? 36 + 6 × 4 − 15 ÷ 3 = 12

A. + and −
B. × and ÷
C. + and ÷
D. − and ×

**Answer:** C. + and ÷
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — + and ÷ (Correct):** Interchanging '+' and '÷' gives: 36 ÷ 6 × 4 − 15 + 3 = 6 × 4 − 15 + 3 = 24 − 15 + 3 = 12.
- **A — + and −:** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives 36 − 6 × 4 + 15 ÷ 3 = 36 − 24 + 5 = 17, not 12.
- **B — × and ÷:** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives 36 + 6 ÷ 4 × 15 − 3 = 36 + 22.5 − 3 = 55.5, not 12.
- **D — − and ×:** Interchanging '−' and '×' gives 36 + 6 − 4 × 15 ÷ 3 = 42 − 20 = 22, not 12.
- **Exam Trick:** In sign-interchange questions, test swaps involving '÷' first, especially where the current division doesn't divide cleanly or where a larger number needs to be reduced.

### Q692 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the following equation correct? 45 − 9 × 3 + 12 ÷ 4 = 23

A. + and −
B. × and ÷
C. + and ÷
D. − and ÷

**Answer:** D. − and ÷
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — − and ÷ (Correct):** Interchanging '−' and '÷' gives: 45 ÷ 9 × 3 + 12 − 4 = 5 × 3 + 12 − 4 = 15 + 12 − 4 = 23.
- **A — + and −:** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives 45 + 9 × 3 − 12 ÷ 4 = 45 + 27 − 3 = 69, not 23.
- **B — × and ÷:** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives 45 − 9 ÷ 3 + 12 × 4 = 45 − 3 + 48 = 90, not 23.
- **C — + and ÷:** Interchanging '+' and '÷' gives 45 − 9 × 3 ÷ 12 + 4 = 45 − 2.25 + 4 = 46.75, not 23.
- **Exam Trick:** Use divisibility as a quick filter: '9 × 3 ÷ 12' produces a fraction, so '+ and ÷' can be eliminated immediately.

### Q693 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs need to be interchanged to make the equation true? 16 + 4 × 8 − 24 ÷ 6 = 22

A. × and ÷
B. + and −
C. + and ×
D. − and ÷

**Answer:** A. × and ÷
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — × and ÷ (Correct):** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives: 16 + 4 ÷ 8 × 24 − 6 = 16 + (4/8) × 24 − 6 = 16 + 12 − 6 = 22.
- **B — + and −:** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives 16 − 4 × 8 + 24 ÷ 6 = 16 − 32 + 4 = −12, not 22.
- **C — + and ×:** Interchanging '+' and '×' gives 16 × 4 + 8 − 24 ÷ 6 = 64 + 8 − 4 = 68, not 22.
- **D — − and ÷:** Interchanging '−' and '÷' gives 16 + 4 × 8 ÷ 24 − 6 = 16 + 4/3 − 6, which is not an integer.
- **Exam Trick:** Even when '4 ÷ 8' looks like a fraction, multiplying by 24 right after turns (4/8) × 24 into the whole number 12.

### Q694 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to balance the equation: 15 − 3 × 4 + 20 ÷ 5 = 23?

A. + and ×
B. + and −
C. × and ÷
D. − and ÷

**Answer:** B. + and −
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — + and − (Correct):** Interchanging '+' and '−' yields: 15 + 3 × 4 − 20 ÷ 5 = 15 + 12 − 4 = 23.
- **A — + and ×:** Interchanging '+' and '×' gives 15 − 3 + 4 × 20 ÷ 5 = 12 + 16 = 28, not 23.
- **C — × and ÷:** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives 15 − 3 ÷ 4 × 20 + 5 = 15 − 15 + 5 = 5, not 23.
- **D — − and ÷:** Interchanging '−' and '÷' gives 15 ÷ 3 × 4 + 20 − 5 = 20 + 20 − 5 = 35, not 23.
- **Exam Trick:** When the original expression evaluates close to the target (here 15 − 12 + 4 = 7 vs 23), swapping '+' and '−' often flips a large negative term into a positive one.

### Q695 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the following equation correct? 8 × 6 + 24 ÷ 4 − 10 = 26

A. × and +
B. + and −
C. × and ÷
D. ÷ and −

**Answer:** C. × and ÷
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — × and ÷ (Correct):** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives: 8 ÷ 6 × 24 + 4 − 10 = (8/6) × 24 + 4 − 10 = 32 + 4 − 10 = 26.
- **A — × and +:** Interchanging '×' and '+' gives 8 + 6 × 24 ÷ 4 − 10 = 8 + 36 − 10 = 34, not 26.
- **B — + and −:** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives 8 × 6 − 24 ÷ 4 + 10 = 48 − 6 + 10 = 52, not 26.
- **D — ÷ and −:** Interchanging '÷' and '−' gives 8 × 6 + 24 − 4 ÷ 10 = 48 + 24 − 0.4 = 71.6, not 26.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check 'a ÷ b × c' as '(a × c) ÷ b' before dismissing a division that doesn't divide evenly on its own.

### Q696 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the given equation correct? 42 ÷ 6 − 5 + 3 × 7 = 31

A. + and ×
B. + and −
C. ÷ and +
D. − and ×

**Answer:** D. − and ×
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — − and × (Correct):** Interchanging '−' and '×' gives: 42 ÷ 6 × 5 + 3 − 7 = 7 × 5 + 3 − 7 = 35 + 3 − 7 = 31.
- **A — + and ×:** Interchanging '+' and '×' gives 42 ÷ 6 − 5 × 3 + 7 = 7 − 15 + 7 = −1, not 31.
- **B — + and −:** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives 42 ÷ 6 + 5 − 3 × 7 = 7 + 5 − 21 = −9, not 31.
- **C — ÷ and +:** Interchanging '÷' and '+' gives 42 + 6 − 5 ÷ 3 × 7, which leaves a non-integer 35/3.
- **Exam Trick:** Notice that '42 ÷ 6' already divides cleanly to 7, so swaps that leave '÷' intact are strong candidates.

### Q697 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the equation correct? 12 × 3 + 18 ÷ 2 − 9 = 30

A. + and ×
B. + and ÷
C. × and ÷
D. ÷ and −

**Answer:** A. + and ×
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — + and × (Correct):** Interchanging '+' and '×' gives: 12 + 3 × 18 ÷ 2 − 9 = 12 + 27 − 9 = 30.
- **B — + and ÷:** Interchanging '+' and '÷' gives 12 × 3 ÷ 18 + 2 − 9 = 2 + 2 − 9 = −5, not 30.
- **C — × and ÷:** Interchanging '×' and '÷' gives 12 ÷ 3 + 18 × 2 − 9 = 4 + 36 − 9 = 31, not 30.
- **D — ÷ and −:** Interchanging '÷' and '−' gives 12 × 3 + 18 − 2 ÷ 9, which produces a fraction.
- **Exam Trick:** Test each option systematically using BODMAS; eliminating fractional results first saves time.

### Q698 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs should be interchanged to make the following equation correct? 54 ÷ 9 + 7 × 2 − 8 = 0

A. + and ×
B. + and −
C. × and −
D. ÷ and −

**Answer:** B. + and −
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — + and − (Correct):** Interchanging '+' and '−' gives: 54 ÷ 9 − 7 × 2 + 8 = 6 − 14 + 8 = 0.
- **A — + and ×:** Interchanging '+' and '×' gives 54 ÷ 9 × 7 + 2 − 8 = 42 + 2 − 8 = 36, not 0.
- **C — × and −:** Interchanging '×' and '−' gives 54 ÷ 9 + 7 − 2 × 8 = 6 + 7 − 16 = −3, not 0.
- **D — ÷ and −:** Interchanging '÷' and '−' gives 54 − 9 + 7 × 2 ÷ 8 = 45 + 1.75 = 46.75, not 0.
- **Exam Trick:** Zero on the right-hand side means positive and negative terms must cancel out completely (6 + 8 = 14).

### Q699 · Mathematical Operations

Which two numbers (not digits) should be interchanged to make the given equation correct? 12 + 18 ÷ 3 × 2 − 6 = 20

A. 12 and 6
B. 3 and 2
C. 12 and 18
D. 18 and 6

**Answer:** C. 12 and 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 12 and 18 (Correct):** Interchanging 12 and 18 gives: 18 + 12 ÷ 3 × 2 − 6 = 18 + 4 × 2 − 6 = 18 + 8 − 6 = 20.
- **A — 12 and 6:** Interchanging 12 and 6 gives 6 + 18 ÷ 3 × 2 − 12 = 6 + 12 − 12 = 6, not 20.
- **B — 3 and 2:** Interchanging 3 and 2 gives 12 + 18 ÷ 2 × 3 − 6 = 12 + 27 − 6 = 33, not 20.
- **D — 18 and 6:** Interchanging 18 and 6 gives 12 + 6 ÷ 3 × 2 − 18 = 12 + 4 − 18 = −2, not 20.
- **Exam Trick:** In number-interchange problems, note the instruction 'not digits': you must swap the whole number (e.g., 12 with 18), not individual digits.

### Q700 · Mathematical Operations

Which two numbers should be interchanged to make the equation correct? 36 ÷ 6 + 5 × 4 − 9 = 30

A. 5 and 9
B. 4 and 9
C. 6 and 9
D. 6 and 4

**Answer:** D. 6 and 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 6 and 4 (Correct):** Interchanging 6 and 4 gives: 36 ÷ 4 + 5 × 6 − 9 = 9 + 30 − 9 = 30.
- **A — 5 and 9:** Interchanging 5 and 9 gives 36 ÷ 6 + 9 × 4 − 5 = 6 + 36 − 5 = 37, not 30.
- **B — 4 and 9:** Interchanging 4 and 9 gives 36 ÷ 6 + 5 × 9 − 4 = 6 + 45 − 4 = 47, not 30.
- **C — 6 and 9:** Interchanging 6 and 9 gives 36 ÷ 9 + 5 × 4 − 6 = 4 + 20 − 6 = 18, not 30.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check whether the new divisor divides the dividend cleanly—here both 4 and 9 divide 36, so both must be evaluated.

### Q701 · Mathematical Operations

Which two numbers should be interchanged to balance the given equation? 14 × 3 − 24 ÷ 6 + 8 = 34

A. 14 and 8
B. 3 and 6
C. 3 and 8
D. 14 and 24

**Answer:** A. 14 and 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 14 and 8 (Correct):** Interchanging 14 and 8 gives: 8 × 3 − 24 ÷ 6 + 14 = 24 − 4 + 14 = 34.
- **B — 3 and 6:** Interchanging 3 and 6 gives 14 × 6 − 24 ÷ 3 + 8 = 84 − 8 + 8 = 84, not 34.
- **C — 3 and 8:** Interchanging 3 and 8 gives 14 × 8 − 24 ÷ 6 + 3 = 112 − 4 + 3 = 111, not 34.
- **D — 14 and 24:** Interchanging 14 and 24 gives 24 × 3 − 14 ÷ 6 + 8, which produces a fraction (14 ÷ 6).
- **Exam Trick:** Look at the size of the product: 14 × 3 = 42 is already larger than 34, so reducing 14 to 8 is a natural first check.

### Q702 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs and two numbers should be interchanged to make the equation correct? 20 − 6 × 2 + 18 ÷ 3 = 23

A. + and −; 2 and 3
B. + and −; 6 and 3
C. × and ÷; 6 and 18
D. − and ×; 2 and 6

**Answer:** B. + and −; 6 and 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — + and −; 6 and 3 (Correct):** Interchanging '+' with '−' and 6 with 3 gives: 20 + 3 × 2 − 18 ÷ 6 = 20 + 6 − 3 = 23.
- **A — + and −; 2 and 3:** Interchanging '+' with '−' and 2 with 3 gives 20 + 6 × 3 − 18 ÷ 2 = 20 + 18 − 9 = 29, not 23.
- **C — × and ÷; 6 and 18:** Interchanging '×' with '÷' and 6 with 18 gives 20 − 18 ÷ 2 + 6 × 3 = 20 − 9 + 18 = 29, not 23.
- **D — − and ×; 2 and 6:** Interchanging '−' with '×' and 2 with 6 gives 20 × 2 − 6 + 18 ÷ 3 = 40 − 6 + 6 = 40, not 23.
- **Exam Trick:** When both signs and numbers are swapped, apply both swaps simultaneously in one rewrite before evaluating.

### Q703 · Mathematical Operations

Which two signs and two numbers should be interchanged to make the equation correct? 18 + 24 ÷ 4 × 3 − 12 = 12

A. + and −; 4 and 3
B. × and ÷; 4 and 3
C. + and −; 18 and 12
D. − and ×; 12 and 3

**Answer:** C. + and −; 18 and 12
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — + and −; 18 and 12 (Correct):** Interchanging '+' with '−' and 18 with 12 gives: 12 − 24 ÷ 4 × 3 + 18 = 12 − 18 + 18 = 12.
- **A — + and −; 4 and 3:** Interchanging '+' with '−' and 4 with 3 gives 18 − 24 ÷ 3 × 4 + 12 = 18 − 32 + 12 = −2, not 12.
- **B — × and ÷; 4 and 3:** Interchanging '×' with '÷' and 4 with 3 gives 18 + 24 × 3 ÷ 4 − 12 = 18 + 18 − 12 = 24, not 12.
- **D — − and ×; 12 and 3:** Interchanging '−' with '×' and 12 with 3 gives 18 + 24 ÷ 4 − 12 × 3 = 18 + 6 − 36 = −12, not 12.
- **Exam Trick:** Always verify that the original equation is NOT already balanced before selecting an identity-preserving swap.

### Q704 · Mathematical Operations

Select the correct combination of mathematical signs that can sequentially replace the '*' signs and balance the equation: 42 * 7 * 5 * 12 * 18

A. ×, ÷, −, =
B. ÷, ×, +, =
C. −, +, ×, =
D. ÷, ×, −, =

**Answer:** D. ÷, ×, −, =
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — ÷, ×, −, = (Correct):** Replacing '*' with '÷, ×, −, =' gives: 42 ÷ 7 × 5 − 12 = 18, which evaluates to 6 × 5 − 12 = 30 − 12 = 18.
- **A — ×, ÷, −, =:** Using '×, ÷, −, =' gives 42 × 7 ÷ 5 − 12 = 58.8 − 12 = 46.8, not 18.
- **B — ÷, ×, +, =:** Using '÷, ×, +, =' gives 42 ÷ 7 × 5 + 12 = 30 + 12 = 42, not 18.
- **C — −, +, ×, =:** Using '−, +, ×, =' gives 42 − 7 + 5 × 12 = 35 + 60 = 95, not 18.
- **Exam Trick:** Check where '=' sits (usually last) and look at the first two numbers (42 and 7): 42 ÷ 7 = 6 is a strong clue that '÷' comes first.

### Q705 · Mathematical Operations

Select the correct combination of mathematical signs to sequentially replace the '*' signs and balance the equation: 15 * 3 * 24 * 6 * 49

A. ×, +, ÷, =
B. ÷, +, ×, =
C. ×, −, ÷, =
D. +, ×, ÷, =

**Answer:** A. ×, +, ÷, =
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — ×, +, ÷, = (Correct):** Replacing '*' with '×, +, ÷, =' gives: 15 × 3 + 24 ÷ 6 = 49, which evaluates to 45 + 4 = 49.
- **B — ÷, +, ×, =:** Using '÷, +, ×, =' gives 15 ÷ 3 + 24 × 6 = 5 + 144 = 149, not 49.
- **C — ×, −, ÷, =:** Using '×, −, ÷, =' gives 15 × 3 − 24 ÷ 6 = 45 − 4 = 41, not 49.
- **D — +, ×, ÷, =:** Using '+, ×, ÷, =' gives 15 + 3 × 24 ÷ 6 = 15 + 12 = 27, not 49.
- **Exam Trick:** Estimate the magnitude of the target (49): 15 × 3 = 45 gets us right near 49, and 24 ÷ 6 = 4 bridges the exact gap.

### Q706 · Mathematical Operations

Which sequence of mathematical signs should replace the '*' signs to balance the equation: 64 * 8 * 12 * 4 * 56?

A. ÷, −, ×, =
B. ÷, +, ×, =
C. ×, ÷, +, =
D. +, ÷, ×, =

**Answer:** B. ÷, +, ×, =
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — ÷, +, ×, = (Correct):** Replacing '*' with '÷, +, ×, =' gives: 64 ÷ 8 + 12 × 4 = 56, which evaluates to 8 + 48 = 56.
- **A — ÷, −, ×, =:** Using '÷, −, ×, =' gives 64 ÷ 8 − 12 × 4 = 8 − 48 = −40, not 56.
- **C — ×, ÷, +, =:** Using '×, ÷, +, =' gives 64 × 8 ÷ 12 + 4 = 42.67 + 4 = 46.67, not 56.
- **D — +, ÷, ×, =:** Using '+, ÷, ×, =' gives 64 + 8 ÷ 12 × 4 = 64 + 2.67 = 66.67, not 56.
- **Exam Trick:** Since 64 is divisible by 8 and 12 × 4 = 48, placing '÷' first and '×' third immediately reaches 56.

### Q707 · Mathematical Operations

If 5 # 3 = 17, 7 # 4 = 31, and 9 # 6 = 57, then what is the value of 8 # 5?

A. 37
B. 41
C. 43
D. 45

**Answer:** C. 43
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 43 (Correct):** The pattern is A # B = (A × B) + (A − B). Thus 5 × 3 + 2 = 17, 7 × 4 + 3 = 31, 9 × 6 + 3 = 57, and 8 # 5 = 8 × 5 + (8 − 5) = 40 + 3 = 43.
- **A — 37:** Obtained by subtracting (A − B) instead of adding it (40 − 3 = 37).
- **B — 41:** Obtained by adding 1 instead of (A − B) = 3 (40 + 1 = 41).
- **D — 45:** Obtained by adding (8 − 3) = 5 instead of (8 − 5) = 3.
- **Exam Trick:** For custom operator puzzles like A # B, test combinations of A × B, A + B, A − B, and squares A² ± B².

### Q708 · Mathematical Operations

If '+' and '−' are interchanged, and '×' and '÷' are interchanged, which of the following equations becomes true?

A. 12 + 4 × 2 − 6 ÷ 3 = 8
B. 15 × 3 + 6 − 8 ÷ 2 = 17
C. 24 − 6 ÷ 2 + 10 × 5 = 30
D. 18 ÷ 2 − 10 × 5 + 4 = 34

**Answer:** D. 18 ÷ 2 − 10 × 5 + 4 = 34
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 18 ÷ 2 − 10 × 5 + 4 = 34 (Correct):** Applying the interchanges to 18 ÷ 2 − 10 × 5 + 4 = 34 gives 18 × 2 + 10 ÷ 5 − 4 = 36 + 2 − 4 = 34.
- **A — 12 + 4 × 2 − 6 ÷ 3 = 8:** After interchanging, 12 − 4 ÷ 2 + 6 × 3 = 12 − 2 + 18 = 28, not 8.
- **B — 15 × 3 + 6 − 8 ÷ 2 = 17:** After interchanging, 15 ÷ 3 − 6 + 8 × 2 = 5 − 6 + 16 = 15, not 17.
- **C — 24 − 6 ÷ 2 + 10 × 5 = 30:** After interchanging, 24 + 6 × 2 − 10 ÷ 5 = 24 + 12 − 2 = 34, not 30.
- **Exam Trick:** In 'which equation is true' questions, scan the '×' positions first (which become '÷') to see which ones divide cleanly.

### Q709 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the third triplet following the same pattern: (7, 9, 32), (11, 13, 48), (15, 17, ?)

A. 64
B. 60
C. 68
D. 56

**Answer:** A. 64
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 64 (Correct):** In each triplet (A, B, C), C = (A + B) × 2. Thus (7 + 9) × 2 = 32, (11 + 13) × 2 = 48, and (15 + 17) × 2 = 32 × 2 = 64.
- **B — 60:** Obtained by miscalculating 15 + 17 as 30 instead of 32.
- **C — 68:** Obtained by adding 4 instead of multiplying by 2 after summing (or miscomputing 32 × 2 + 4).
- **D — 56:** Obtained by taking (15 + 13) × 2 = 56 instead of (15 + 17) × 2.
- **Exam Trick:** In triplet puzzles where the third number is roughly twice the sum of the first two, test C = k(A + B) immediately.

### Q710 · Missing Numbers

Select the number that replaces the question mark: (4, 7, 23), (6, 9, 45), (8, 11, ?)

A. 72
B. 75
C. 79
D. 83

**Answer:** B. 75
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 75 (Correct):** The rule is C = A² + B: 4² + 7 = 23, 6² + 9 = 45, and 8² + 11 = 64 + 11 = 75.
- **A — 72:** Obtained by computing 8² + 8 = 72 instead of adding B = 11.
- **C — 79:** Obtained by computing 8² + 15 = 79 or miscalculating 8² as 68.
- **D — 83:** Obtained by computing 9² + 2 or adding 8 instead of subtracting in a wrong formula.
- **Exam Trick:** When the third number grows quadratically compared to the first, check A² ± B or B² ± A.

### Q711 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the pattern: (9, 5, 56), (11, 7, 72), (13, 8, ?)

A. 95
B. 101
C. 105
D. 113

**Answer:** C. 105
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 105 (Correct):** Each triplet follows C = A² − B²: 9² − 5² = 81 − 25 = 56, 11² − 7² = 121 − 49 = 72, and 13² − 8² = 169 − 64 = 105.
- **A — 95:** Obtained by miscalculating 13² − 8² as 159 − 64 = 95.
- **B — 101:** Obtained by subtracting 68 instead of 64 (169 − 68 = 101).
- **D — 113:** Obtained by miscalculating 8² as 56 (169 − 56 = 113).
- **Exam Trick:** Remember the identity A² − B² = (A + B)(A − B): here (13 + 8)(13 − 8) = 21 × 5 = 105, which is faster than squaring.

### Q712 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number: (5, 4, 11), (7, 6, 29), (9, 8, ?)

A. 51
B. 53
C. 59
D. 55

**Answer:** D. 55
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 55 (Correct):** The pattern is C = (A × B) − (A + B): 5×4 − 9 = 11, 7×6 − 13 = 29, and 9×8 − (9 + 8) = 72 − 17 = 55.
- **A — 51:** Obtained by subtracting 21 instead of 17 from 72.
- **B — 53:** Obtained by subtracting 19 instead of 17 from 72.
- **C — 59:** Obtained by subtracting 13 (from the previous triplet) instead of 17.
- **Exam Trick:** Notice that (A × B) − (A + B) is equivalent to (A − 1)(B − 1) − 1: here 8 × 7 − 1 = 55.

### Q713 · Missing Numbers

Select the missing number: (6, 10, 24), (8, 12, 30), (14, 18, ?)

A. 48
B. 44
C. 52
D. 42

**Answer:** A. 48
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 48 (Correct):** In each triplet, C = [(A + B) ÷ 2] × 3. Thus [(6 + 10) ÷ 2] × 3 = 24, [(8 + 12) ÷ 2] × 3 = 30, and [(14 + 18) ÷ 2] × 3 = 16 × 3 = 48.
- **B — 44:** Obtained by adding 12 to 32 instead of taking 1.5 times the sum.
- **C — 52:** Obtained by miscalculating 16 × 3 as 52.
- **D — 42:** Obtained by computing 14 × 3 = 42 instead of 16 × 3.
- **Exam Trick:** When the third number is 1.5 times (A + B), check [(A + B) ÷ 2] × 3.

### Q714 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the third triplet: (7, 4, 27), (9, 5, 64), (12, 7, ?)

A. 100
B. 125
C. 144
D. 216

**Answer:** B. 125
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 125 (Correct):** The pattern is C = (A − B)³: (7 − 4)³ = 3³ = 27, (9 − 5)³ = 4³ = 64, and (12 − 7)³ = 5³ = 125.
- **A — 100:** Obtained by computing 10² = 100 or misapplying a square instead of a cube.
- **C — 144:** Obtained by computing 12² = 144 instead of (12 − 7)³.
- **D — 216:** Obtained by cubing 6 instead of 5 (6³ = 216).
- **Exam Trick:** Recognizing perfect cubes (27, 64, 125, 216) immediately points to cubing the sum or difference of the first two numbers.

### Q715 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number: (5, 6, 28), (8, 4, 28), (9, 7, ?)

A. 35
B. 37
C. 39
D. 41

**Answer:** C. 39
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 39 (Correct):** Each triplet follows C = 2A + 3B: 2(5) + 3(6) = 28, 2(8) + 3(4) = 28, and 2(9) + 3(7) = 18 + 21 = 39.
- **A — 35:** Obtained by computing 2(9) + 2(7) + 3 = 35 or swapping the multipliers (3×9 + 2×7 = 41, minus 6).
- **B — 37:** Obtained by an addition slip (18 + 19 = 37).
- **D — 41:** Obtained by reversing the weights to 3A + 2B = 3(9) + 2(7) = 41.
- **Exam Trick:** When two different pairs give the same result (28 here), test a weighted linear combination like mA + nB.

### Q716 · Missing Numbers

Select the number that can replace the question mark: (4, 3, 21), (5, 4, 31), (6, 5, ?)

A. 39
B. 41
C. 45
D. 43

**Answer:** D. 43
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 43 (Correct):** The rule is C = 3A + B²: 3(4) + 3² = 12 + 9 = 21, 3(5) + 4² = 15 + 16 = 31, and 3(6) + 5² = 18 + 25 = 43.
- **A — 39:** Obtained by computing 3(6) + 21 = 39 instead of 3(6) + 25.
- **B — 41:** Obtained by computing A² + B = 36 + 5 = 41 (which fails for the first two triplets).
- **C — 45:** Obtained by computing 4(5) + 5² = 45 or misadding 18 + 25.
- **Exam Trick:** Check differences between consecutive triplets: 21 → 31 (+10), 31 → 43 (+12) also confirms the steady quadratic growth.

### Q717 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number: (3, 5, 17), (4, 6, 26), (5, 7, ?)

A. 37
B. 35
C. 39
D. 41

**Answer:** A. 37
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 37 (Correct):** Each triplet follows C = (A² + B²) ÷ 2: (9 + 25) ÷ 2 = 17, (16 + 36) ÷ 2 = 26, and (25 + 49) ÷ 2 = 74 ÷ 2 = 37.
- **B — 35:** Obtained by computing 5 × 7 = 35 without applying the squares-average rule.
- **C — 39:** Obtained by miscalculating 74 ÷ 2 as 39.
- **D — 41:** Obtained by adding 4 instead of dividing by 2 properly, or computing (5² + 7²) ÷ 2 + 4.
- **Exam Trick:** Notice also that A × B + 2 gives 3×5+2=17, 4×6+2=26, and 5×7+2=37—both valid patterns yield the exact same answer 37!

### Q718 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the third triplet: (9, 4, 12), (12, 5, 20), (15, 7, ?)

A. 30
B. 35
C. 40
D. 45

**Answer:** B. 35
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 35 (Correct):** The pattern is C = (A × B) ÷ 3: (9 × 4) ÷ 3 = 12, (12 × 5) ÷ 3 = 20, and (15 × 7) ÷ 3 = 5 × 7 = 35.
- **A — 30:** Obtained by computing 15 × 6 ÷ 3 = 30 instead of using B = 7.
- **C — 40:** Obtained by computing (15 + 7) × 2 − 4 = 40 or miscalculating 5 × 7.
- **D — 45:** Obtained by multiplying 15 × 3 = 45 instead of 5 × 7.
- **Exam Trick:** Whenever A is a multiple of 3 (9, 12, 15), check whether C = (A ÷ 3) × B.

### Q719 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the 3×3 matrix — Row 1: 14, 19, 27 | Row 2: 22, 15, 23 | Row 3: 18, 26, ?

A. 12
B. 14
C. 16
D. 18

**Answer:** C. 16
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 16 (Correct):** The sum of numbers in each row is 60: 14 + 19 + 27 = 60 and 22 + 15 + 23 = 60. Thus 18 + 26 + ? = 60, giving ? = 60 − 44 = 16.
- **A — 12:** Obtained by subtracting 48 instead of 44 from 60.
- **B — 14:** Obtained by miscalculating 18 + 26 as 46 (60 − 46 = 14).
- **D — 18:** Obtained by repeating 18 from the first column instead of balancing the row sum.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check the row sum and column sum first in 3×3 matrix puzzles—it takes 5 seconds and solves many CGL/CHSL questions directly.

### Q720 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the matrix — Row 1: 6, 9, 21 | Row 2: 8, 11, 27 | Row 3: 10, 13, ?

A. 31
B. 35
C. 29
D. 33

**Answer:** D. 33
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 33 (Correct):** In each row, (First number × 2) + Second number = Third number: 6×2 + 9 = 21, 8×2 + 11 = 27, and 10×2 + 13 = 20 + 13 = 33.
- **A — 31:** Obtained by adding 11 instead of 13 to 20 (20 + 11 = 31).
- **B — 35:** Obtained by adding 15 instead of 13 to 20 (20 + 15 = 35).
- **C — 29:** Obtained by computing 10 + 13 + 6 = 29.
- **Exam Trick:** Notice the column-wise arithmetic progression too: 21, 27, 33 (+6 each row).

### Q721 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the 3×3 matrix — Row 1: 3, 4, 5 | Row 2: 5, 7, 9 | Row 3: 14, 23, ?

A. 34
B. 32
C. 36
D. 30

**Answer:** A. 34
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 34 (Correct):** Column-wise, (Row 1)² + Row 2 = Row 3: 3² + 5 = 14, 4² + 7 = 23, and 5² + 9 = 25 + 9 = 34.
- **B — 32:** Obtained by adding 7 instead of 9 to 25 (25 + 7 = 32).
- **C — 36:** Obtained by adding 11 instead of 9 to 25 (25 + 11 = 36).
- **D — 30:** Obtained by computing 5² + 5 = 30 instead of adding Row 2 = 9.
- **Exam Trick:** When numbers in Row 3 are much larger than Rows 1 and 2, look for a column-wise operation producing Row 3.

### Q722 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the matrix — Row 1: 12, 18, 24 | Row 2: 15, 22, 29 | Row 3: 19, 25, ?

A. 29
B. 31
C. 33
D. 35

**Answer:** B. 31
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 31 (Correct):** In each row, the middle number is the average of the first and third numbers: (12 + 24)÷2 = 18, (15 + 29)÷2 = 22. Thus (19 + ?)÷2 = 25, so ? = 50 − 19 = 31.
- **A — 29:** Obtained by repeating 29 from the previous row instead of computing 50 − 19.
- **C — 33:** Obtained by miscalculating 50 − 19 as 33.
- **D — 35:** Obtained by adding 16 instead of 6 to 19 (19 + 16 = 35).
- **Exam Trick:** In an arithmetic-progression row (19, 25, ?), the step from 19 to 25 is +6, so the next term is 25 + 6 = 31.

### Q723 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number — Row 1: 4, 5, 16 | Row 2: 6, 7, 38 | Row 3: 8, 9, ?

A. 64
B. 66
C. 68
D. 70

**Answer:** C. 68
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 68 (Correct):** Each row follows (First × Second) − 4 = Third: 4×5 − 4 = 16, 6×7 − 4 = 38, and 8×9 − 4 = 72 − 4 = 68.
- **A — 64:** Obtained by subtracting 8 instead of 4 from 72 (72 − 8 = 64).
- **B — 66:** Obtained by subtracting 6 instead of 4 from 72 (72 − 6 = 66).
- **D — 70:** Obtained by subtracting 2 instead of 4 from 72 (72 − 2 = 70).
- **Exam Trick:** When the third number is slightly below the product of the first two, test (A × B) − constant.

### Q724 · Missing Numbers

Select the missing number in the 3×3 matrix — Row 1: 4, 5, 6 | Row 2: 6, 8, 10 | Row 3: 12, 20, ?

A. 24
B. 28
C. 32
D. 30

**Answer:** D. 30
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 30 (Correct):** Column-wise, (Row 1 × Row 2) ÷ 2 = Row 3: (4 × 6)÷2 = 12, (5 × 8)÷2 = 20, and (6 × 10)÷2 = 30.
- **A — 24:** Obtained by adding 6 + 10 + 8 = 24 or misapplying a linear difference.
- **B — 28:** Obtained by computing 12 + 20 − 4 = 28.
- **C — 32:** Obtained by miscalculating 60 ÷ 2 as 32.
- **Exam Trick:** Always check if Row 3 is half the product of Rows 1 and 2 when all entries in Row 2 are even.

### Q725 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the matrix — Row 1: 7, 13, 36 | Row 2: 8, 15, 49 | Row 3: 9, 17, ?

A. 64
B. 62
C. 66
D. 60

**Answer:** A. 64
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 64 (Correct):** In each row, (First)² − Second = Third: 7² − 13 = 36, 8² − 15 = 49, and 9² − 17 = 81 − 17 = 64.
- **B — 62:** Obtained by miscalculating 81 − 17 as 62.
- **C — 66:** Obtained by subtracting 15 instead of 17 from 81 (81 − 15 = 66).
- **D — 60:** Obtained by subtracting 21 instead of 17 from 81 (81 − 21 = 60).
- **Exam Trick:** Notice that the third column consists of consecutive perfect squares: 6² (36), 7² (49), 8² (64).

### Q726 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number — Row 1: 5, 8, 11 | Row 2: 7, 6, 9 | Row 3: 19, 20, ?

A. 27
B. 29
C. 31
D. 25

**Answer:** B. 29
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 29 (Correct):** Column-wise, Row 1 + (2 × Row 2) = Row 3: 5 + 2(7) = 19, 8 + 2(6) = 20, and 11 + 2(9) = 11 + 18 = 29.
- **A — 27:** Obtained by adding 11 + 16 = 27 instead of 11 + 18.
- **C — 31:** Obtained by adding 11 + 20 = 31 instead of 11 + 18.
- **D — 25:** Obtained by computing (11 + 9) + 5 = 25.
- **Exam Trick:** If Row 1 + Row 2 doesn't equal Row 3, test doubling one of the rows: Row 1 + 2×Row 2 or 2×Row 1 + Row 2.

### Q727 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number in the 3×3 matrix — Row 1: 14, 9, 25 | Row 2: 18, 11, 49 | Row 3: 23, 14, ?

A. 64
B. 72
C. 81
D. 100

**Answer:** C. 81
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 81 (Correct):** In each row, (First − Second)² = Third: (14 − 9)² = 5² = 25, (18 − 11)² = 7² = 49, and (23 − 14)² = 9² = 81.
- **A — 64:** Obtained by squaring 8 instead of 9 (8² = 64).
- **B — 72:** Obtained by multiplying 9 × 8 = 72 instead of squaring 9.
- **D — 100:** Obtained by squaring 10 instead of 9 (10² = 100).
- **Exam Trick:** When the third column shows perfect squares (25, 49), immediately check the difference or sum of the first two columns.

### Q728 · Missing Numbers

Find the missing number — Row 1: 3, 5, 7 | Row 2: 4, 6, 8 | Row 3: 16, 36, ?

A. 49
B. 56
C. 72
D. 64

**Answer:** D. 64
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 64 (Correct):** Column-wise, Row 3 = (Row 2)² (or equivalently Row 2 × (Row 1 + 1)): 4² = 16, 6² = 36, and 8² = 64.
- **A — 49:** Obtained by squaring Row 1 (7² = 49) instead of Row 2 (8² = 64).
- **B — 56:** Obtained by multiplying 7 × 8 = 56 instead of squaring 8.
- **C — 72:** Obtained by multiplying 8 × 9 = 72 instead of 8 × 8.
- **Exam Trick:** Look at Row 3 directly: 16 = 4², 36 = 6², so 8² = 64.

### Q729 · Missing Numbers

Select the missing number in the matrix — Row 1: 9, 7, 20 | Row 2: 12, 11, 25 | Row 3: 15, 14, ?

A. 31
B. 29
C. 33
D. 35

**Answer:** A. 31
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 31 (Correct):** Each row follows (First × 3) − Second = Third: 9×3 − 7 = 20, 12×3 − 11 = 25, and 15×3 − 14 = 45 − 14 = 31.
- **B — 29:** Obtained by subtracting 16 instead of 14 from 45 (45 − 16 = 29).
- **C — 33:** Obtained by subtracting 12 instead of 14 from 45 (45 − 12 = 33).
- **D — 35:** Obtained by computing 15×3 − 10 = 35.
- **Exam Trick:** Notice the third column also increases by +5 each row (20, 25, 31? Wait: 20, 25, 31 is +5, +6, so always verify the row-wise algebraic rule!).

### Q730 · Cube & Dice

Two positions of a single die are shown. Position 1 has 2 on top, 1 on the front, and 3 on the right. Position 2 has 2 on top, 6 on the front, and 4 on the right. Which number is on the face opposite to 1?

A. 4
B. 6
C. 5
D. 3

**Answer:** B. 6
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 6 (Correct):** Face 2 is common and in the exact same position (top) in both views. Therefore, corresponding faces are opposite each other: 1 (front) is opposite 6 (front), and 3 (right) is opposite 4 (right).
- **A — 4:** 4 is on the right face in Position 2, making it opposite to 3 (right face in Position 1).
- **C — 5:** 5 is the missing number, which is opposite to the common face 2.
- **D — 3:** 3 is adjacent to 1 in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to 1.
- **Exam Trick:** When two positions of a die have one common face in the same position, the remaining corresponding faces are directly opposite each other.

### Q731 · Cube & Dice

Two positions of a die are described: Position 1 has 1 on top, 2 on the front, and 3 on the right; Position 2 has 6 on top, 3 on the front, and 4 on the right. Which number is opposite to 3?

A. 1
B. 4
C. 5
D. 6

**Answer:** C. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 5 (Correct):** 3 is the only common face in both positions, and it is adjacent to 1, 2, 6, and 4. Since a die has 6 faces and 4 of them are adjacent to 3, the only remaining number—5—must be opposite to 3.
- **A — 1:** 1 is adjacent to 3 in Position 1 (1 is opposite 4).
- **B — 4:** 4 is adjacent to 3 in Position 2 (4 is opposite 1).
- **D — 6:** 6 is adjacent to 3 in Position 2 (6 is opposite 2).
- **Exam Trick:** To find the face opposite to the single common number in two views, you don't even need to rotate—it is simply the one number out of 1–6 that does not appear in either view!

### Q732 · Cube & Dice

In Position 1 of a die, the visible faces are 1 (top), 2 (front), and 3 (right). In Position 2, the visible faces are 5 (top), 3 (front), and 2 (right). Which number is opposite to 1?

A. 2
B. 3
C. 4
D. 5

**Answer:** D. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 5 (Correct):** Faces 2 and 3 are common to both positions. By the two-common-faces rule, the remaining two visible faces—1 and 5—must be opposite each other.
- **A — 2:** 2 is adjacent to 1 in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to 1.
- **B — 3:** 3 is adjacent to 1 in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to 1.
- **C — 4:** 4 is not visible in either position and is opposite either 2 or 3.
- **Exam Trick:** Two-common-faces rule: Whenever two faces appear in both views of a die, the third visible face in each view are guaranteed to be opposite each other.

### Q733 · Cube & Dice

Three positions of a cube marked with letters A, B, C, D, E, and F are shown: Position 1 shows A (top), B (front), C (right); Position 2 shows A (top), C (front), E (right); Position 3 shows D (top), E (front), C (right). Which letter is opposite to B?

A. E
B. D
C. F
D. A

**Answer:** A. E
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — E (Correct):** Comparing Position 1 (A, B, C) and Position 2 (A, C, E), letters A and C are common. Therefore, the remaining letters B and E are opposite each other.
- **B — D:** D is opposite to A (since C and E are common in Positions 2 and 3, leaving A opposite D).
- **C — F:** F is the remaining letter and is opposite to C.
- **D — A:** A is adjacent to B in Position 1, so A cannot be opposite to B.
- **Exam Trick:** With three positions given, always pick the pair of views that shares two common faces—it gives an opposite pair in one glance.

### Q734 · Cube & Dice

Three positions of a die are given: Position 1 has 1 (top), 2 (front), 3 (right); Position 2 has 1 (top), 3 (front), 4 (right); Position 3 has 6 (top), 4 (front), 3 (right). Which number is opposite to 3?

A. 1
B. 5
C. 6
D. 4

**Answer:** B. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 5 (Correct):** From Positions 1, 2, and 3, face 3 is adjacent to 1, 2, 4, and 6. The only number among 1–6 not adjacent to 3 is 5, so 5 is opposite to 3.
- **A — 1:** 1 is adjacent to 3 in Positions 1 and 2 (and is opposite 6 from Positions 2 and 3).
- **C — 6:** 6 is adjacent to 3 in Position 3 (and is opposite 1).
- **D — 4:** 4 is adjacent to 3 in Positions 2 and 3 (and is opposite 2 from Positions 1 and 2).
- **Exam Trick:** Listing all faces adjacent to the target number is the fastest elimination method when 3 positions are given.

### Q735 · Cube & Dice

In a standard die, opposite faces always sum to 7. If 2 is on the top face and 3 is on the front face, which of the following numbers CAN be on the right face?

A. 2
B. 4
C. 6
D. 5

**Answer:** C. 6
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 6 (Correct):** In a standard die, 2 is opposite 5 (so 5 is on the bottom) and 3 is opposite 4 (so 4 is on the back). The only remaining faces available for the left and right sides are 1 and 6.
- **A — 2:** 2 is already on the top face, so it cannot also be on the right face.
- **B — 4:** 4 is opposite to 3 (the front face), so 4 must be on the back face.
- **D — 5:** 5 is opposite to 2 (the top face), so 5 must be on the bottom face.
- **Exam Trick:** In a standard die (1–6), the three opposite pairs are always (1, 6), (2, 5), and (3, 4).

### Q736 · Cube & Dice

Two positions of a cube with symbols @, #, $, %, &, and * are shown. Position 1 has @ (top), # (front), $ (right). Position 2 has @ (top), $ (front), & (right). Which symbol is opposite to #?

A. $
B. &
C. @
D. *

**Answer:** B. &
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — & (Correct):** Both positions have @ on the top face. Moving from Position 1 to Position 2, $ shifts from the right face to the front face (a 90° turn), which shifts # from the front face to the hidden left face—directly opposite to & on the right face.
- **A — $:** $ is adjacent to # in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to #.
- **C — @:** @ is on the top face in both positions, so it is adjacent to #.
- **D — *:** * is opposite to $, not # (since @ is opposite %, and # is opposite &).
- **Exam Trick:** When one face (@) stays on top and an adjacent face ($) moves one quarter-turn from right to front, the old front (#) moves to the left—making it opposite to the new right (&).

### Q737 · Cube & Dice

A cube has its six faces painted Red, Blue, Green, Yellow, White, and Black. In Position 1, Red is on top, Blue is in front, and Green is on the right. In Position 2, Green is on top, Blue is in front, and White is on the right. Which colour is opposite to Red?

A. Blue
B. Green
C. White
D. Black

**Answer:** C. White
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — White (Correct):** Blue and Green are common to both positions. By the two-common-faces rule, the third visible faces in each position—Red and White—must be opposite each other.
- **A — Blue:** Blue is adjacent to Red in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to Red.
- **B — Green:** Green is adjacent to Red in Position 1, so it cannot be opposite to Red.
- **D — Black:** Black is not visible in either position and is opposite either Blue or Green.
- **Exam Trick:** The two-common-faces rule works identically for numbers, letters, symbols, and colours.

### Q738 · Cube & Dice

Three positions of a die are shown: Position 1 has 1 (top), 2 (front), 3 (right); Position 2 has 1 (top), 3 (front), 6 (right); Position 3 has 5 (top), 3 (front), 2 (right). What is the sum of the numbers opposite to 1 and 2?

A. 9
B. 10
C. 11
D. 8

**Answer:** C. 11
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 11 (Correct):** Comparing Positions 1 (1, 2, 3) and 3 (5, 3, 2), faces 2 and 3 are common, so 1 is opposite 5. Comparing Positions 1 and 2, 3 shifts from right to front while 1 stays on top, so 2 (old front) is opposite 6 (new right). Thus the sum is 5 + 6 = 11.
- **A — 9:** Obtained if 1 is paired with 4 instead of 5 (4 + 5 = 9 or 3 + 6 = 9).
- **B — 10:** Obtained if 2 is paired with 5 instead of 6 (5 + 5 = 10).
- **D — 8:** Obtained if 1 is paired with 4 and 2 with 4, or misadding 5 + 3.
- **Exam Trick:** Multi-step dice questions ask for the sum or product of two opposite faces—find each opposite pair cleanly before adding.

### Q739 · Cube & Dice

Two positions of a die are given: Position 1 shows 1 (top), 2 (front), 3 (right); Position 2 shows 1 (top), 3 (front), 5 (right). If 6 is placed on the top face, which number will be on the bottom face?

A. 1
B. 2
C. 4
D. 3

**Answer:** D. 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 3 (Correct):** In both positions, 1 is on top. Moving from Position 1 to Position 2, 3 shifts from right to front, so 2 (old front) is opposite 5 (new right). Since 1 is adjacent to 2, 3, and 5, and 2 is opposite 5, 1 must be opposite 4, leaving 3 opposite 6. Thus when 6 is on top, 3 is on the bottom.
- **A — 1:** 1 is opposite to 4, not 6.
- **B — 2:** 2 is opposite to 5, not 6.
- **C — 4:** 4 is opposite to 1, not 6.
- **Exam Trick:** In any die, the bottom face is simply the face opposite to the top face.

### Q740 · Cube & Dice

Two positions of a die are shown: Position 1 has 1 (top), 3 (front), 4 (right); Position 2 has 3 (top), 5 (front), 2 (right). Which number is opposite to 4?

A. 5
B. 2
C. 6
D. 1

**Answer:** A. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 5 (Correct):** 3 is the common face. Looking at the visible corner (clockwise: top → right → front → top), Position 1 (top 1, right 4, front 3) gives 3 → 1 → 4, and Position 2 (top 3, right 2, front 5) gives 3 → 2 → 5. Pairing corresponding positions gives 1 opposite 2, and 4 opposite 5.
- **B — 2:** 2 is opposite to 1, not 4.
- **C — 6:** 6 is the missing number, which is opposite to the common face 3.
- **D — 1:** 1 is opposite to 2, not 4.
- **Exam Trick:** Be consistent with clockwise direction: looking at the visible corner, top → right → front → top is always clockwise.

### Q741 · Cube & Dice

A standard die (opposite faces sum to 7) has 4 on the top face and 1 on the front face. If the die is tilted 90° forward towards you so that the top face moves to the front, which number will now be on the top face?

A. 3
B. 6
C. 2
D. 5

**Answer:** B. 6
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 6 (Correct):** In a standard die, 1 (front) is opposite 6 (back). When you tilt the die 90° forward so that the top face (4) moves to the front, the back face (6) rotates onto the top.
- **A — 3:** 3 is opposite to 4 (the old top face), so 3 moves to the back face, not the top.
- **C — 2:** 2 is on one of the side faces (left or right), which do not change when tilting forward.
- **D — 5:** 5 is on the other side face (opposite 2) and stays on the side.
- **Exam Trick:** When a cube tilts 90° forward, the cycle is: Bottom → Back → Top → Front → Bottom, while Left and Right stay fixed.

### Q742 · Cube & Dice

A solid cube of side 3 cm is painted red on all six faces and then cut into 27 smaller cubes of side 1 cm each. How many smaller cubes have NO face painted?

A. 0
B. 6
C. 1
D. 8

**Answer:** C. 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1 (Correct):** Here n = 3 ÷ 1 = 3. The number of smaller cubes with 0 faces painted is (n − 2)³ = (3 − 2)³ = 1³ = 1 (the single centermost cube).
- **A — 0:** 0 would only happen if n = 2 (an 8-cube 2×2×2 cut, where every small cube is a corner).
- **B — 6:** 6 is the number of smaller cubes with 1 face painted: 6(3 − 2)² = 6.
- **D — 8:** 8 is the number of smaller cubes with 3 faces painted (the 8 corners).
- **Exam Trick:** For an n × n × n painted cube, remember the four counts: 3-face = 8, 2-face = 12(n − 2), 1-face = 6(n − 2)², 0-face = (n − 2)³.

### Q743 · Cube & Dice

A cube is painted blue on all faces and cut into 64 identical smaller cubes. How many of these smaller cubes have exactly two faces painted?

A. 8
B. 16
C. 20
D. 24

**Answer:** D. 24
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 24 (Correct):** Since 64 = 4³, we have n = 4. Cubes with exactly 2 faces painted lie along the 12 edges (excluding corners): 12(n − 2) = 12 × (4 − 2) = 24.
- **A — 8:** 8 is the number of corner cubes, which have 3 faces painted.
- **B — 16:** 16 is obtained by multiplying by 8 edges instead of 12 edges.
- **C — 20:** 20 is obtained by an arithmetic slip instead of 12 × 2 = 24.
- **Exam Trick:** A cube has 12 edges, and each edge contributes (n − 2) two-face-painted cubes.

### Q744 · Cube & Dice

A large cube is painted green on all six faces and divided into 125 equal smaller cubes. How many smaller cubes have exactly one face painted?

A. 54
B. 36
C. 27
D. 48

**Answer:** A. 54
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 54 (Correct):** Since 125 = 5³, n = 5. Smaller cubes with exactly 1 face painted lie in the interior of the 6 faces: 6(n − 2)² = 6 × (5 − 2)² = 6 × 9 = 54.
- **B — 36:** 36 is the number of smaller cubes with 2 faces painted: 12(5 − 2) = 36.
- **C — 27:** 27 is the number of smaller cubes with 0 faces painted: (5 − 2)³ = 27.
- **D — 48:** 48 is obtained by miscomputing 6 × 8 instead of 6 × 9.
- **Exam Trick:** Check your total for n = 5: 8 (3-face) + 36 (2-face) + 54 (1-face) + 27 (0-face) = 125!

### Q745 · Cube & Dice

A cube of side 12 cm is painted yellow on all faces and cut into smaller cubes of side 2 cm each. How many smaller cubes have no face painted at all?

A. 96
B. 64
C. 48
D. 125

**Answer:** B. 64
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 64 (Correct):** Here n = 12 ÷ 2 = 6 (giving 6³ = 216 smaller cubes). The number of cubes with 0 faces painted is (n − 2)³ = (6 − 2)³ = 4³ = 64.
- **A — 96:** 96 is the number of smaller cubes with 1 face painted: 6(6 − 2)² = 6 × 16 = 96.
- **C — 48:** 48 is the number of smaller cubes with 2 faces painted: 12(6 − 2) = 48.
- **D — 125:** 125 would be (n − 1)³ = 5³, not (n − 2)³.
- **Exam Trick:** Always compute n = (side of large cube) ÷ (side of small cube) first—here n = 12 ÷ 2 = 6, not 12.

### Q746 · Cube & Dice

A solid cube is painted on all six faces and cut into 64 equal smaller cubes. How many smaller cubes have at least two faces painted?

A. 24
B. 28
C. 32
D. 36

**Answer:** C. 32
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 32 (Correct):** With 64 = 4³ cubes, n = 4. 'At least 2 faces painted' means 2 faces OR 3 faces painted: 12(4 − 2) + 8 = 24 + 8 = 32.
- **A — 24:** 24 counts only the cubes with exactly 2 faces painted, forgetting the 8 corner cubes with 3 faces painted.
- **B — 28:** 28 is an arithmetic slip (24 + 4 instead of 24 + 8).
- **D — 36:** 36 is the 2-face count for a 5×5×5 cube, not a 4×4×4 cube.
- **Exam Trick:** 'At least 2 faces painted' = (2-face) + (3-face) = 12(n − 2) + 8.

### Q747 · Cube & Dice

A solid cube is painted on all faces and cut into 125 identical smaller cubes. How many smaller cubes have at most one face painted?

A. 54
B. 64
C. 75
D. 81

**Answer:** D. 81
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 81 (Correct):** With 125 = 5³ cubes, n = 5. 'At most 1 face painted' means 0 faces OR 1 face painted: (5 − 2)³ + 6(5 − 2)² = 27 + 54 = 81.
- **A — 54:** 54 counts only the cubes with exactly 1 face painted, omitting the 27 unpainted inner cubes.
- **B — 64:** 64 is the unpainted core of a 6×6×6 cube, not 5×5×5.
- **C — 75:** 75 is an arithmetic error when adding 27 + 54.
- **Exam Trick:** 'At most 1 face painted' = Total − (at least 2 faces painted) = 125 − (36 + 8) = 81.

### Q748 · Cube & Dice

A cube of side 4 cm has two opposite faces painted Red, two opposite faces painted Blue, and the remaining two opposite faces painted Green. It is then cut into 64 smaller cubes of side 1 cm each. How many smaller cubes have ONLY Red colour painted on them?

A. 8
B. 4
C. 16
D. 12

**Answer:** A. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 8 (Correct):** Only cubes in the interior of the Red faces (not on edges or corners) have solely Red paint. Each Red face has (n − 2)² = (4 − 2)² = 4 such cubes. Since there are 2 Red faces, the total is 2 × 4 = 8.
- **B — 4:** 4 is the number of Red-only cubes on a single Red face, forgetting that there are two opposite Red faces.
- **C — 16:** 16 is the total number of cubes on one entire 4×4 Red face (which includes edge and corner cubes that also have Blue or Green).
- **D — 12:** 12 is the number of cubes on edges between Red and Blue, not Red-only.
- **Exam Trick:** For opposite-face colourings, 'only colour X' = 2 × (n − 2)² (since 2 faces carry colour X).

### Q749 · Cube & Dice

A 4×4×4 cube has each pair of opposite faces painted Red, Blue, and Green respectively, and is cut into 64 unit cubes. How many unit cubes have ONLY Red and Blue painted on them (and no Green)?

A. 4
B. 8
C. 12
D. 16

**Answer:** B. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 (Correct):** Cubes with only Red and Blue lie along the edges where a Red face meets a Blue face (excluding corners, which also have Green). There are 4 such Red–Blue edges, and each has (n − 2) = 2 such cubes, giving 4 × 2 = 8.
- **A — 4:** 4 counts only one cube per Red–Blue edge instead of (n − 2) = 2.
- **C — 12:** 12 is the total number of edges on a cube, but only 4 edges lie between Red and Blue.
- **D — 16:** 16 includes the 8 corner cubes (which have Red, Blue, AND Green).
- **Exam Trick:** Between any two non-opposite colours on a cube, there are always 4 shared edges; each edge gives (n − 2) two-colour-only cubes.

### Q750 · Cube & Dice

What is the minimum number of straight cuts required to divide a solid cube into 216 identical smaller cubes?

A. 12
B. 18
C. 15
D. 21

**Answer:** C. 15
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 15 (Correct):** Since 216 = 6 × 6 × 6, we must divide each of the 3 dimensions into 6 parts. Dividing a dimension into 6 parts requires 6 − 1 = 5 cuts. Thus the total cuts = 5 + 5 + 5 = 15.
- **A — 12:** 12 is the number of cuts for 125 = 5³ cubes (4 + 4 + 4 = 12).
- **B — 18:** 18 mistakenly uses 6 cuts per axis instead of (6 − 1) = 5.
- **D — 21:** 21 is the number of cuts for 512 = 8³ cubes (7 + 7 + 7 = 21).
- **Exam Trick:** To cut a cube into n³ identical smaller cubes, you always need 3(n − 1) straight cuts.
